    6.1

    Pure-tone audiometer and pure-tone audiometry (PTA)

    Supplementary3 hoursPractical P8

    Pure-tone audiometry measures the softest level of a tone that a person can detect at each test frequency, separately for each ear and for air and bone conduction. The audiometer that makes this possible is a precision signal generator with calibrated transducers, and the procedure that turns its output into a valid audiogram depends on a disciplined threshold search, correct masking and a quiet, checked test environment. These notes take the instrument apart block by block and then follow the signal into the patient and the result onto the audiogram.

    What it is

    A pure-tone audiometer is an electroacoustic instrument that generates tones at defined frequencies and levels, gates them on and off smoothly, delivers them to one ear at a time through earphones, a bone vibrator or loudspeakers, supplies masking noise to the other ear and records the patient's responses. Pure-tone audiometry (PTA in the sense of the procedure) is the behavioural test performed with it. The abbreviation PTA is also used for the pure-tone average, a number computed from the thresholds; the meaning is always clear from context.

    Why it is required

    The pure-tone audiogram is the reference description of hearing sensitivity. It establishes whether hearing is normal, how much hearing is lost at each frequency, whether the loss is conductive, sensorineural or mixed, and how the two ears compare. It is the starting point for medical referral, hearing-aid and implant candidacy, fitting prescriptions, occupational and medicolegal assessment, and monitoring of ototoxicity or progressive disease.

    How it works

    A crystal-referenced digital oscillator makes the tone, an envelope generator ramps it on and off, digital and analogue attenuators set its level so that the dial reads in dB hearing level, and a power amplifier drives the chosen transducer. The tester presents tones by a bracketing rule (the modified Hughson-Westlake procedure), the patient signals each detection with a response switch, and masking noise keeps the non-test ear out of the test when sound could cross the head.

    RememberThe basics of audiometer operation, the audiometer types and the calibration couplers are covered in 4.1. This topic goes further into the signal chain, the transducers, the test procedure, masking, audiogram interpretation and the checking regime. Filters are discussed in 1.8.

    1. Basic working principle

    Threshold as a behavioural measurement

    A hearing threshold is a statistical quantity. If a tone is presented many times at one level, the probability that the listener detects it rises smoothly from near 0 to near 1 over a range of roughly 5 to 10 dB. This curve is the psychometric function. Clinical audiometry defines the threshold as the lowest level at which the listener responds on at least half of the ascending presentations, which corresponds approximately to the 50 % point of the psychometric function. Because the step size is 5 dB, the reported threshold carries an inherent uncertainty: a test-retest difference of 5 dB is normal, and differences of 10 dB occur occasionally even with a perfect instrument.

    Sound pressure level and hearing level

    Sound pressure level (SPL) expresses the physical pressure of a sound relative to 20 µPa:

    L_p = 20 log10(p / p0) dB SPL where p is the RMS sound pressure in pascals and p0 = 20 µPa is the reference pressure.

    The ear is not equally sensitive at all frequencies. A young otologically normal adult needs roughly 25 to 45 dB SPL (depending on the earphone and coupler) to detect a 125 Hz tone, but only a few dB SPL at 1 to 4 kHz. If audiograms were plotted in dB SPL, a normal result would be a bowl-shaped curve and every result would have to be read against it. The hearing level (HL) scale removes this curve. For every combination of frequency, transducer and measuring coupler, a reference equivalent threshold sound pressure level (RETSPL) is defined: the SPL, measured in the specified coupler or ear simulator, that corresponds to the median threshold of a large group of otologically normal young adults. The audiometer adds the RETSPL internally, so 0 dB HL means "normal median threshold" at every frequency.

    L_SPL(coupler) = L_HL(dial) + RETSPL(f, transducer, coupler) L_F(mech. coupler) = L_HL(dial) + RETFL(f, vibrator, site) where L_HL is the dial setting in dB HL, RETSPL the reference equivalent threshold sound pressure level (dB re 20 µPa) and RETFL the reference equivalent threshold force level for bone conduction (dB re 1 µN), each specific to frequency f, transducer and coupling.

    Worked example: from the dial to the ear canal (illustrative values)

    The numbers below are illustrative, chosen to be of the right order for teaching. Always take reference values from the standard and table that apply to your transducer and coupler.

    1. An insert earphone is set to 40 dB HL at 1 kHz. Take an illustrative RETSPL of 5.5 dB SPL for this earphone measured in an occluded-ear simulator. The simulator should then read 40 + 5.5 = 45.5 dB SPL.
    2. The corresponding RMS pressure is p = 20 µPa × 1045.5/20 = 20 µPa × 188.4 = 3.77 mPa.
    3. The occluded-ear simulator represents an average adult ear. In an average adult canal the SPL at the eardrum is therefore close to 45.5 dB SPL. In a young child the residual canal volume is smaller, so the same insert produces a higher SPL. If the real-ear-to-coupler difference of this child is 5 dB larger than the adult average at 1 kHz, the child's eardrum receives about 50.5 dB SPL. A threshold recorded as 40 dB HL in this child therefore corresponds to more sound pressure than the same number in an adult, which matters when the audiogram is used to prescribe hearing-aid gain.
    4. Bone conduction: the vibrator is set to 30 dB HL at 1 kHz and an illustrative RETFL of 42.5 dB re 1 µN applies for mastoid placement. The artificial mastoid should read 30 + 42.5 = 72.5 dB re 1 µN, which is a force of 1 µN × 1072.5/20 = 4217 µN, about 4.2 mN.
    Key ideaCalibration is transducer-specific because each transducer couples to the ear differently. A supra-aural earphone presses on the pinna and drives a volume that includes the concha and canal; a circumaural earphone encloses the pinna; an insert earphone drives only the residual canal volume; a bone vibrator produces force, not pressure. Each therefore needs a different electrical drive to reach the same normal threshold, and each has its own RETSPL or RETFL table. When the tester selects a transducer in the software, the audiometer switches to that transducer's calibration table. Selecting the wrong transducer in software is a calibration error even if every component is perfect.

    Air-conduction and bone-conduction pathways

    In air conduction (AC) the sound travels through the ear canal, sets the tympanic membrane and ossicles in motion and reaches the cochlea through the oval window. An AC threshold therefore reflects the whole pathway: outer ear, middle ear, cochlea and neural pathway. In bone conduction (BC) a vibrator on the mastoid or forehead vibrates the skull. The cochlea is stimulated through several mechanisms that act together: compressional (distortion of the bony cochlear shell), inertial (the ossicles lag behind the vibrating skull because of their mass), osseotympanic (the vibrating canal walls radiate sound into the canal) and fluid pathways. Because BC largely bypasses the outer and middle ear, a BC threshold estimates cochlear (sensorineural) sensitivity. The difference between AC and BC thresholds at a frequency, the air-bone gap, estimates the conductive component of the loss.

    Figure 6.1a. Air conduction tests the whole pathway; bone conduction vibrates the skull and reaches both cochleae with almost no loss between them, which is why unmasked BC thresholds cannot be assigned to a particular ear.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.1b expands the audiometer into the blocks an engineer would see on the circuit board or in the firmware. The test (stimulus) channel runs along the top two rows, the masking channel along the third, and the communication and response paths along the bottom.

    Figure 6.1b. Detailed signal chain of a two-channel diagnostic audiometer. Digital blocks (top row) may run in a DSP or microcontroller; analogue blocks (second row) set the final level and drive the transducers. The masking channel joins at the output switch so that noise can go to the ear opposite the tone.

    Tone generation: direct digital synthesis and the DAC

    Direct digital synthesis (DDS), also called a numerically controlled oscillator (NCO), is the standard method in modern audiometers and in the sound cards used by PC-based systems. A phase accumulator of N bits is incremented by a phase step Δφ at every tick of the sample clock. The top bits of the accumulator address a table holding one cycle of a sine wave, and the table output is the next sample. The digital-to-analogue converter (DAC) turns the samples into a staircase voltage and a reconstruction (anti-imaging) low-pass filter smooths the staircase into a sinusoid. The mathematics is given in 4.1; the practical consequences are these:

    • Frequency accuracy is set by the crystal oscillator that provides the sample clock. Crystals are typically accurate to tens of parts per million (ppm). At 50 ppm a 4000 Hz tone is off by 4000 × 50 × 10−6 = 0.2 Hz, while audiometer standards typically allow about ±1 % to ±2 % (40 to 80 Hz at 4 kHz). Frequency errors in a modern audiometer therefore indicate a fault or a wrong setting, not drift.
    • Spectral purity depends on the sine table length, the DAC resolution and linearity, and the reconstruction filter. Truncating the phase to address a finite table produces small spurious tones; a good design keeps them far below the fundamental.
    • Dynamic range: an ideal N-bit converter has a signal-to-quantisation-noise ratio of about 6.02 N + 1.76 dB, 98.1 dB for 16 bits and 146.2 dB for 24 bits. Real converters achieve less, often around 100 to 120 dB. The audiometer must produce levels from about −10 dB HL to 120 dB HL at mid frequencies (about 130 dB), which is why the level is usually set partly by digital gain and partly by switched analogue attenuators. At low dial settings the DAC then still runs near full scale and the signal stays well above the converter noise.

    Rise and fall shaping

    Switching a tone on instantaneously multiplies it by a step function. In the frequency domain this spreads energy over a wide band (spectral splatter), heard as a click. A patient with a steep high-frequency loss tested at 4 kHz may hear the low-frequency part of the click and respond although the 4 kHz tone itself is inaudible. The envelope gate multiplies the tone by a smooth ramp, commonly a raised cosine, with rise and fall times of typically a few tens of milliseconds. The on-off ratio of the gate is also specified: when the tone is off, any residual output must be far below the level that could be heard.

    g(t) = 0.5 × (1 − cos(π t / T_r)) for 0 ≤ t ≤ T_r g(t) = 1 during the plateau where g(t) is the gain applied to the tone and T_r the rise time; the fall is the mirror image. Rise time is usually quoted between the 10 % and 90 % (or −1 dB and −20 dB) amplitude points; check which definition your standard uses.

    Attenuator and step size

    The hearing-level control changes the output in steps of 5 dB for routine testing, with 1 or 2 dB steps available for research, Stenger-type tests or fine threshold work. Each 5 dB step multiplies the voltage by 10−5/20 = 0.562. The attenuator must be accurate over the whole range: standards specify how much the actual change may differ from the indicated change for each step and cumulatively. Analogue attenuators are resistor networks switched by relays or analogue switches; their errors grow if contacts are dirty or a resistor drifts. Digital attenuation is exact in the arithmetic but can lose resolution at very low levels, which is why hybrid designs are common. The maximum level available is lower at 125 Hz, at 8 kHz and for bone conduction because the transducer would otherwise distort; the audiometer shows a maximum-output indicator when the dial reaches the limit.

    Output amplifier and transducer switching

    Earphones and bone vibrators are low-impedance loads (typically of the order of ten to a few tens of ohms, depending on the model). The power amplifier must deliver clean current into this load up to the maximum level, with low harmonic distortion and without hum or hiss audible at −10 dB HL. The bone vibrator needs much more drive than an earphone for the same hearing level because it is an inefficient transducer and the head is a heavy mechanical load; the highest BC levels are limited by vibrator distortion and by the patient feeling the vibration (vibrotactile response) at low frequencies. The output switch routes the test and masking signals to the right or left earphone, the bone vibrator, or the loudspeakers, and simultaneously selects the matching calibration table. Crosstalk from the test earphone into the other earphone lead must be negligible (standards typically require it to be many tens of dB below the test level).

    Masking-noise generator

    The masking channel starts from a pseudo-random number generator that produces white noise (equal power per hertz). Filters then shape it:

    • Narrowband noise (NBN) for pure-tone masking: a band-pass filter centred on the test frequency, typically about one-third to one-half octave wide with steep skirts. It concentrates energy in the auditory filter (critical band) around the tone, so a modest overall level masks effectively.
    • Speech-shaped noise for speech audiometry: a filter that follows the long-term average speech spectrum, roughly flat to about 1 kHz and falling above that.
    • White noise: broadband, inefficient for masking a single tone because most of its energy lies outside the critical band. It remains available on many instruments for special purposes.

    The masking dial is calibrated in effective masking level (dB EM), using reference levels for narrowband masking noise. By definition, NBN at X dB EM just masks a tone at X dB HL at the same frequency in an average normal ear. This lets the tester think in hearing-level terms when setting masking.

    Talk-over, talk-back and monitoring

    Talk-over routes the tester's microphone to the patient's earphones (or loudspeakers) while a key is held, so instructions can be given without removing the earphones. Talk-back carries sound from a microphone in the test booth to the tester's monitor headset or loudspeaker, so the tester hears the patient and can check that the booth is quiet. The monitor also lets the tester listen to the test and masking signals, which is essential in monitored live-voice speech testing. Talk-over must never remain open during threshold testing: the tester's voice or breathing in the patient's ear is an uncontrolled stimulus.

    Patient response switch

    The response switch is a hand-held push button that lights an indicator for the tester and, in computer-controlled audiometers, logs a time-stamped event. The firmware debounces the contact (ignores the mechanical bounce for a few milliseconds) and can measure response latency. Automated procedures use the timing to reject responses that come too soon after tone onset (probably guesses) or too late (probably not linked to the tone). A stuck or intermittent switch is a common cause of apparently inconsistent thresholds and is part of the daily check.

    Computer-based audiometers and USB audio interfaces

    In PC-based audiometers the signal generation and attenuation happen in a dedicated USB (or wireless) hardware unit, while the computer runs the user interface, the test procedure and the database. Two architectures exist:

    Dedicated audiometric hardware

    • The unit contains its own DAC, attenuators, amplifiers and calibration memory.
    • The computer sends commands (frequency, level, route), not audio samples.
    • Calibration stays with the hardware and its transducers, independent of the operating system.
    • This is the architecture of clinically validated PC-based audiometers.

    Generic sound interface

    • The software generates samples and sends them through the operating system to a USB sound card.
    • Level depends on the OS mixer, driver, sample-rate conversion and any "enhancement" effects.
    • Calibration is valid only for that exact interface, driver, volume setting and transducer.
    • Acceptable for teaching and some research after careful calibration; not a substitute for a certified audiometer.
    Common errorWith a generic sound card, an operating-system update, a change of default output device, an automatic volume normalisation feature or a different sample rate can change the output level by many decibels without any warning. Clinical use requires hardware whose calibration cannot be altered by the host computer, and a check of the output before each session.

    Components and what can go wrong

    BlockFunctionTypical faults and their effect on the audiogram
    Crystal clock and DDSFrequency and waveform of the toneVery rare frequency faults; firmware fault giving wrong frequency on one key
    DAC and reconstruction filterConverts samples to voltageRaised noise floor audible at low levels; distortion that lets a patient hear harmonics of a low tone
    Envelope gateSmooth on and off; silence when offClicks at onset (false responses at high frequencies); tone leaking when off
    Digital gain and analogue attenuatorSets dB HLStep errors (thresholds jump), dead relay (level stuck), wrong calibration table after a firmware update
    Power amplifierDrives transducerClipping at high levels, hum, channel crosstalk
    Output switchRouting and table selectionSignal to wrong ear, noise and tone in the same ear, intermittent relay
    Transducers and cordsConvert voltage to sound or forceIntermittent cord (tone cuts out), aged cushions, blocked insert tubes, dropped bone vibrator with changed output
    Masking channelNBN, speech noise, white noiseNo noise in one earphone, wrong band, uncalibrated dB EM
    Talk-over, talk-back, monitorCommunicationTalk key stuck open, acoustic feedback, tester unable to hear patient
    Response switchPatient signalStuck or intermittent contact, cable damage
    Controller, software, PC linkProcedure, data, calibration memoryWrong transducer selected, corrupted calibration, lost data, OS-level volume changes in generic set-ups

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    SignalHow it is generatedUse
    Continuous pure toneDDS sine at 125 to 8000 Hz (octave and inter-octave frequencies)Standard AC and BC thresholds
    Pulsed pure toneTone gated automatically, for example about 200 ms on and 200 ms off, each pulse with its own rampsEasier detection for many listeners, especially with tinnitus; reduces false responses
    Warble tone (FM)Frequency modulated by a few percent at a few hertzSound field testing, where it reduces standing waves; paediatric testing
    Narrowband noiseWhite noise through a band-pass filter of roughly 1/3 to 1/2 octaveMasking in pure-tone audiometry; sometimes as a stimulus for young children
    Speech-shaped noiseWhite noise through a speech-spectrum filterMasking in speech audiometry; speech-in-noise tests
    White noisePRNG output, flat spectrumSpecial tests; not an efficient pure-tone masker
    Extended high-frequency tonesDDS at about 9 to 16 kHz (to 20 kHz on some instruments)Ototoxicity and noise monitoring
    SpeechMonitored live voice or recorded listsSpeech audiometry (see 6.6)
    Warble: f(t) = fc × (1 + d × sin(2π fm t)) NBN band: f_lo = fc / 2^(B/2), f_hi = fc × 2^(B/2) where fc is the centre frequency, d the fractional deviation, fm the modulation rate and B the bandwidth in octaves.

    Worked example: a one-third-octave NBN centred on 1000 Hz has B = 1/3, so flo = 1000 / 21/6 = 1000 / 1.1225 = 891 Hz and fhi = 1000 × 1.1225 = 1122 Hz, a bandwidth of 231 Hz. A half-octave band gives 1000 / 21/4 = 841 Hz to 1189 Hz (348 Hz). Both are wider than the auditory filter at 1 kHz (roughly 130 Hz equivalent rectangular bandwidth), so the band covers the critical region around the tone with a margin for filter skirts.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Transducers

    TransducerConstruction and couplingAdvantagesLimitations
    Supra-aural earphone (for example TDH-39/49/50 and DD45 type)Dynamic driver in a cushion resting on the pinna, held by a headband with a specified forceRobust, quick to fit, long history of reference dataLowest interaural attenuation (conservative value about 40 dB); little attenuation of room noise; can collapse soft ear canals; placement changes the high-frequency output
    Circumaural earphone (for example HDA 200 and HDA 300 type)Large cup that encloses the pinna and seals on the headMore attenuation of ambient noise; suitable for extended high frequenciesBulky; needs its own reference values; seal affected by glasses and hair
    Insert earphone (for example ER-3A / E-A-RTONE 3A and ER-3C type)Transducer box connected by a plastic tube to a foam or rubber tip in the canalHigher interaural attenuation (commonly 55 dB or more is used as a conservative value, larger at low frequencies); prevents canal collapse; more noise exclusion; smaller occlusion effect when deeply insertedTube adds a delay of about 1 ms and shapes the response; output depends on insertion depth and canal volume; not usable with discharge, some atresias or canal obstructions
    Bone vibrator (for example B71 and B81 type)Electromagnetic vibrator with a plastic contact surface, held on the mastoid (or forehead) by a headband with a defined static forceEstimates cochlear sensitivity independent of the middle earInteraural attenuation near 0 dB; limited maximum output; vibrotactile responses at low frequencies; acoustic radiation at high frequencies that the ear can hear by air
    Sound field loudspeakersLoudspeakers in the booth at a defined distance and azimuthTests without earphones (children, aided testing, cochlear implant users)Cannot separate ears unless one is masked; standing waves (use warble tones or NBN); level depends on head position

    Placement is part of calibration. A supra-aural earphone must have its diaphragm centred over the canal opening; a 1 cm offset can reduce the high-frequency output by several dB. An insert tip is compressed, inserted until its outer face is flush with the canal entrance and allowed to expand. The bone vibrator sits on the mastoid process without touching the pinna, with the headband tension within the specified static force; hair under the contact surface reduces the output. For forehead placement a different reference table applies, and thresholds are typically poorer (the forehead needs more force) but less affected by placement variability.

    Timing of presentations

    Each tone is presented for about 1 to 2 seconds. Intervals between presentations are varied irregularly so that the patient cannot respond to a rhythm. Pulsed tones (three pulses per presentation, for example) are often preferred because the on-off pattern is distinct from tinnitus. The tester watches the patient, not the audiometer, and keeps hand movements out of the patient's view.

    Test environment and ambient noise

    Room noise masks soft tones. A threshold of 0 dB HL can be measured only if the noise that reaches the cochlea in the critical band around the test frequency is below the level that would raise that threshold. Standards therefore specify maximum permissible ambient noise levels (MPANLs) in octave or third-octave bands, chosen so that thresholds down to 0 dB HL (or to the lowest level to be tested) are raised by no more than a small amount, typically about 2 dB. The concept can be expressed simply:

    MPANL(f) = L_quiet(f) + A_transducer(f) where L_quiet is the band noise level that the uncovered ear can tolerate without a threshold shift at the lowest test level, and A_transducer is the attenuation of room noise provided by the earphone at that frequency (0 dB for bone conduction and sound field).

    Consequences: the permitted room noise is lowest for bone conduction and sound field testing (ears uncovered) and at the low frequencies, where earphones attenuate least; insert earphones allow a noisier room than supra-aural earphones; testing down to −10 dB HL requires about 10 dB less noise than testing down to 0 dB HL. In an illustrative case, if the limit for uncovered ears in the 1 kHz band is L and an earphone attenuates room noise by 20 dB at 1 kHz, the room may be 20 dB noisier in that band when testing by that earphone, but not when testing BC. This is why a room that seems "quiet enough" for AC can still produce elevated BC thresholds and false air-bone gaps in reverse (BC poorer than AC).

    Clinical linkIf BC thresholds in the low frequencies are consistently poorer than AC in patients with normal middle ears, suspect ambient noise (or an occluding vibrator headband) before suspecting the patient. Measure the booth noise with a sound level meter with octave-band filters (see 4.6) and compare it with the MPANL table for ears uncovered.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The modified Hughson-Westlake procedure

    The response is behavioural: the patient presses the switch (or raises a hand, or says yes) whenever a tone is heard. The tester controls the level by a bracketing rule that approaches threshold from below, because ascending presentations give more stable thresholds than descending ones (the patient is less likely to "hang on" to a tone that has faded). The widely used procedure, often called the modified Hughson-Westlake method (after Carhart and Jerger), runs as follows:

    1. Instruct the patient; fit transducers; begin with the better ear (by report) at 1 kHz.
    2. Familiarise: present a clearly audible tone (for example 30 or 40 dB HL, or higher for a known loss) and confirm the response.
    3. After each response, decrease the level by 10 dB. After each failure to respond, increase the level by 5 dB ("down 10, up 5").
    4. Threshold is the lowest level at which responses occur on at least two of three ascending presentations (at least 50 % of ascending trials, with a minimum of two responses).
    5. Test 1, 2, 3 (as required), 4 and 8 kHz, retest 1 kHz (agreement within 5 dB checks reliability), then 500 and 250 Hz. Test inter-octave frequencies (750, 1500, 3000, 6000 Hz) when adjacent octave thresholds differ by 20 dB or more.
    6. Repeat for the other ear, then for bone conduction (commonly 250 or 500 to 4000 Hz), with masking whenever the rules below require it.

    Figure 6.1c. A threshold search at 1 kHz. Responses at 40 and 30 dB lead to 10 dB drops; no response at 20 and 25 dB leads to 5 dB rises. Ascending presentations at 25 dB (trials 4, 7 and 10) give no, yes, yes: two of three, so threshold is 25 dB HL.

    Cross-hearing and interaural attenuation

    A tone delivered to one ear also vibrates the skull. If it is loud enough, the vibration stimulates the other cochlea by bone conduction and the patient responds although the test cochlea did not hear the tone. The reduction in level between the test earphone and the non-test cochlea is the interaural attenuation (IA). IA varies between people and with frequency; clinical rules therefore use conservative minimum values, commonly about 40 dB for supra-aural earphones, about 55 dB or more for deeply inserted insert earphones (larger at low frequencies), and 0 dB for bone conduction. A threshold obtained through cross-hearing is called a shadow response.

    Figure 6.1d. Cross-hearing and masking. The tone reaches the non-test cochlea attenuated by IA; masking noise in the non-test ear prevents that cochlea from responding. The same IA also limits how much masking can be used before the noise reaches the test cochlea.

    When to mask

    AC masking needed if: AC_test − IA ≥ BC_nontest BC masking needed if: AC_test − BC_test > 10 dB (IA for BC ≈ 0) where AC_test is the unmasked air-conduction threshold of the test ear, BC_nontest the best bone-conduction threshold of the non-test ear at that frequency (if the non-test BC has not yet been measured, its AC threshold is used provisionally and the decision is revisited once BC is known), and IA the interaural attenuation for the transducer in use.

    The BC rule reflects the fact that an unmasked BC threshold may come from either cochlea. Whenever the test ear shows an air-bone gap larger than about 10 dB, the tester must mask the non-test ear to find out whether the gap belongs to the test ear.

    Effective masking, occlusion effect and central masking

    • Effective masking (EM): the dial reading in dB EM gives the hearing level of a tone that the noise would just mask in a normal ear. Masking of X dB EM shifts the non-test ear's AC threshold to about X dB HL.
    • Occlusion effect (OE): covering the ear canal with an earphone improves BC thresholds at low frequencies because sound radiated into the canal by the vibrating canal walls can no longer escape. Commonly used clinical values for supra-aural earphones are in the region of 15 to 30 dB at 250 Hz, 15 to 20 dB at 500 Hz and about 5 to 10 dB at 1 kHz, with little effect at 2 kHz and above. With deeply inserted foam tips the effect is much smaller. The non-test ear is occluded by the masking earphone during BC masking, so the masker must be raised by OE to compensate.
    • Central masking: masking noise in one ear can raise the threshold in the other ear by a few dB (typically about 5 dB) through central interactions, even without crossover. A 5 dB shift with masking is therefore not by itself evidence of a shadow response.

    The plateau method

    Initial masking (AC test): M_min = AC_nontest + 10 Initial masking (BC test): M_min = AC_nontest + OE + 10 Maximum masking: M_max = BC_test + IA − 5 where levels are in dB HL or dB EM, 10 dB is a safety factor for variability, OE the occlusion effect at that frequency (0 dB when the non-test ear is not occluded or at 2 kHz and above), and 5 dB a safety margin below the level at which the masker crosses to the test cochlea (overmasking).

    The plateau (Hood) procedure: with masking at Mmin, find the test-ear threshold. If the patient responds, raise the masker by 5 dB and present the tone again at the same level; if the patient does not respond, raise the tone by 5 dB until a response. Continue until the threshold stays the same over three successive 5 dB masker increases (a plateau of 15 dB; some protocols accept 10 dB). That threshold is the masked threshold of the test ear. If the threshold keeps rising with every masker step, the earlier responses were shadows (undermasking); if it starts rising again after a plateau, the masker has reached the test cochlea (overmasking).

    Worked example 1: air-conduction masking

    At 1 kHz the left ear has AC 10 dB HL and BC 5 dB HL. The unmasked right AC threshold with supra-aural earphones is 55 dB HL. Right BC (masked, measured later) is 55 dB HL.

    1. Need to mask? 55 − 40 = 15 ≥ 5, so yes.
    2. Initial masking: Mmin = 10 + 10 = 20 dB EM in the left ear.
    3. With 20 dB EM the patient does not respond at 55 but responds at 60 dB HL. The masker is raised to 25, 30 and 35 dB EM and the patient still responds at 60 each time: a 15 dB plateau. The masked right AC threshold is 60 dB HL.
    4. Maximum masking: Mmax = 55 + 40 − 5 = 90 dB EM, so the plateau lies well inside the safe range.
    5. Interpretation: the unmasked 55 dB HL was a shadow. This patient's actual IA was about 55 − 5 = 50 dB, greater than the conservative 40 dB used in the rule, which is typical: the rule is deliberately cautious.

    Worked example 2: bone-conduction masking and the occlusion effect

    At 500 Hz: right AC 45 dB HL, unmasked BC 15 dB HL; left AC 15 dB HL, BC 10 dB HL. The right air-bone gap is 30 dB, so masking is needed.

    1. Supra-aural masking earphone on the left, OE taken as 15 dB at 500 Hz: Mmin = 15 + 15 + 10 = 40 dB EM. If the masked right BC threshold turns out to be 20 dB HL, Mmax = 20 + 40 − 5 = 55 dB EM. The usable range is 40 to 55 dB EM, just enough for a 15 dB plateau.
    2. Insert earphone for the masker, OE taken as 5 dB and IA as 55 dB: Mmin = 15 + 5 + 10 = 30 dB EM and Mmax = 20 + 55 − 5 = 70 dB EM. The window is now 40 dB wide. This is the main practical reason for masking through an insert earphone.

    Worked example 3: the masking dilemma

    Bilateral conductive loss at 1 kHz: both ears AC 50 dB HL and BC 10 dB HL (air-bone gaps of 40 dB).

    • Testing right AC with supra-aural earphones: Mmin = 50 + 10 = 60 dB EM, but Mmax = 10 + 40 − 5 = 45 dB EM. The minimum needed exceeds the maximum allowed: any effective masker overmasks. This is the masking dilemma.
    • With insert earphones (IA taken as 55 dB): Mmax = 10 + 55 − 5 = 60 dB EM, equal to Mmin. A single masking level is usable and no plateau can be demonstrated.
    • Options: use deeply inserted inserts for both tone and masker, rely on tympanometry, acoustic reflexes and tuning-fork tests to support the conductive diagnosis, or use bone-conduction ABR with masking where ear-specific cochlear thresholds are essential (see 6.2).

    A useful summary follows from the model in the demo below: in theory the width of the region where masking works is about 2 × IA − ABGtest − ABGnon-test. Large air-bone gaps in both ears shrink it; higher IA (inserts) widens it.

    Try it: the masking plateau

    The graph shows the threshold the tester would record in the test ear (air conduction) as the masking level in the non-test ear increases. Change the ear conditions and the interaural attenuation and watch the undermasking, plateau and overmasking regions move. The model is idealised (no central masking, no response variability).

    Unmasked threshold recorded
    Shadow (cross-hearing) level
    Plateau from / to
    Plateau width
    Clinical start (AC_nt + 10)

    Recorded thresholdTrue test-ear thresholdPlateau limitsClinical starting level

    The model behind the demo: the non-test cochlea's effective threshold under masking is max(BCnt, M − ABGnt), because noise delivered by air is weakened by the non-test ear's own conductive loss. A shadow response occurs at that level plus IA. The masker reaches the test cochlea at M − IA; once this exceeds BCtest, the test ear's AC threshold rises by the excess. The recorded threshold is whichever route gives the lower level.

    Automated audiometry

    Automated audiometers carry out the threshold search without a tester. Two families exist. In Békésy (tracking) audiometry the level falls while the patient holds the button and rises when it is released; the threshold is the mid-point of the resulting zig-zag. In computer-controlled bracketing the software applies a Hughson-Westlake-type rule, randomises intervals, inserts catch trials (no tone) to estimate false responses, and checks test-retest agreement. Some systems also perform masked AC and BC testing with automated masking rules, and some test outside a booth while monitoring ambient noise through microphones in the earphone cups and pausing when the noise is too high. For cooperative adults, validated automated systems generally produce AC thresholds close to manual thresholds, typically within about 5 to 10 dB for most frequencies. They are less suitable for young children, people with cognitive impairment, and complex masking cases, and their results need review by an audiologist.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning

    The binding values are those of the audiometer standard (IEC 60645-1 or ANSI S3.6), the reference value tables for the transducer (ISO 389 series) and the environment and procedure standards (ISO 8253-1, ANSI S3.1, ANSI S3.21) used in your country. Follow the equipment manual and local protocol.

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    • Interacoustics: stand-alone clinical audiometers (for example the AC40 and AD629 families) and PC-based audiometry (Equinox and the Affinity platform).
    • GSI (Grason-Stadler): clinical audiometers (for example GSI 61 and AudioStar Pro), the portable Pello, and GSI AMTAS automated audiometry.
    • Natus (Otometrics / MADSEN): MADSEN Astera and Itera audiometer families.
    • MAICO: diagnostic and screening audiometers (MA series).
    • Inventis: diagnostic audiometers such as the Piano, Harp and Cello (PC-based).
    • Amplivox and other manufacturers supply portable and screening audiometers; several companies offer tablet-based and boothless audiometry systems with active noise monitoring.
    • Transducers: supra-aural, circumaural and insert earphones and B71/B81-type bone vibrators are supplied by transducer makers (for example RadioEar and Sennheiser for some circumaural models) and must be calibrated with the audiometer they are used on.
    RememberExamples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation. Listing a product is not an endorsement. The choice of instrument should follow the required audiometer type, the transducers needed, service and calibration support available locally, and data integration with the clinic's records.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation basics

    Audiogram conventions

    The audiogram plots frequency on a logarithmic horizontal axis (125 Hz to 8 kHz) and hearing level on a vertical axis with 0 dB HL near the top and louder levels downwards, so that poorer hearing plots lower. A common recommendation is that one octave on the frequency axis occupies the same distance as 20 dB on the level axis, so that slopes look the same on every audiogram. Symbols follow a widely used convention (the ASHA symbol system is the most common reference); right-ear symbols are traditionally red and left-ear symbols blue, but the shape of the symbol carries the information, so the audiogram must still be readable in black and white.

    MeasurementRight earLeft ear
    AC, unmaskedO (circle)X (cross)
    AC, masked△ (triangle)□ (square)
    BC mastoid, unmasked<>
    BC mastoid, masked[]
    BC forehead, unmaskedV (either ear; masked forehead symbols also exist)
    Sound fieldS (not ear-specific)
    No response at the maximum levelthe symbol with a downward arrow, placed at the maximum level tested

    The right-ear BC symbols open towards the right of the page and sit just to the left of the frequency line; left-ear symbols sit just to the right. AC symbols of each ear are joined by lines; BC symbols are usually not joined. The audiogram form should also record the transducers used, the masking levels, the reliability of the responses and any factor affecting validity.

    Type of hearing loss

    • Normal: AC and BC within the normal range.
    • Conductive: BC normal, AC elevated, air-bone gap greater than about 10 dB (the exact criterion varies; 15 dB or more at two or more frequencies is a common practical rule). Causes include wax, effusion, perforation, otosclerosis and ossicular discontinuity.
    • Sensorineural: AC and BC both elevated and within about 10 dB of each other.
    • Mixed: BC elevated, and AC elevated further with a significant air-bone gap.
    ABG(f) = AC(f) − BC(f) PTA3 = (T500 + T1000 + T2000) / 3 PTA4 = (T500 + T1000 + T2000 + T4000) / 4 where AC and BC are the (masked where required) thresholds at frequency f and T are AC thresholds in dB HL.

    Worked example: right AC thresholds at 0.5, 1, 2 and 4 kHz are 45, 40, 35 and 40 dB HL. PTA3 = (45 + 40 + 35) / 3 = 40.0 dB HL; PTA4 = (45 + 40 + 35 + 40) / 4 = 40.0 dB HL. With masked BC of 10, 10, 15 and 15 dB HL, the air-bone gaps are 35, 30, 20 and 25 dB, indicating a conductive loss.

    Degree of hearing loss (example scale: WHO 2021 grades)

    Many degree scales exist. As an example, the grades in the WHO World report on hearing (2021) use the four-frequency average of 0.5, 1, 2 and 4 kHz in the better ear:

    GradeBetter-ear PTA4 (dB HL)
    No hearing loss (normal)less than 20
    Mild20 to less than 35
    Moderate35 to less than 50
    Moderately severe50 to less than 65
    Severe65 to less than 80
    Profound80 to less than 95
    Complete or total95 or greater
    Unilateralless than 20 in the better ear, 35 or greater in the worse ear

    Other scales (for example those in common clinical textbooks, or national disability certification rules) use different frequencies and boundaries. Always state the scale used. Describing each ear with the same labels, as the demo below does, is a descriptive convenience; the WHO grade itself refers to the better ear.

    Configuration

    Configuration describes the shape: flat (thresholds within about 20 dB across frequencies), sloping (poorer with increasing frequency), precipitous (steeply sloping), rising (poorer in the low frequencies, as in some cases of Ménière's disease), notched (a dip near 3 to 6 kHz with recovery at 8 kHz, typical of noise exposure), cookie-bite or trough-shaped (poorer mid frequencies), and corner audiogram (responses only at low frequencies at high levels, which may be vibrotactile). Symmetry is judged between ears; a clinically significant asymmetry (local criteria, often 15 to 20 dB at two or more adjacent frequencies) is a reason for further investigation.

    Try it: audiogram editor

    Choose ear, conduction and masking, then click on the audiogram to place a threshold (it snaps to the nearest test frequency and 5 dB). Clicking again at the same frequency replaces the point; use Erase mode to remove one. Pure-tone averages, air-bone gaps, degree (WHO 2021 grades used as an example) and masking flags update automatically. An example case is preloaded.

    Right PTA3 / PTA4
    Right grade
    Left PTA3 / PTA4
    Left grade
    Better-ear grade

    Right earLeft ear

    How the demo decides: averages use the masked threshold where one exists, otherwise the unmasked one; a missing frequency leaves the average undefined. The AC flag appears when ACtest − IAAC ≥ BCnon-test (or the non-test AC if no BC is entered). The BC flag appears when the test ear's air-bone gap exceeds 10 dB and BCtest − IABC ≥ BCnon-test (with IABC = 0 the second condition is almost always met). A flag disappears once a masked point is entered for that ear, conduction and frequency.

    Extended high-frequency audiometry

    Extended high-frequency (EHF) audiometry tests roughly 9 to 16 kHz (to 20 kHz on some systems) using circumaural earphones or insert earphones for which reference values in this range exist. Above about 6 to 8 kHz the wavelength becomes comparable with the ear canal length, so standing waves make the SPL at the eardrum depend strongly on earphone position and canal geometry. EHF thresholds therefore show wide normal ranges and larger test-retest differences than conventional frequencies, and they vary strongly with age. They are most useful for comparison with the same person's baseline. Main applications are monitoring ototoxic drugs (cisplatin, aminoglycosides), where damage typically starts at the highest frequencies, and research on noise exposure and early age-related change. A commonly cited set of criteria for an ototoxic change (from ASHA guidance) is a shift of 20 dB or more at one frequency, 10 dB or more at two adjacent frequencies, or loss of response at three consecutive frequencies where responses were previously obtained, confirmed by retest.

    Clinical applications

    • Diagnosis: type, degree, configuration and symmetry; combined with tympanometry, reflexes, OAEs and speech tests to locate the lesion.
    • Rehabilitation: hearing-aid prescription formulas take the AC thresholds (and the air-bone gap) as input; cochlear implant candidacy combines audiometric and speech criteria.
    • Monitoring: ototoxicity, sudden sensorineural hearing loss recovery, progressive and fluctuating losses, post-operative outcome after middle-ear surgery (closure of the air-bone gap).
    • Occupational and medicolegal: baseline and periodic audiograms in hearing conservation programmes; disability assessment using the rules of the jurisdiction.
    • Screening: fixed-level sweeps in schools and workplaces (see 6.6).

    Advantages and limitations

    Advantages

    • Frequency-specific and ear-specific
    • Quick, inexpensive, universally understood
    • Separates conductive and sensorineural components
    • Large normative and standards base

    Limitations

    • Needs a cooperative, attentive patient; not suitable for infants below the developmental age for conditioned responses
    • Detection only: says little about suprathreshold processing or speech in noise
    • 5 dB resolution and test-retest variability of about 5 dB
    • Masking can be complex and sometimes impossible
    • Vulnerable to non-organic (functional) responses

    Errors and troubleshooting

    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    High-frequency AC loss that is not confirmed laterCollapsing ear canals under supra-aural cushionsInspect canals; retest with insert earphones
    Air-bone gap at 4 kHz only, normal middle earAcoustic radiation from the bone vibrator heard by air, or vibrator placementCheck placement; consider an earplug in the test ear at 4 kHz as some protocols do; interpret cautiously
    BC poorer than AC by more than about 10 dBAmbient noise, poor vibrator contact, hair under vibrator, normal variabilityMeasure booth noise, reposition, retest
    Low-frequency BC responses at high levels in a profound lossVibrotactile sensationAsk whether the tone is heard or felt; mark as possibly vibrotactile
    Thresholds inconsistent, 1 kHz retest differs by more than 5 dBPatient fatigue, misunderstanding, false responses, intermittent cord or switchRe-instruct, use pulsed tones, check the equipment
    Shadow curve: poorer ear's unmasked AC runs about 40 to 60 dB below the better earCross-hearingApply the masking rules
    Threshold keeps rising with every masking increaseUndermasking, overmasking or central maskingRecalculate Mmin and Mmax; use inserts; consider masking dilemma
    Speech recognition threshold much better than PTAFunctional (non-organic) hearing loss, or a steep configurationCompare SRT with PTA; objective tests (OAE, ABR, ASSR)
    Whole audiogram shifted in a PC-based set-upOS volume or device changed, wrong transducer tableOutput check with coupler or sound level meter before testing

    Calibration regime and the daily equipment check

    Calibration is organised in three levels. ISO 8253-1 describes them as stages A, B and C; ASHA and many national guidelines use equivalent daily (listening), periodic and exhaustive checks. Coupler and reference-value details are in 4.1.

    LevelWhen (typical)Who and with whatContent
    Daily / routine (stage A)Every day of use, before the first patientThe audiologist, by listening; no instruments except perhaps a simple checkerVisual inspection, listening check of every transducer and channel, attenuator, masking, response switch, talk-over and talk-back (see list below)
    Periodic objective (stage B)At fixed intervals (at least annually is a common minimum; more often for heavily used or portable equipment)Trained technician with sound level meter, couplers or ear simulators, artificial mastoid, frequency counter or analyserOutput level at each frequency and transducer, attenuator linearity, frequency, distortion, masking levels, rise and fall, crosstalk; adjustment of the calibration table
    Exhaustive / basic (stage C)After repair, after a fault found in stage B, or at longer intervalsCalibration laboratory or manufacturerAll parameters against the audiometer standard, including rarely checked items (off-state level, warble parameters, speech channel frequency response)

    Daily listening check (a practical sequence)

    1. Inspect: cords for kinks and cracks, cushions and headband, insert tubes and tips for splits and blockage, bone vibrator headband tension, loudspeaker positions, response switch cable.
    2. Switch on and let the instrument warm up as the manual advises; confirm date, time and the correct transducer selection in software.
    3. Listen through each earphone at a low level (for example 10 to 15 dB HL, just above your own threshold) at every frequency: the tone should be present, clean and equal in the two earphones.
    4. At a moderate level (for example 60 dB HL) sweep the attenuator: the level should change smoothly with no crackles, jumps or dead steps. Wiggle the cords during a continuous tone: no dropouts or crackle.
    5. Check the tone switch: no click at onset or offset, no tone audible when off. Check pulsed and warble modes.
    6. Crosstalk: with the tone at a high level in one earphone, listen with the other earphone alone (the active earphone held away or covered): nothing should be heard.
    7. Bone vibrator: listen at low and moderate levels with the vibrator on your own mastoid; check that the output is clean.
    8. Masking: each noise type in each earphone, level control smooth, noise in the correct ear.
    9. Response switch and indicator; talk-over and talk-back levels; monitor; speech channel with a recorded track.
    10. Sound field: loudspeakers at their marked positions, no distortion.
    11. Record the check in the log, with the name of the checker and any action taken. If anything is wrong, withdraw the channel from use.
    Key ideaA biological check uses a person with known, stable thresholds (often the audiologist). A shift of 10 dB or more at any frequency, compared with that person's reference audiogram, suggests an equipment fault and calls for an objective check. The biological check cannot prove calibration; it detects gross change.

    Demonstration

    Equipment: a two-channel diagnostic audiometer with supra-aural and insert earphones and a bone vibrator; a sound level meter with a coupler or ear simulator and, if available, an artificial mastoid; a frequency counter or FFT analyser app on a computer; a volunteer; this page with the two demos.

    1. Open the audiometer case or show the service block diagram, and trace the signal chain of Figure 6.1b. Point out the crystal, DAC, attenuator relays (listen to them click as the dial moves) and transducer outputs.
    2. Couple an earphone to the coupler. Set 70 dB HL at 1 kHz and read the SPL; subtract the dial value to recover the RETSPL in use. Repeat with the insert earphone and show that the SPL differs for the same dial value.
    3. Step the attenuator from 70 to 40 dB HL in 5 dB steps and record the coupler readings. Students compute the step errors.
    4. Show the rise and fall: record the output with an oscilloscope or audio interface and compare the tone switch with a hard-switched tone from a signal generator; listen for the click.
    5. Measure the frequency of the 4 kHz tone with the analyser and compute the percentage error.
    6. Test the volunteer's thresholds at 1 kHz with the modified Hughson-Westlake procedure while students record every presentation and response on a sheet like Figure 6.1c.
    7. Simulate a unilateral loss by occluding one ear with a foam plug, show the change in AC and the unchanged BC, and show why masking is required. Run the plateau procedure while students follow it on the u6-1-masking demo.
    8. Plot the results in the u6-1-audiogram demo and discuss type, degree and masking flags.
    9. Finish with a full daily listening check and log entry.

    What students should observe: the dial reads the same number for different SPLs depending on the transducer; attenuator steps are accurate to a fraction of a dB; a hard-switched tone clicks; occlusion creates an air-bone gap in the low frequencies; shadow thresholds disappear under adequate masking.

    Current developments

    • Boothless audiometry with high-attenuation earphones and continuous ambient-noise monitoring, allowing testing in clinics without a sound-treated room.
    • Automated and tablet-based audiometry for screening and tele-audiology, with automated masking and quality indicators; validation studies continue for different populations.
    • Adaptive threshold estimation (Bayesian and machine-learning methods) that estimates the whole audiogram with fewer presentations by modelling the psychometric function across frequency.
    • Wider use of extended high-frequency audiometry in ototoxicity monitoring and in research on hidden or early hearing damage.
    • Networked audiometers with direct export of results to electronic health records and standardised data formats.
    • Calibration and quality control of consumer devices (headphones, earbuds) used in hearing self-tests; these remain screening tools unless validated with calibrated hardware.

    Practical activity and assessment

    Practical P8, audiometer instrumentation. Work in pairs.

    1. Complete the daily listening check on a clinic audiometer and fill in the log sheet.
    2. With a sound level meter and coupler, measure the output at 70 dB HL at 250, 1000 and 4000 Hz for the supra-aural earphone. Compare with the RETSPL table in use and state whether each is within a ±3 dB tolerance.
    3. Measure attenuator linearity at 1 kHz from 90 to 40 dB HL; tabulate step errors and cumulative error.
    4. Obtain AC thresholds (both ears) and BC thresholds (one ear, masked where required) on your partner. Record every presentation at 1 kHz for one ear and show the threshold decision.
    5. Plot the audiogram with correct symbols; compute PTA3 and PTA4, air-bone gaps, and state the degree using the WHO 2021 grades.
    6. Deliverable: a two-page report with the calibration table, the audiogram, the masking calculations for one frequency (Mmin, Mmax, plateau) and a short interpretation.

    Assessment: correctness of calibration arithmetic, correct procedure and symbols, correct masking decisions, and clarity of interpretation.
