    6.2

    BERA / ABR instrumentation

    Supplementary3 hoursPractical P9Practical P8

    Brainstem evoked response audiometry (BERA), more often called auditory brainstem response (ABR) testing, records the sub-microvolt electrical activity of the auditory nerve and brainstem that follows a brief sound. The instrument is a precisely timed stimulus generator joined to a very low-noise biopotential recorder and a synchronous averager. Understanding each block explains why the test estimates hearing thresholds in infants, detects neural disorders, and why it sometimes fails.

    What it is

    A computer-controlled system that presents calibrated clicks, tone bursts or chirps through insert earphones, supra-aural earphones or a bone vibrator, and records the scalp voltage between electrodes on the vertex (or forehead) and the mastoid or earlobe. Thousands of recording sweeps, each started by the stimulus trigger, are averaged to reveal waves I to V within the first 10 to 15 ms.

    Why it is required

    The ABR needs no behavioural response. It is the standard objective method for confirming hearing loss after a failed newborn screen, estimating frequency-specific thresholds in infants and difficult-to-test patients, identifying auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder, screening for retrocochlear pathology and monitoring the auditory nerve and brainstem during surgery.

    How it works

    Each stimulus produces the same small response at the same delay. Background EEG and muscle activity, ten to a hundred times larger, are random with respect to the stimulus. A differential amplifier rejects interference common to both electrodes, filters limit the bandwidth, noisy sweeps are rejected, and averaging N sweeps reduces random noise by about √N while the response stays constant. Statistics such as Fsp and replication correlation decide whether a response is present.

    Key ideaThese notes build on the general AEP chain in 4.4 (including its averaging demo) and on filter theory in 1.8. Electrodes, montages, impedance and artefact control are treated in depth in 6.3; the ASSR is in 6.4; ECochG, MLR, cortical responses and eABR are in 6.5. Here the focus is the complete ABR instrument, its settings and the interpretation that depends on them.

    1. Basic working principle

    What the ABR is

    An evoked potential is a voltage change in the nervous system that is caused by, and time-locked to, a stimulus. When an abrupt sound reaches the cochlea, the basal turn of the basilar membrane moves first, inner hair cells release neurotransmitter and thousands of auditory nerve fibres fire within a fraction of a millisecond of each other. This near-simultaneous firing (neural synchrony) is carried along the ascending auditory pathway through a series of brainstem nuclei. Each time a large group of neurons fires together, or a volley of action potentials crosses a boundary where the conducting tissue changes direction or geometry, the summed current produces a small potential at the scalp. The ABR is the sequence of these potentials, labelled with Roman numerals I to VII in the order they appear. Waves I, III and V are the most reliable and are the ones measured clinically.

    The ABR is described as an onset response. It is driven mainly by the synchronous discharge at the start of a stimulus, which is why brief stimuli with fast rise times (clicks, short tone bursts, chirps) are used. A slowly rising tone produces little ABR because the fibres fire at slightly different times and their contributions smear out.

    Generators of waves I to V

    The link between each wave and an anatomical structure comes from intracranial recordings made during neurosurgery, lesion studies and animal work. The current understanding, summarised in standard texts such as Hall's handbook and Møller's work, is that waves I and II arise from the auditory nerve, and later waves reflect several brainstem structures firing in overlapping time windows. Each wave may have more than one generator, and one structure can contribute to more than one wave, so the table below is a simplified guide rather than a one-to-one map.

    Figure 6.2a. Simplified view of the generators of the ABR. The cochlear microphonic (CM) and summating potential (SP) come from hair cells and are seen best with ECochG (6.5). AN: auditory nerve; SOC: superior olivary complex; LL: lateral lemniscus; IC: inferior colliculus.

    WaveTypical latency, high-level click, adultMain generator (current understanding)Clinical use
    Iabout 1.5 to 1.7 msDistal (cochlear end) of the auditory nerve, ipsilateral to the stimulated earMarks the cochlear output; reference point for interpeak intervals; small or absent in many high-frequency losses
    IIabout 2.6 to 2.9 msProximal auditory nerve as it enters the brainstemNot usually measured; often merged with I or III
    IIIabout 3.6 to 3.9 msCochlear nucleus region in the lower pons, with contributions from the proximal nerve and trapezoid bodyDivides the I-V interval into a lower (I-III) and upper (III-V) segment
    IVabout 4.7 to 5.1 msSuperior olivary complex region, often fused with V as a IV/V complexRarely measured separately; the IV/V complex shape varies between normal ears
    Vabout 5.5 to 6 msTermination of the lateral lemniscus as it enters the inferior colliculus, mainly contralateral to the stimulated earLargest and most robust wave; followed by a broad negative trough (SN10); the wave tracked to threshold

    These latencies are typical values only. Every clinic should use normative data collected on its own equipment and protocol, because latency depends on the stimulus, the transducer, the level calibration, the filter and the way peaks are marked.

    Far-field recording

    In a near-field recording, an electrode lies close to the generator: for example a needle electrode on the promontory in transtympanic ECochG, or an electrode placed directly on the auditory nerve during surgery. Near-field responses can be tens of microvolts. The ABR at the scalp is a far-field recording: the generators are several centimetres deep, and the currents they produce spread through brain, cerebrospinal fluid, skull and scalp, which act as a volume conductor. By the time the field reaches the surface, it has been attenuated and spatially smoothed, and the scalp electrodes see only the component of the dipole field that projects onto the line between them.

    Two practical consequences follow. First, amplitudes are small: wave V is typically about 0.3 to 0.7 µV in a normal adult at high click levels, and wave I is often smaller. Second, the electrode positions determine which components are seen well. A vertex (Cz) to ipsilateral mastoid montage places the mastoid electrode close to the cochlea and auditory nerve, so wave I appears as a negative-going deflection at the mastoid that is displayed as a positive peak in the vertex-minus-mastoid difference. Wave V has a mainly vertical dipole orientation and is seen well at the vertex. This is why the same brain response looks different in the ipsilateral and contralateral channels.

    The signal-to-noise problem

    The scalp voltage in any single sweep is the sum of the ABR and much larger activities that have nothing to do with the stimulus:

    Signal at the scalpTypical sizeFrequency contentRelation to stimulus
    ABR wave Vabout 0.3 to 0.7 µV (normal adult, high level)mainly about 100 to 1000 Hz, with sharper early waves up to about 1.5 to 2 kHztime-locked (what we want)
    Spontaneous EEGtens of µV peak to peakmostly below 30 to 40 Hz, some energy higherrandom
    Muscle (myogenic) activity: neck, jaw, forehead, eyestens to hundreds of µV when tensebroadband, about 20 Hz to several kHz, overlapping the ABR bandrandom, occasionally reflex (post-auricular muscle)
    Mains interference (50 Hz in India and Europe, 60 Hz in the Americas) and harmonicsmillivolts to volts of common-mode voltage on the body50 Hz, 100 Hz, 150 Hz and so onperiodic; locked only if the stimulus rate is badly chosen
    Electrode and amplifier noisebelow about 1 µV RMS in the pass-band with good electrodesbroadbandrandom
    Stimulus artefact and cochlear microphoniccan exceed the ABR near time zerofollows the stimulus waveformtime-locked, polarity dependent

    In a relaxed or sleeping patient, the residual background in the ABR pass-band is typically a few to about 20 µV RMS per sweep. With a 0.5 µV wave V, the single-sweep signal-to-noise ratio is somewhere between about 1:10 and 1:40. The ABR is invisible in one sweep. Every stage of the instrument exists to improve this ratio: electrode preparation lowers noise pick-up, the differential amplifier removes common-mode interference, filters remove noise outside the ABR band, artefact rejection removes the worst sweeps, and averaging removes the remainder. Time-locked unwanted signals (stimulus artefact, cochlear microphonic) are not removed by averaging and need other methods such as alternating polarity and insert earphones.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    An ABR system has two paths joined by a trigger. The stimulus path converts a digital waveform into a calibrated sound in the ear. The recording path converts microvolt scalp potentials into a digital average. The trigger ensures that sample number k in every sweep corresponds to the same time after stimulus onset, which is the whole basis of averaging.

    Figure 6.2b. Complete BERA / ABR system. Top row: stimulus path. Middle row: recording path, running from the patient back towards the computer. Bottom row: digital processing. The dashed trigger line is the link that makes averaging possible: every sweep of the ADC starts at a fixed time relative to stimulus onset. The isolation barrier separates patient-connected circuits from mains-powered equipment.

    Walk-through of the stimulus path

    1. Controller. The software holds the protocol (stimulus type, level, rate, polarity, transducer, masking, filter, window, sweep count and rejection limit), the calibration table for each transducer, the normative data and the patient record.
    2. Stimulus generator. A digital waveform (click, tone burst or chirp) is computed or read from memory. At each stimulus onset the generator issues a trigger pulse to the recording side.
    3. Digital-to-analogue converter (DAC) and attenuator. The DAC converts the samples to a voltage. Level is set by digital scaling, a programmable attenuator, or both, so that the full DAC resolution is used even at low levels. Polarity is set here by the sign of the waveform.
    4. Power amplifier. It drives the low-impedance transducer with enough current and without clipping at the highest levels (clicks often up to about 95 to 100 dB nHL).
    5. Transducer. Insert earphones, supra-aural earphones or a bone vibrator convert the voltage to sound pressure in the ear canal or vibration of the skull. A second channel of the DAC delivers contralateral masking noise when required.

    Walk-through of the recording path

    1. Electrodes. Disposable pre-gelled electrodes or reusable silver/silver-chloride discs convert ionic current in the skin to electronic current in the leads. Low and balanced impedances are essential (6.3).
    2. Head box (preamplifier). The first amplifier stage sits close to the patient, on a short cable. Keeping the high-impedance, microvolt-level part of the circuit short reduces pick-up of electric and magnetic fields and cable movement artefact. The head box contains the differential input stage with high input impedance and high common-mode rejection, input protection, and the patient isolation barrier (often an isolated power supply and optical or transformer coupling of the signal). Some systems place a first amplifier directly at the electrode (active electrodes, or the approach Vivosonic markets as the Amplitrode).
    3. Filters. Analogue filters limit the bandwidth before digitisation (anti-aliasing), and further digital band-pass filtering shapes the recording to the ABR band.
    4. ADC. The analogue-to-digital converter samples the amplified signal at a fixed rate for a fixed window after each trigger. Modern systems often use 16- to 24-bit converters.
    5. Artefact rejection. Each sweep is tested against an amplitude limit and either accepted into the average or discarded.
    6. Averager. Accepted sweeps are added into one of two buffers (A and B, often alternate sweeps), giving two independent replications and their combined average.
    7. Detection and display. The software computes residual noise, Fsp or related statistics and replication correlation, displays the running average, and lets the clinician mark peaks and print a report.

    Components and what goes wrong

    ComponentFunctionWhat can go wrong
    Stimulus generator and DACCreates click, tone-burst or chirp waveform with correct timing, polarity and levelWrong protocol or polarity selected; level calibration out of date; trigger timing offset
    Attenuator and power amplifierSets level over a range of about 100 dB; drives transducerClipping at high levels (spectral splatter); noise floor audible at low levels
    Insert earphone and tubeDelivers sound to the ear canal; delays it by the tube travel timeKinked or blocked tube, foam tip not inserted, wrong tube length for the calibration, tube delay not corrected
    Supra-aural earphoneDelivers sound when inserts cannot be usedEar canal collapse; large electromagnetic stimulus artefact; poor interaural attenuation
    Bone vibratorDelivers BC stimulus for BC-ABRPlacement and contact force vary; large artefact; limited maximum output
    Electrodes, leadsPick up scalp potentialsHigh or unbalanced impedance, dried gel, lead loops or movement
    Head box, differential amplifierAmplifies difference signal, rejects common-mode, isolates patientSaturation from large offset or artefact, poor CMRR at mains frequency, damaged input
    FiltersLimit bandwidth to the ABR bandWrong settings distort waves or shift latency; notch filter ringing
    ADCSamples each sweepClipping, too-short analysis window for low levels or infants
    Artefact rejectionDiscards noisy sweepsLimit too tight (nothing accepted) or too loose (noisy sweeps dominate)
    Averager and statisticsSums sweeps, estimates noise, tests for responseStopping too early; trusting a number without inspecting the waveforms
    Software, norms and reportApplies corrections, stores resultsWrong normative set (age, transducer), wrong nHL-to-eHL correction

    Timing of a single sweep

    The trigger starts a sweep. The ADC then records a fixed analysis window, for example 15 ms, and often a short pre-stimulus baseline. The sweep is tested for artefact and either added to a buffer or discarded. The system then waits for the next stimulus. At 21.1 stimuli per second the interval between stimuli is 1/21.1 = 47.4 ms, so only about a third of the time is spent recording; the rest is waiting for the auditory system to recover.

    Figure 6.2c. Timing of a single sweep at 21.1 stimuli per second. The trigger, the stimulus and the start of the analysis window coincide (or differ by a fixed, known offset such as the insert tube delay). Only sweeps that pass the artefact test are added to the average.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    The stimulus has to do two conflicting jobs. It must be brief and abrupt, so that many nerve fibres fire together and produce a large, sharp response. It should also be frequency-specific, so that the response tells us about hearing in one part of the cochlea. A very short signal has a wide spectrum and a long signal has a narrow one: the product of duration and bandwidth cannot be made arbitrarily small. The three stimulus families used in ABR sit at different points on this trade-off.

    Click

    The standard click is a rectangular electrical pulse, typically 100 µs long, applied to the transducer. The spectrum of a rectangular pulse of duration T has a sinc shape:

    |X(f)| = A T |sin(π f T) / (π f T)| first spectral null: f = 1 / T where A is the pulse amplitude, T the pulse duration (s) and f the frequency (Hz).

    Worked example: for T = 100 µs the first null is at 1/0.0001 = 10 000 Hz, and the spectrum falls by 3 dB at about 0.443/T = 4.4 kHz. The electrical click is therefore flat to a few kilohertz. The acoustic click actually delivered to the ear is shaped by the transducer, which rings and has limited high-frequency response, so the energy reaching the ear is typically concentrated roughly between 500 Hz and 4 to 6 kHz, depending on the earphone. Because the basal (high-frequency) end of the cochlea is excited first and its fibres fire most synchronously, the click ABR mainly reflects hearing in about the 2 to 4 kHz region. A normal click ABR does not exclude a low-frequency hearing loss, and a click threshold cannot describe the audiogram shape.

    Tone bursts (tone pips)

    A tone burst is a short sinusoid switched on and off with a shaped envelope called a gate or window. Durations are often expressed in cycles of the carrier, so that the same protocol gives a proportionally similar bandwidth at each frequency. A common linear-gated design is 2-1-2: a rise of 2 cycles, a plateau of 1 cycle and a fall of 2 cycles. Another common design is a Blackman-windowed burst, typically with a total of about 4 to 5 cycles and no plateau. The Blackman window has much lower spectral side lobes than a linear (trapezoidal) ramp, so less energy spreads to frequencies far from the carrier (less spectral splatter). This improves frequency specificity, especially at high stimulus levels.

    cycle duration = 1 / f rise = 2/f, plateau = 1/f, fall = 2/f, total = 5/f Blackman: w(n) = 0.42 - 0.5 cos(2πn/(M-1)) + 0.08 cos(4πn/(M-1)) where f is the carrier frequency, n = 0, 1, ..., M-1 is the sample index and M the number of samples in the burst.
    CarrierOne cycle2-1-2 linear gate: rise / plateau / fallTotal duration
    500 Hz2 ms4 / 2 / 4 ms10 ms
    1000 Hz1 ms2 / 1 / 2 ms5 ms
    2000 Hz0.5 ms1 / 0.5 / 1 ms2.5 ms
    4000 Hz0.25 ms0.5 / 0.25 / 0.5 ms1.25 ms

    Consequences for recording follow directly. The 500 Hz burst lasts 10 ms, and the low-frequency region of the cochlea is reached later along the travelling wave, so the 500 Hz tone-burst wave V appears later (often around 8 to 12 ms at moderate levels in adults and later still in infants) and is broader. The analysis window must therefore be longer (about 20 to 25 ms) and the high-pass filter lower (about 30 Hz) so that the broad low-frequency wave V is not attenuated. Tone-burst ABR is the main tool for estimating an audiogram in infants, typically at 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz, and for bone conduction usually at 500 and 2000 Hz or 4000 Hz, following the local protocol.

    RememberFrequency specificity has two parts: the acoustic spectrum of the stimulus (controlled by the gate) and the place of excitation in the cochlea. At high levels, and in ears with a steeply sloping loss, a low-frequency burst can still produce a response from the better high-frequency region. Notched-noise masking is used in some research and clinical protocols to restrict the response to the region around the carrier.

    Chirps

    In a click, all frequencies leave the earphone at the same instant, but the travelling wave reaches the low-frequency (apical) regions several milliseconds after the high-frequency (basal) regions. Neurons along the cochlea therefore fire at slightly different times, and their contributions partly cancel in the far-field sum. A chirp is a stimulus in which the low frequencies are presented first and the high frequencies last, with the timing designed from a model of cochlear delay so that all regions are excited at nearly the same moment. The result is a more synchronous neural discharge and typically a larger wave V than a click at the same level, often described as roughly 1.5 to 2 times larger in normal-hearing ears at moderate levels.

    Chirps come in two main forms. Broadband chirps are used like clicks (threshold screening, neurodiagnostic work where validated). Narrow-band chirps, octave-wide around 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz, are used like tone bursts for frequency-specific thresholds. The CE-Chirp family, described by Elberling and colleagues and implemented in Interacoustics systems, is a well-known example. Because cochlear delays become shorter as level increases, level-specific versions (for example the LS CE-Chirp) change the chirp duration with stimulus level, making the chirp shorter at high levels. Chirp latencies are not directly comparable with click latencies, because the definition of time zero depends on the chirp design; they need their own normative values, and for neurodiagnostic latency interpretation many clinics still use clicks.

    Stimulus polarity

    The first movement of the earphone diaphragm defines polarity:

    • Rarefaction: the diaphragm first moves away from the eardrum, producing an initial negative pressure. This moves the basilar membrane towards scala vestibuli, the direction that depolarises inner hair cells, so rarefaction clicks often give a slightly shorter latency and a clearer wave I in many ears.
    • Condensation: the diaphragm first pushes towards the eardrum, producing an initial positive pressure.
    • Alternating: successive stimuli alternate between rarefaction and condensation, so half the sweeps are of each polarity.

    Two time-locked contaminants follow the stimulus waveform itself: the stimulus artefact (the electromagnetic field of the transducer and its lead coupling directly into the electrodes) and the cochlear microphonic (CM, the alternating current generated by outer and inner hair cells that follows the basilar membrane motion). Both change sign when the stimulus polarity changes. The ABR neural response, by contrast, is mainly an onset response whose shape is largely the same for both polarities. If R(t) and C(t) are the averaged responses to rarefaction and condensation stimuli:

    R(t) = N(t) + M(t) C(t) = N(t) - M(t) alternating average = (R + C)/2 = N(t) difference (R - C)/2 = M(t) where N(t) is the polarity-invariant neural response and M(t) the polarity-following part (CM plus stimulus artefact).

    Averaging equal numbers of the two polarities therefore cancels the CM and the stimulus artefact and keeps the neural response. The same algebra, used in reverse, is the basis of the auditory neuropathy test described later: recording rarefaction and condensation separately, and comparing them, shows a component that inverts with polarity (CM) and a component that does not (neural).

    Common errorAlternating polarity is not always harmless. In some ears, particularly with low-frequency tone bursts or some hearing losses, the neural latencies for rarefaction and condensation differ; adding them can broaden or partly cancel waves. With 500 Hz tone bursts, part of the response phase-locks to the carrier and inverts with polarity. Protocols therefore specify polarity by stimulus and purpose, and single-polarity averages should be compared when the alternating waveform looks unusual.

    Signals generated by the patient and the system

    SignalOriginWhere it appearsHow to handle it
    ABR waves I to V and SN10Auditory nerve and brainstemAbout 1 to 10 ms (clicks, adults); later for low frequencies, low levels and infantsThe target; average and mark
    Cochlear microphonicHair cellsFrom stimulus arrival, lasting about as long as the stimulusCancels with alternating polarity; separate polarities to identify it
    Stimulus artefactTransducer field coupling to electrodesAt electrical stimulus onset (before the acoustic arrival with inserts)Inserts, lead routing, alternating polarity, blanking the first samples
    Post-auricular muscle (PAM) reflexSound-evoked reflex of the muscle behind the earTypically after about 10 to 15 ms, larger when tenseRelaxation, head position; may look like a late large wave
    Frequency-following responseBrainstem phase-locking to low-frequency carriersDuring longer low-frequency burstsAlternating polarity reduces it; relevant to 500 Hz bursts
    Myogenic and EEG noiseMuscle, cortexThroughoutRelaxation, sleep, rejection, averaging, weighting

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Transducers

    Insert earphones (for example Etymotic ER-3A type and RadioEar IP30 type) are the first choice for ABR. A small transducer box sits away from the head and sound reaches the ear through a plastic tube and a foam or rubber tip. They offer four advantages: the transducer is further from the electrodes, so the electromagnetic stimulus artefact is smaller and separated in time from the response; interaural attenuation is higher, which reduces cross-hearing and the need for masking; ear canal collapse is avoided in infants and older adults; and the tips are hygienic and disposable.

    The tube introduces a fixed acoustic delay, equal to the tube length divided by the speed of sound:

    delay = L / c where L is the tube length (m) and c is the speed of sound in air, about 343 m/s at 20 °C.

    Worked example: a tube of 280 mm gives 0.28 / 343 = 0.00082 s, that is about 0.82 ms. With the short path inside the transducer and ear tip, common insert systems have a delay of typically about 0.8 to 1 ms for tube lengths of about 250 to 300 mm. Most software corrects for this automatically by shifting time zero, so that displayed latencies are comparable with supra-aural norms. The clinician must know whether the correction is active: an uncorrected recording shows every wave about 0.9 ms late, which could be mistaken for a conductive or retrocochlear delay. If a tube is replaced by one of a different length, both the delay correction and the calibration are affected.

    Supra-aural earphones (for example TDH-39 or DD45 types) sit on the pinna. They have no tube delay but a larger stimulus artefact close to time zero and lower interaural attenuation, and the cushion can collapse soft infant ear canals. They are still used when inserts cannot be placed.

    Bone vibrators (for example B-71 or B-81 types) are used for bone-conduction ABR (BC-ABR). The vibrator is placed on the mastoid (or, in infants, sometimes on the temporal bone above and behind the ear) with a controlled force, often with an elastic band. BC-ABR separates conductive from sensorineural components in infants. Its practical limits are a lower maximum output (often around 50 to 60 dB nHL for clicks, varying by device and frequency), a large stimulus artefact because the vibrator is near the electrodes, and near-zero interaural attenuation in adults (masking the non-test ear is needed); in young infants, skull transmission gives some interaural attenuation, which varies with age.

    Level and calibration

    Brief stimuli cannot be calibrated in the same way as steady tones because their RMS level depends on the measurement time. Two related scales are used:

    • dB peSPL (peak-equivalent sound pressure level): the SPL of a steady sinusoid whose peak-to-peak amplitude, displayed on an oscilloscope, equals that of the transient. It is a physical level measured in a coupler.
    • dB nHL (normalised hearing level): level relative to the average behavioural threshold of a group of normal-hearing young adults for that exact stimulus, transducer and rate. Reference values for short-duration signals are standardised in ISO 389-6, and the measurement methods in IEC 60645-3; many clinics also establish local biological references. For a click through insert earphones, 0 dB nHL corresponds typically to about 30 to 36 dB peSPL, depending on transducer and rate.

    A threshold of 30 dB nHL therefore means that a response was seen 30 dB above average normal behavioural threshold for that stimulus. It is not the same as a pure-tone threshold in dB HL; the conversion is discussed under threshold estimation. Calibration is checked daily by listening (biological check) and periodically with a coupler and sound level meter (4.1).

    Stimulus rate and mains synchronisation

    The stimulus rate controls test time and the state of the auditory system. A faster rate collects more sweeps per minute, but neurons need time to recover. As rate increases above about 20 to 30 per second, wave latencies increase slightly (wave V typically by a few tenths of a millisecond between about 10 and 80 per second), and waves I and III lose amplitude before wave V does. Neurodiagnostic protocols typically use about 11 to 21 per second to obtain clear early waves; threshold protocols typically use about 27 to 40 per second (sometimes higher for chirps) because only wave V is needed.

    The exact rate is chosen as an odd, non-integer value such as 11.1, 21.1, 27.7, 33.3 or 39.1 per second. The reason is mains interference. The amplifier never removes 50 Hz pick-up completely, and averaging only removes it if its phase is different in successive sweeps. If the stimulus period contains a whole number of mains cycles, the mains waveform has the same phase at the start of every sweep; it is then time-locked to the stimulus and averages exactly like a response, producing a large sinusoid in the average. If the period contains a fractional number of cycles, the phase advances each sweep and the interference cancels over many sweeps.

    cycles per sweep = f_mains / rate residue after N sweeps = |(1/N) Σ exp(j 2π k d)|, k = 0 to N-1 where d is the fractional part of the cycles per sweep. If d = 0 the residue is 1 (no cancellation).
    Rate (per s)50 Hz cycles per sweep100 Hz (2nd harmonic) cycles per sweepMains left after 2000 sweeps (fraction of single-sweep amplitude)
    105.00010.0001 at 50 and 100 Hz: fully locked, very poor
    252.0004.0001: fully locked, very poor
    202.5005.000about 0 at 50 Hz but 1 at 100 Hz: harmonic locked
    21.12.3704.739below 0.001 at both
    27.71.8053.610below 0.001 at both
    33.31.5023.003about 0.001, but only after many sweeps (see text)
    11.14.5059.009about 0.001, but only after many sweeps (see text)

    The table shows a subtle point for 50 Hz countries. At 33.3 and 11.1 per second the 100 Hz harmonic drifts by only about 0.003 and 0.009 cycles per sweep. It still cancels after thousands of sweeps, but in the first few hundred sweeps it is nearly locked: after 200 sweeps at 33.3 per second, about half of the 100 Hz component is still present. When hum is a problem, a rate such as 21.1 or 27.7 per second, or a small change of rate, is a simple remedy, alongside fixing the source (electrode impedance, lead routing, nearby equipment).

    Masking

    Sound presented to one ear can cross the head and stimulate the other cochlea, mainly by bone conduction. The minimum level difference needed for this is the interaural attenuation, typically at least about 50 dB for insert earphones for clicks and lower (around 40 dB) for supra-aural earphones, and close to 0 dB for bone conduction in adults. If the test ear has a severe loss, a high-level stimulus may produce a wave V from the non-test ear, with a delayed latency and no wave I in the ipsilateral channel. Contralateral masking with broadband noise delivered to the non-test ear prevents this. Masking is typically considered when the stimulus level exceeds the expected interaural attenuation and is almost always needed for BC-ABR in older children and adults. Levels are set by protocol, commonly with a masker level that is a fixed amount below the stimulus level in dB nHL; follow the local protocol and the equipment manual. A two-channel recording helps: a response in the contralateral channel only, without wave I in the ipsilateral channel, suggests crossover.

    Presentation sequence in practice

    1. Check the equipment: listen to the stimulus through each transducer, check the tube and tips, and confirm the protocol.
    2. Prepare the patient: explain the test, settle an adult comfortably on a couch with the neck supported, or wait for an infant to fall asleep after a feed.
    3. Prepare the skin and place electrodes (6.3); check impedances.
    4. Insert the tips (red for right, blue for left by convention) with deep, comfortable insertion.
    5. Present stimuli monaurally, one ear at a time, starting at a level and with a sequence defined by the protocol (for example a high-level click for neurodiagnosis, or a moderate-level tone burst in a descending search for threshold).
    6. Record at least two replications at each level; change level in steps (often 10 or 20 dB, then 5 or 10 dB near threshold).

    5. How the physiological response is detected and processed

    Electrodes and the standard montage (brief)

    A single ABR channel needs three electrodes: a non-inverting input (+), an inverting input (−) and a ground or common reference for the amplifier. The standard clinical montage places the non-inverting electrode at the vertex (Cz) or high forehead (Fz), the inverting electrode on the mastoid (M1 left, M2 right) or earlobe (A1, A2) of the test ear, and the ground on the low forehead (Fpz) or the contralateral mastoid. A two-channel recording adds a contralateral channel (vertex to the non-test mastoid), which helps identify wave I (small or absent contralaterally) and separate waves IV and V. Electrode sites, skin preparation, impedance measurement, montage variants and artefact control are covered in detail in 6.3.

    Figure 6.2d. Standard ABR electrode montage viewed from above. For a left-ear test the ipsilateral channel is Cz to M1 and the contralateral channel Cz to M2; roles swap for the right ear. Follow local protocol and the equipment manual.

    Preamplifier and differential amplifier

    A differential amplifier has two inputs and amplifies the difference between them. Any voltage that is identical at both inputs, called the common-mode voltage, should ideally produce no output. The patient's body picks up mains electric fields from wiring and equipment and sits at a common-mode voltage relative to the amplifier ground that can be millivolts, and in poor environments much more. This voltage is almost the same at the vertex and mastoid electrodes, while the ABR is a small difference between them. The differential amplifier therefore separates what we want (difference) from most interference (common).

    Vout = Ad (V+ - V-) + Acm (V+ + V-)/2 CMRR = Ad / Acm CMRR (dB) = 20 log10(Ad / Acm) input-referred error = Vcm / CMRR where V+ and V- are the voltages at the non-inverting and inverting inputs, Ad is the differential gain, Acm the common-mode gain and Vcm the common-mode voltage.

    Worked example: suppose poor grounding leaves 1 V of 50 Hz common-mode voltage on the patient (a large but instructive value), and the amplifier has a CMRR of 100 dB at 50 Hz.

    1. CMRR as a ratio: 10^(100/20) = 10^5 = 100 000.
    2. Input-referred error: 1 V / 100 000 = 10 µV of 50 Hz that looks like a genuine difference signal.
    3. With an overall gain of 100 000, the output contains 10 µV × 100 000 = 1 V of 50 Hz, while a 0.5 µV wave V gives only 0.05 V. The interference is 20 times larger than the response (26 dB).
    4. With 120 dB CMRR (10^6) the error falls to 1 µV; with a proper ground electrode and low common-mode voltage (say 10 mV) and 100 dB CMRR it is 0.1 µV.

    Two lessons follow. CMRR alone is not enough: the real limit is often the electrode impedance imbalance, which converts common-mode voltage into a differential error before the amplifier ever sees it (worked in 4.4 and 6.3). And what the amplifier leaves behind is removed by averaging only if the stimulus rate is not locked to the mains, as shown in section 4.

    The first stage is placed in a head box near the patient because the electrode signals are microvolts at a source impedance of kilohms. Long high-impedance leads act as antennas and move with the patient; amplifying close to the head, with short twisted or shielded leads, keeps the signal robust before it travels to the main unit. The head box also includes input protection against static discharge and a medical isolation barrier, so that no hazardous current can flow through the electrodes if a mains fault occurs (IEC 60601-1 and its particular standard for evoked response equipment set the requirements).

    Gain and digitisation

    Overall gain in classic ABR systems is typically about 100 000 (100 dB), often split between the head box (for example about 1000 to 10 000) and later stages. Gain maps microvolts onto the input range of the ADC. Systems with 24-bit converters use lower analogue gain and apply more amplification digitally, because the converter itself resolves very small voltages.

    input range = ADC range / gain input-referred step (LSB) = ADC range / (2^bits × gain) where ADC range is the full-scale span in volts and bits the converter resolution.

    Worked example: a 16-bit ADC with a span of 10 V (±5 V) and a gain of 100 000. The input range is ±5 V / 100 000 = ±50 µV. One step is 10 / (65 536 × 100 000) = 1.5 × 10^-9 V, about 1.5 nV at the input, far below the ABR. The main risk is clipping, not resolution: a movement artefact above about 50 µV saturates the converter, which is one reason artefact rejection limits are set below full scale.

    Filtering: analogue anti-alias and digital band-pass

    An analogue low-pass filter is placed before the ADC to remove energy above half the sampling rate, which would otherwise alias into the recording (1.8). After digitisation, a band-pass filter restricts the recording to the ABR band. Typical ABR settings are a high-pass edge of about 30 or 100 Hz and a low-pass edge of about 1500 to 3000 Hz. Each edge is a compromise.

    High-pass edge (30 or 100 Hz)

    • Removes electrode drift, slow EEG and much low-frequency muscle noise.
    • 100 Hz gives sharper, easier-to-mark waves I and III and a quieter trace; common for adult neurodiagnostic click ABR.
    • Wave V and its following trough (SN10) contain energy around 100 to 500 Hz. A 100 Hz or higher high-pass reduces wave V amplitude near threshold, especially in infants and for 500 Hz bursts.
    • 30 Hz is therefore preferred for threshold estimation, infants and low-frequency tone bursts, at the cost of more EEG in the trace and a need for more sweeps or a quieter patient.
    • Steep causal high-pass filters distort wave shape and can shift peaks slightly earlier.

    Low-pass edge (1500 to 3000 Hz)

    • Removes high-frequency muscle and amplifier noise.
    • 3000 Hz preserves the sharp early waves and accurate peak latency; common for neurodiagnostic work.
    • 1500 Hz gives a smoother trace and helps wave V identification near threshold, but rounds the peaks.
    • Too low a cut-off (for example below about 1000 Hz) attenuates and delays the peaks, and can merge waves.
    • Causal low-pass filters add a small delay; digital zero-phase (forward and backward) filtering avoids it for offline processing.

    Normative latencies are valid only for the filter settings with which they were collected. Changing filters on a recording to be compared with norms, or comparing ears recorded with different filters, is a common source of false interpretation.

    Common errorSwitching on a 50 Hz notch filter to hide mains hum is tempting but usually unwise for ABR. A narrow (high-Q) notch filter is a resonant system: any transient, such as the stimulus artefact or a small movement, makes it ring at 50 Hz for tens of milliseconds, adding a slow oscillation to every sweep. It also removes genuine response energy near 50 Hz and its phase shift distorts slow components such as SN10 and low-frequency wave V. Find and remove the source of the hum (impedances, ground electrode, lead routing, nearby equipment, stimulus rate) instead.

    Sampling rate and analysis window

    The sampling rate must be well above twice the highest frequency passed by the filter, and high enough to define peak latency to about 0.02 to 0.05 ms. Typical ABR systems sample at roughly 10 to 50 kHz or more. For example, 512 samples over a 12.8 ms window is a sampling interval of 25 µs (40 kHz); 1024 samples over 15 ms is 14.6 µs (about 68 kHz). The analysis window (time base) must include the latest wave expected. Typical choices are about 10 to 15 ms for click ABR in adults (wave V at threshold can reach about 8 to 9 ms) and about 20 to 25 ms for low-frequency tone bursts, low levels in infants and bone conduction. Too short a window cuts off wave V at low levels, which can lead to a false threshold estimate. Many systems also record a short pre-stimulus baseline (for example 1 to 2 ms), which shows the noise level and any stimulus artefact.

    Artefact rejection

    Sweeps that contain a large transient (swallowing, movement, jaw clench, a cry) would dominate the average, because averaging weights every sweep equally. Artefact rejection compares the peak absolute voltage in each sweep, referred to the amplifier input, with a limit and discards the sweep if the limit is exceeded. Typical limits are about ±20 to 30 µV (some protocols use tighter or looser limits, and some systems adapt the limit automatically). Many systems exclude the first 1 ms or so from the test, so that a stimulus artefact does not cause every sweep to be rejected.

    Setting the limit is a trade-off. A very tight limit rejects most sweeps in an active patient and the test never finishes. A very loose limit accepts noisy sweeps, and the residual noise stays high however many sweeps are collected. A rising rejection count is a useful signal to pause, settle the patient and check the electrodes rather than continuing to collect poor data.

    Synchronous averaging mathematics

    Model each accepted sweep k as the same response plus noise that is independent from sweep to sweep:

    x_k(t) = s(t) + n_k(t), k = 1 ... N sum: Σ x_k(t) = N s(t) + Σ n_k(t) average: x_avg(t) = s(t) + (1/N) Σ n_k(t) residual noise RMS = σ / √N amplitude SNR gain = √N; power SNR gain = 10 log10(N) dB where s(t) is the time-locked response, n_k(t) the noise in sweep k with RMS value σ, and N the number of accepted sweeps.

    The response adds coherently: N identical copies sum to N times the response. The noise adds incoherently: the variances of independent random signals add, so the sum of N noise sweeps has variance Nσ² and RMS √N σ. Dividing by N leaves the response unchanged and the noise at σ/√N.

    Worked example: background noise σ = 10 µV RMS per sweep, N = 2000 accepted sweeps, wave V = 0.5 µV.

    1. √2000 = 44.7, so the residual noise is 10 / 44.7 = 0.224 µV.
    2. Power SNR improvement: 10 log10(2000) = 33.0 dB (the same as 20 log10(44.7)).
    3. Single-sweep amplitude SNR: 0.5 / 10 = 0.05 (−26 dB). After averaging: 0.5 / 0.224 = 2.2 (about +7 dB).
    4. To halve the residual noise to 0.112 µV needs four times as many sweeps (8000). At 27.7 per second, 2000 sweeps take 2000 / 27.7 = 72 s and 8000 take nearly 5 minutes, before any rejected sweeps. This is why a quiet patient (lower σ) saves far more time than extra sweeps.

    The model rests on assumptions that are only approximately true: the response must be identical in every sweep (habituation, latency jitter or state changes smear it), the noise must be unrelated to the stimulus (stimulus artefact, CM and locked mains violate this), and the noise should be stationary (a patient who wakes and moves changes σ during the recording, which motivates weighted averaging).

    Figure 6.2e. Averaging concept. Each single sweep (red) contains the ABR at a fixed delay but it is invisible under the noise. The average of many sweeps (teal) keeps the time-locked response and reduces the random noise by √N.

    Estimating residual noise

    The clinician needs to know how much noise remains in the average, because a flat trace means "no response" only if the residual noise is small enough that a response would have been seen. Two estimates are common:

    • Plus-minus (±) reference: if the sweeps are split into two buffers A and B, the half-difference (A − B)/2 contains no response (it cancels) and noise with the same variance as the full average. Its RMS over the window estimates the residual noise directly.
    • Single-point variance: the voltage at one fixed time point is collected across all sweeps. Its variance across sweeps is the single-sweep noise variance at that point, and dividing by N gives the variance of the noise in the average. This is the basis of Fsp.
    RN_pm = RMS over window of (A(t) - B(t)) / 2 RN_sp = √( Var_k[x_k(t_sp)] / N ) where A and B are the two sub-averages, t_sp the chosen single time point and Var_k the variance across sweeps k.

    Protocols often set a target residual noise level (commonly a few tens of nanovolts) that must be reached before a "no response" decision is accepted at a given level. Follow local guidance, which is typically stated for a specific equipment family.

    The Fsp statistic and Fmp

    Elberling and Don described the Fsp statistic in 1984 as an objective measure of averaged ABR quality. It compares the variance of the averaged waveform across time within a response window with the estimated variance of the noise in that average:

    Fsp = VAR(S) / VAR(SP) VAR(S) = variance across time of x_avg(t) within the window VAR(SP) = Var_k[x_k(t_sp)] / N where VAR(S) contains response plus residual noise and VAR(SP) is the residual noise variance estimated from a single point.

    If there is no response, both variances estimate the same residual noise and Fsp is close to 1. If a response is present, VAR(S) grows and Fsp rises. Because the samples in the window are strongly correlated after filtering, the numerator has few effective degrees of freedom; Elberling and Don used about 5 for the numerator and a large number (about 250) for the denominator. With those degrees of freedom, the F distribution gives critical values of about 2.25 for 95% and about 3.1 for 99% confidence, so Fsp of 3.1 or more is commonly taken as a response present with about 99% confidence. The degrees of freedom depend on filter settings and window length, so the critical value belongs to a protocol, not to Fsp in general.

    Worked example: in a threshold search, the averaged waveform has a variance across the window of 0.010 µV² (RMS 0.1 µV) and the single-point estimate of residual noise is 0.05 µV (variance 0.0025 µV²). Fsp = 0.010 / 0.0025 = 4.0, above 3.1, so the response would be accepted at about the 99% level. If the residual noise were 0.08 µV (variance 0.0064 µV²), Fsp would be 0.010 / 0.0064 = 1.6 and more sweeps would be needed.

    The single-point estimate is noisy, because it rests on one time point. The multiple-point variant, Fmp, developed from the same line of work and implemented in some commercial systems (Interacoustics documents it for its Eclipse platform), estimates the noise from several points across the window, giving a more stable noise estimate and therefore a more stable statistic. Other systems use related statistics such as Hotelling's T² or correlation-based measures. All of them answer the same question: is the waveform larger than would be expected from noise alone?

    Weighted averaging

    Ordinary averaging gives every sweep equal weight. If a patient is quiet for half the test and restless for the other half, the restless sweeps add a lot of noise even when they pass the rejection limit. Weighted averaging gives each sweep, or each block of sweeps, a weight inversely proportional to its noise variance, which is the minimum-variance way of combining measurements of the same quantity. Bayesian weighted averaging (described by Elberling and Wahlgreen) and Kalman-weighted averaging (used by Vivosonic) are examples.

    x_w(t) = Σ w_b x_b(t) / Σ w_b, w_b = N_b / σ_b² residual variance = 1 / Σ (N_b / σ_b²) where x_b(t) is the average of block b, N_b the number of sweeps in the block and σ_b² its single-sweep noise variance.

    Worked example: 1000 sweeps with σ = 5 µV and 1000 sweeps with σ = 20 µV.

    • Plain average of all 2000: noise variance = (1000 × 25 + 1000 × 400) / 2000² = 425 000 / 4 000 000 = 0.106 µV², residual RMS 0.33 µV.
    • Quiet block only: 5 / √1000 = 0.158 µV.
    • Weighted average: 1 / (1000/25 + 1000/400) = 1 / 42.5 = 0.0235 µV², residual RMS 0.153 µV, slightly better than using the quiet block alone and half the plain average.

    Replications and cross-correlation

    A single average can contain a noise bump that looks like a wave. The classical safeguard is replication: record two averages under identical conditions and accept a peak only if it appears at the same latency in both. Most systems do this automatically by sending alternate sweeps into buffers A and B (the split-buffer method) and overlaying them. The similarity can be quantified with the Pearson correlation coefficient over a window that contains the expected response:

    r = Σ (a_i - a_mean)(b_i - b_mean) / √( Σ(a_i - a_mean)² × Σ(b_i - b_mean)² ) where a_i and b_i are the samples of the two replications within the window.

    r close to 1 means the two traces have the same shape; r near 0 means they are unrelated, as for two noise-only averages. Because each buffer contains only half the sweeps, its residual noise is √2 larger than that of the combined average, so r depends strongly on how much noise remains. Correlation criteria (for example r of about 0.7 or more over a defined window) are set by protocol. A correlation can also be computed with a small time shift allowed, to tolerate slight latency differences.

    import numpy as np
from scipy.signal import butter, sosfiltfilt
fs = 20000                                   # samples per second
t = np.arange(0, 0.012, 1/fs) * 1000         # 12 ms window, in ms
g = lambda L, a, w: a * np.exp(-((t - L) / w)**2)
s = g(1.6, .28, .16) + g(3.75, .26, .2) + g(5.65, .36, .24) + g(6.6, -.24, .5)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
N = 4000
sos = butter(2, [100, 3000], btype="band", fs=fs, output="sos")
eeg = sosfiltfilt(sos, rng.normal(size=(N, t.size)), axis=1)
eeg *= 3 / eeg.std()                         # 3 µV RMS per sweep
sweeps = s + eeg
A = sweeps[0::2].mean(axis=0)                # buffer A (odd sweeps)
B = sweeps[1::2].mean(axis=0)                # buffer B (even sweeps)
avg = sweeps.mean(axis=0)
print("predicted residual noise:", round(3 / np.sqrt(N), 3), "uV")
print("(A - B)/2 estimate:      ", round(np.std((A - B) / 2), 3), "uV")
print("correlation r(A, B):     ", round(np.corrcoef(A, B)[0, 1], 2))
k = 113                                      # fixed single point, 5.65 ms
fsp = np.var(avg) / (np.var(sweeps[:, k]) / N)
print("Fsp:", round(fsp, 1))
    predicted residual noise: 0.047 uV
(A - B)/2 estimate:       0.042 uV
correlation r(A, B):      0.7
Fsp: 6.6

    The simulated patient is quiet (3 µV RMS per sweep in the 100 to 3000 Hz band). After 4000 sweeps the residual noise is about 0.05 µV, the plus-minus reference agrees with the √N prediction, the replications correlate at r = 0.7 and Fsp is well above 3.1. Try reducing N to 1000 or raising the noise: r and Fsp both fall, and the response becomes uncertain.

    Response detection criteria

    A response is usually accepted when several criteria agree:

    1. Replicability: the wave is present at the same latency in two replications (visual overlay, correlation).
    2. Size relative to noise: wave V amplitude is typically at least about 2 to 3 times the residual noise, or a statistic (Fsp, Fmp or similar) exceeds its criterion.
    3. Plausible latency: the latency fits the stimulus, level and age, and follows the latency-intensity trend from higher levels.
    4. Consistency: a response at a lower level implies responses at higher levels; its absence at a higher level suggests an error.

    A response is judged absent only when the residual noise is low enough that a response of plausible size would have been detected. "No response, residual noise 0.2 µV" is not the same finding as "no response, residual noise 0.03 µV". Reports should record the residual noise or the statistic, the number of sweeps and the rejection count.

    Try it: a simulated ABR system

    Try it: simulated ABR recording

    Change the stimulus and recording settings and watch the average: lower levels delay and shrink wave V, fast rates add a small delay, single polarities show the cochlear microphonic and stimulus artefact that alternating polarity cancels, filters reshape the waves, and few sweeps leave noise. The time axis is corrected for insert tube delay, so the electrical stimulus artefact appears just before 0 ms.

    The values below are typical of clinical ABR protocols and equipment. They vary between manufacturers, protocols and national guidelines; always follow the local protocol and the equipment manual.

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    The product families below are examples of diagnostic evoked-potential platforms that include ABR, confirmed from manufacturer and published sources. They differ in stimulus libraries (for example chirp families), detection statistics, weighting methods, amplifier design and integration with ASSR, OAE and other tests.

    ManufacturerProduct family (example)Notes relevant to this topic

    Automated ABR (AABR) screeners used in newborn hearing screening, from several of these and other manufacturers, apply the same principles with a fixed stimulus (often a click or chirp at about 35 dB nHL), fixed electrodes and an automatic pass or refer decision based on a detection statistic. They are screening devices and do not estimate thresholds.

    RememberExamples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation. No endorsement is implied, and specifications should be taken from the current manual of the actual device, not from these notes.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation basics

    Reading the waveform

    An ABR report shows the averaged waveforms (usually two replications per condition) with the stimulus conditions. Conventionally vertex-positive is plotted upwards in many clinics, but some display the opposite; check the display convention before reading any waveform. Peaks are marked at their highest point (or at a defined point on a broad peak, such as the shoulder of a IV/V complex, according to local convention).

    Figure 6.2f. Idealised adult ABR to a high-level click (vertex positive up). Absolute latency is measured from stimulus arrival to each peak; interpeak intervals are differences between absolute latencies; wave V amplitude is commonly measured from the peak to the following trough (SN10). Values are typical and illustrative only.

    Absolute latencies and interpeak intervals

    MeasureTypical adult value, click about 80 dB nHLCommonly used caution limit (illustrative)Meaning when prolonged
    Wave I latencyabout 1.5 to 1.7 msabove about 2.0 msConductive loss or high-frequency cochlear loss (the stimulus reaches effective threshold later)
    Wave III latencyabout 3.6 to 3.9 msabove about 4.2 msPeripheral or lower brainstem delay
    Wave V latencyabout 5.5 to 6.0 msabove about 6.2 to 6.4 msAny delay along the pathway, including peripheral
    I-III intervalabout 2.0 to 2.2 msabove about 2.4 to 2.5 msDelay between distal nerve and lower pons (for example a cerebellopontine angle lesion)
    III-V intervalabout 1.8 to 2.0 msabove about 2.3 to 2.4 msUpper brainstem delay
    I-V intervalabout 3.9 to 4.1 msabove about 4.4 to 4.5 msCentral conduction delay; largely independent of peripheral loss
    Interaural wave V differenceusually below about 0.2 msabove about 0.3 to 0.4 msAsymmetry suggesting retrocochlear involvement, after allowing for hearing asymmetry

    The interpeak intervals are the key neurodiagnostic measures because a peripheral hearing loss delays all waves roughly equally, leaving the intervals normal, whereas a lesion along the nerve or brainstem prolongs the interval that spans it. Where wave I is absent (common with high-frequency loss), ECochG-style electrodes in the ear canal (tiptrodes) or a higher level can enhance it; otherwise the wave V latency must be interpreted with a correction for the degree of hearing loss, applied cautiously according to local practice.

    Amplitude and the V/I ratio

    Absolute amplitudes vary widely with head size, electrode position, noise and filter settings, so they are rarely interpreted alone. The ratio of wave V amplitude to wave I amplitude is more stable. In normal adults, wave V is usually larger than wave I (ratio above 1). A V/I ratio below about 0.5 is commonly regarded as abnormal and may indicate a brainstem conduction problem. Both amplitudes must be measured the same way, for example peak to following trough.

    Latency-intensity function

    As level decreases, fewer and more apical fibres contribute, synchrony falls and the travelling wave takes longer to reach the effective place, so wave V latency increases and amplitude decreases. A plot of wave V latency against level is the latency-intensity function (LIF). In normal adults with clicks, wave V moves from about 5.5 to 6 ms at 80 dB nHL to roughly 7.5 to 8.5 ms near threshold, a change of about 0.3 to 0.4 ms per 10 dB over much of the range. The shape of the LIF helps distinguish types of loss:

    • Conductive loss: the effective level at the cochlea is reduced by the air-bone gap, so the whole function shifts to the right (higher threshold) and upwards by about the latency expected for that level reduction. Wave I is delayed too and the I-V interval is normal. BC-ABR is closer to normal.
    • Cochlear (sensory) loss, especially sloping high-frequency: threshold is raised; latencies near threshold are prolonged, but at high levels they approach normal. The LIF is steep, a pattern linked to loudness recruitment. I-V interval normal or slightly short.
    • Retrocochlear lesion: wave V latency is prolonged at all levels by a roughly constant amount, so the LIF lies above the normal region and roughly parallel to it; the I-V interval is prolonged. Threshold may be near normal or raised, morphology may be poor, and waves may be absent at high rates.

    Try it: latency-intensity function patterns

    Choose a pattern and a degree, and compare the wave V latencies (points) with the shaded normal region. The shading and all patterns are illustrative models, not clinical norms.

    Wave V at 80 dB nHL

    Threshold estimation and correction factors

    For threshold estimation, wave V is followed down in level (for example in 10 or 20 dB steps, then 5 or 10 dB steps) until it can no longer be detected with adequate residual noise. The lowest level with a reliable response is the ABR threshold in dB nHL. Even in normal ears this is typically somewhat above behavioural threshold, because the ABR must rise above the residual noise; the difference depends on stimulus frequency, the noise achieved and the detection criteria.

    To convert an ABR threshold to an estimate of the behavioural threshold, many protocols subtract a frequency-specific correction to give an estimated hearing level (eHL):

    eHL = ABR threshold (dB nHL) - correction(f) where the correction is specific to the stimulus, frequency, transducer and protocol, and is typically largest at 500 Hz.

    Worked example with illustrative numbers only: if a protocol specifies a correction of 10 dB at 2000 Hz and the tone-burst ABR threshold is 50 dB nHL, the estimated threshold is 40 dB eHL. The actual corrections differ between programmes, stimuli (tone bursts versus narrow-band chirps) and calibration methods; they must be taken from the protocol that matches the equipment and calibration in use, never from a different programme. The estimate has an uncertainty of typically around ±10 dB, and the result is used to fit hearing aids in infants alongside later behavioural testing.

    Maturation in infants

    The auditory pathway myelinates and its synapses mature during the first years of life. At term, wave I latency is near adult values or slightly prolonged, but waves III and V are later and the I-V interval is longer (roughly around 5 ms at term compared with about 4 ms in adults). Wave V latency and the interpeak intervals shorten over the first 18 months to 2 years, approaching adult values. Premature infants show even longer latencies. The practical consequences are: use age-specific (corrected-age) norms; use a longer analysis window (20 to 25 ms for tone bursts); use a lower high-pass setting (30 Hz) because infant waves are broader; expect wave V to dominate and waves I and III to be less clear; and remember that infant ear canals and middle ears change the effective stimulus level. Bone-conduction responses in infants are relatively larger at low frequencies than in adults because of the immature skull.

    Retrocochlear signs

    A lesion of the auditory nerve or brainstem, such as a vestibular schwannoma, can desynchronise or slow conduction. Typical ABR signs include:

    • Prolonged I-III or I-V interval on the affected side.
    • Interaural wave V latency difference above about 0.3 to 0.4 ms (with correction for asymmetric hearing loss).
    • Absent later waves with a present wave I, or absent ABR with only a mild or moderate hearing loss.
    • Reduced V/I ratio and poor waveform morphology or replicability.
    • Abnormal deterioration of waves at high stimulus rates.

    MRI with contrast is the reference method for detecting such lesions, and small tumours can give a normal ABR. The ABR remains useful where MRI is unavailable, contraindicated or waiting times are long, and as a functional measure of the nerve.

    Auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder (ANSD)

    In ANSD the outer hair cells function (OAEs and/or a cochlear microphonic are present) but the neural response is absent or severely abnormal, because of inner hair cell, synaptic or auditory nerve dysfunction. Behavioural thresholds can range from normal to profound, and speech understanding is often poorer than the audiogram predicts. The ABR is central to the diagnosis:

    1. Record high-level click ABRs (for example about 80 to 90 dB nHL) with insert earphones, separately with rarefaction and with condensation polarity. Alternating polarity must not be used alone, because it cancels the CM that is being looked for.
    2. Overlay the two polarities. A CM appears as a ringing waveform starting soon after stimulus arrival that inverts with polarity, often lasting several milliseconds. Neural waves, if present, do not invert.
    3. Run a control with the insert tube clamped or disconnected from the ear, so that no sound reaches the ear while the transducer is still driven. Any remaining inverting waveform is stimulus artefact, not CM. Inserts help because the tube delays the acoustic stimulus relative to the electrical artefact.
    4. Interpret with OAEs, acoustic reflexes (usually absent) and later cortical responses.

    A CM with no ABR is the ABR signature of ANSD. The ABR threshold cannot be used to estimate hearing levels in ANSD, which changes infant management considerably.

    Neurological applications and intraoperative monitoring

    The ABR assesses the integrity of the auditory brainstem pathway in neurology and critical care, for example in coma, in suspected brainstem lesions and demyelinating disease, and as one element of neurophysiological assessment where local policy includes it. In surgery near the auditory nerve (for example cerebellopontine angle tumour removal with hearing preservation, or microvascular decompression), ABR is recorded continuously under anaesthesia. The surgeon is warned when wave V latency or amplitude changes beyond local alarm criteria (changes of the order of 1 ms latency or 50% amplitude are commonly quoted, but criteria vary). Because averaging takes time, faster methods are often added: higher rates, fewer sweeps with weighted averaging, and near-field recordings such as direct nerve action potentials or ECochG, which are larger and need far fewer sweeps. Anaesthesia has little effect on the ABR, but temperature changes affect latency and must be noted.

    Bone-conduction ABR

    BC-ABR shows whether a raised air-conduction threshold has a conductive component, which is especially important in infants with middle-ear effusion or atresia. The same recording chain is used with a bone vibrator. Practical points: the stimulus artefact is large because the vibrator is close to the electrodes, so alternating polarity and careful lead placement are needed; the maximum level is lower than for air conduction; the vibrator placement and force affect level; and masking of the non-test ear is needed in older children and adults.

    Sedation and natural sleep

    Muscle activity is the main enemy of ABR recording, so the patient must be still and relaxed. The ABR itself is largely unaffected by sleep, sedation or general anaesthesia, unlike cortical responses. In young infants (commonly up to about 3 to 4 months), natural sleep after a feed is usually enough: the appointment is timed around the feed, the infant is swaddled and the test begins once asleep. Older infants and toddlers who do not sleep naturally may need sedation or general anaesthesia, which is a medical procedure: it requires a medical order, fasting instructions, trained staff, monitoring (for example pulse oximetry) and a recovery area, according to institutional policy. Test efficiency matters: plan the order of tests (for example the most important frequencies and ears first) because the sleep may end early. Adults are tested lying down with the neck supported; a darkened, quiet room helps, and some adults fall asleep, which improves the recording.

    Advantages and limitations

    Advantages

    • Objective: no behavioural response needed.
    • Largely unaffected by sleep, sedation and anaesthesia.
    • Frequency-specific thresholds with tone bursts or narrow-band chirps.
    • Separates conductive, cochlear and neural components with AC and BC and latency analysis.
    • Essential for ANSD diagnosis and useful in intraoperative monitoring.

    Limitations

    • Not a test of hearing: it shows neural synchrony, not perception.
    • Needs a still patient; testing time is long in active children.
    • Click ABR does not show low-frequency hearing or audiogram shape.
    • Limited maximum output for severe to profound losses and for BC.
    • Absent in ANSD and in some neurological conditions even when hearing is present.
    • Threshold estimates carry uncertainty and depend on the correction used.

    Troubleshooting

    ProblemLikely causeWhat to check or do
    Large 50 Hz sinusoid in the averageHigh or unbalanced impedance, poor ground, rate locked to mains, nearby equipmentRe-prepare skin, check ground, change rate (for example to 21.1 or 27.7 /s), move or switch off equipment, route leads away from mains cables
    Most sweeps rejectedPatient awake or tense, rejection limit too tight, electrode movementSettle or reposition patient, support the neck, check electrodes; adjust limit only within protocol
    Large deflection at time zeroStimulus artefact (supra-aural or BC transducer, leads near transducer)Use inserts, separate transducer and electrode leads, alternating polarity
    All latencies about 0.9 ms too longInsert tube delay not correctedCheck software delay setting and tube length
    No response at any level, normal OAEsBlocked tube, disconnected transducer, or ANSDListening check; tube check; separate polarity recordings for CM
    Replications disagreeToo few sweeps, high noise, patient state changingCollect more sweeps, reduce noise, check residual noise estimate
    Wave V missing at low levels in an infantWindow too short, high-pass too highUse a 20 to 25 ms window and 30 Hz high-pass
    Slow wave or ringing across the windowNotch filter ringing, electrode drift, high-pass too low with poor electrodesTurn off notch, fix impedance, confirm filter settings
    Response in a severely impaired ear with long latency and no wave ICrossover from the non-test earCheck contralateral channel; apply contralateral masking
    Latencies differ from last visit with no clinical changeDifferent filters, transducer, calibration or peak-marking conventionCompare protocols; use consistent settings and norms

    Demonstration

    Equipment: a clinical ABR system with insert earphones (and a bone vibrator if available), disposable electrodes, skin preparation gel, impedance meter (usually built in), a volunteer on a couch, and the two browser demos in this topic.

    1. Using Figure 6.2b, identify the stimulus unit, transducers, head box and computer on the real system, and trace the trigger link between them.
    2. Open the protocol settings and read out the stimulus, level, rate, polarity, filters, window, sweeps and rejection limit. Students record each value.
    3. Prepare the skin and place electrodes in the standard montage (Figure 6.2d); show the impedance display and the effect of a poorly prepared site (6.3).
    4. Show the raw EEG. Ask the volunteer to clench the jaw and then relax: students watch the rejection counter rise and fall.
    5. Record a click ABR at about 80 dB nHL with alternating polarity and two replications. Mark waves I, III and V and measure the interpeak intervals.
    6. Repeat with rarefaction and condensation separately, and overlay them; with a supra-aural earphone, show the stimulus artefact at time zero and its cancellation with alternating polarity.
    7. Temporarily set the insert tube delay correction off (if the software allows) and show the 0.8 to 1 ms latency shift; restore it.
    8. Record at 60, 40 and 20 dB nHL and plot the latency-intensity function; compare with the u6-2-lif demo.
    9. Use the u6-2-abr demo to show the effect of filters, rate and sweep count, and the relationship between residual noise, correlation and Fsp.

    Students should observe that the response grows out of the noise as sweeps accumulate, that wave V is the last wave to disappear as level falls, and that settings change latencies enough to matter clinically.

    Current developments

    • Level-specific and narrow-band chirp stimuli that increase response amplitude and shorten test time for threshold estimation.
    • Statistical and adaptive stopping rules that end averaging when a response is detected or the residual noise target is met, reducing test time.
    • Amplifiers at or near the electrode, wireless head boxes and advanced weighting methods aimed at recording in less quiet patients and reducing the need for sedation.
    • Parallel or multi-frequency, multi-ear ABR methods and continuous-stimulation approaches that collect frequency-specific information faster.
    • Research into machine-learning-assisted peak marking and response detection; these remain research or decision-support tools until validated for clinical use.
    • Integration of ABR with ASSR (6.4), OAE and cortical testing on one platform and in tele-audiology workflows.

    Practical activity and assessment

    Task (Practicals P8 and P9): record and interpret a click and tone-burst ABR, and document the instrumentation settings.

    1. In pairs, prepare a volunteer and record click ABRs in each ear at 80, 60, 40 and 20 dB nHL with two replications per level.
    2. Record a 500 Hz and a 2000 Hz tone-burst ABR at one moderate level, using a window and high-pass setting suited to each frequency.
    3. Tabulate all settings: transducer and tube delay correction, stimulus, gating, level, polarity, rate, filters, window, sampling rate (if shown), rejection limit, sweeps accepted and rejected, and residual noise or Fsp.
    4. Mark waves I, III and V, measure absolute latencies, interpeak intervals, wave V amplitude and V/I ratio, and compare with the clinic norms.
    5. Plot the latency-intensity function for each ear and comment on its slope and position.
    6. Using the averaging equation and your residual noise value, estimate the single-sweep noise and state how many sweeps would halve the residual noise.

    Deliverable: a two-page laboratory report with the annotated waveforms, the settings table, the LIF plot and a short interpretation, including one paragraph on data quality.
