    6.3

    Electrodes, montages, impedance, artefacts and SNR for ABR and ASSR

    Supplementary3 hours (lecture plus hands-on)Practical P9Practical P8

    The auditory brainstem response (ABR) and the auditory steady-state response (ASSR) are fractions of a microvolt, recorded through three small electrodes on the skin. Almost every poor recording in the clinic is caused at this interface: where the electrodes sit, how well the skin was prepared, how the leads are routed and how relaxed the patient is. This topic explains what each electrode does electrically, why the standard montages are placed where they are, how impedance, interference and patient noise enter the recording, and how to find and fix problems quickly so that test time is spent on useful averaging.

    What it is

    The recording interface of an evoked-potential system: the active (non-inverting), reference (inverting) and ground (common) electrodes, the gel and skin under them, the leads, and the differential input stage of the amplifier. A montage is the named set of electrode positions and the way they are connected to the amplifier inputs.

    Why it is required

    The signal of interest (about 0.1 to 0.7 µV for the ABR, often less than 0.1 µV for the ASSR near threshold) is thousands of times smaller than mains interference on the body and tens of times smaller than ongoing EEG and muscle activity. The electrodes and montage decide how much signal is picked up and how much interference is rejected, and therefore how many sweeps, and how many minutes, a test needs.

    How it works

    Two recording electrodes sample the scalp potential field produced by the auditory nerve and brainstem. A differential amplifier amplifies the difference between them and rejects what is common to both. The ground electrode ties the patient to the amplifier's isolated reference so that the common-mode voltage stays within range. Low, balanced impedances, short twisted leads and a relaxed patient keep the noise low; averaging and statistical tests then recover the response.

    Key ideaThe instrumentation of ABR systems (stimulus generator, filters, averager) is covered in 6.2 and the ASSR analyser in 6.4. The basics of differential amplification and averaging were introduced in 4.4. This topic goes deeper into the part of the chain that the clinician controls with their own hands: electrodes, placement and the recording environment.

    1. Basic working principle: what each electrode does

    An evoked potential is a voltage between two points on the body. The brainstem generators behave like small current sources (dipoles) inside a conducting medium, the head. Their currents spread through brain, cerebrospinal fluid, skull and scalp; this spread is called volume conduction. At every point on the scalp there is therefore a tiny potential that rises and falls in time with the neural activity. One electrode on its own cannot measure anything, because voltage is always a difference between two points. An evoked-potential recording uses three electrodes, each with a distinct electrical job.

    The differential amplifier in one paragraph

    A differential amplifier has two signal inputs, called non-inverting (+) and inverting (−), and a reference terminal. Ideally its output is a gain G times the difference between the two input voltages. Anything that appears identically on both inputs, measured relative to the reference terminal, is called a common-mode signal and ideally produces no output. Anything that differs between the inputs is a differential signal and is amplified. Mains interference that couples into the whole body is almost identical at the vertex and at the mastoid, so it is mostly common-mode. The ABR produces different potentials at the vertex and the mastoid, so it is differential.

    V+ = Vcm + Vd/2 V− = Vcm − Vd/2 Vout = Gd (V+ − V−) + Gcm Vcm = Gd Vd + Gcm Vcm CMRR = Gd / Gcm ; CMRR(dB) = 20 log10(Gd / Gcm) where V+ and V− are the voltages at the non-inverting and inverting inputs relative to the patient common, Vd is the differential signal (the wanted ABR plus differential noise), Vcm is the common-mode voltage (mostly mains), Gd is the differential gain, Gcm the (small, unwanted) common-mode gain and CMRR the common-mode rejection ratio.

    Active (non-inverting) electrode

    The active electrode is connected to the non-inverting (+) input. By convention in ABR work it sits where the wanted response is large and positive-going for the main waves: the vertex (Cz) or the high forehead (Fz or just below the hairline). The name "active" is historical. The vertex is not the only place where the response exists; the mastoid also carries a sizeable potential (see below), so both recording electrodes are "active" in the physical sense. The terms non-inverting and inverting are more precise and are preferred in modern texts.

    Reference (inverting) electrode

    The reference electrode is connected to the inverting (−) input. In the standard ABR montage it sits on the mastoid (M1 left, M2 right) or earlobe (A1 left, A2 right) on the side of the stimulated ear (ipsilateral). Whatever voltage appears at this electrode is subtracted. Because the earlobe and mastoid are close to the auditory nerve, they record the nerve's compound action potential as a negative deflection at the time of wave I. Subtracting a negative voltage gives a positive deflection, so wave I appears as an upward peak largely because of what the reference electrode sees.

    Ground (common, isolated common) electrode

    The ground electrode is connected to the amplifier's reference terminal, which is the patient-side common of the isolated input stage. It does not carry the signal. Its job is to hold the patient's body near the amplifier's own zero, so that the common-mode voltage on the two recording inputs stays small and within the amplifier's input range, and to provide a return path for the tiny bias and interference currents. In most evoked-potential equipment this terminal is electrically isolated from mains earth for safety, which is why it is often called the isolated common or patient common rather than earth. Typical ground sites are the low forehead (Fpz), the contralateral mastoid or earlobe, or the cheek, shoulder or collarbone in some protocols.

    Figure 6.3a. The three electrodes and the differential amplifier. The active electrode feeds the non-inverting input, the reference electrode the inverting input, and the ground electrode ties the patient to the isolated common of the input stage. Mains interference reaches the body through stray capacitance and appears almost equally on both recording electrodes (common-mode); the ABR differs between vertex and mastoid (differential).

    Sign conventions and why the waves point up

    The amplifier simply computes V+ minus V−. Whether a given wave points up or down on the screen depends on three choices: which electrode goes to which input, which polarity the software plots as upward, and where the generator's field is positive or negative. In the conventional North American and British ABR display, the vertex (or high forehead) goes to the non-inverting input and positive voltage (vertex relative to mastoid) is plotted upward. Waves I, III and V then appear as upward peaks, and the troughs that follow them, such as the slow negativity after wave V (often called SN10), point down. Some older European and research displays plotted vertex-negative upward; the waveform is then a mirror image. The physiology is unchanged; only the convention differs.

    If the active and reference leads are swapped at the head box, V+ and V− exchange places and the output becomes minus what it was. Every peak becomes a trough. The latencies stay the same, but a clinician who looks for upward peaks will mark the troughs and report wrong latencies (typically the troughs sit about 0.5 to 1 ms after each peak). A fully inverted waveform with a normal-looking shape is therefore almost always a lead-order or display-polarity error, not a physiological finding.

    Common errorDo not confuse lead-swap inversion with stimulus polarity. Changing the click from rarefaction to condensation reverses the cochlear microphonic and the stimulus artefact but leaves the neural waves I to V pointing the same way (with small latency changes). Swapping the electrode leads inverts everything, neural waves included.

    What the ground does and does not do

    Moving the ground electrode does not change which voltage difference is measured, so in an ideal system it would not change the waveform at all. In a real system it matters for three reasons. First, the ground's own impedance sets how much common-mode voltage develops on the body when interference current flows back through it. Second, if the ground is placed between or on top of the recording pair it can shunt part of the field and distort the difference. Third, a ground placed on a large muscle or near the heart adds large common-mode signals (EMG, ECG) that the amplifier must then reject perfectly. Placing the ground away from the recording pair, on a quiet bony site such as the low forehead or the contralateral mastoid, keeps the common-mode voltage small and stable.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow of the recording interface

    From the neural generator to the analogue-to-digital converter (ADC), the ABR passes through a chain in which each link can add noise or lose signal. The clinician controls the first five links directly.

    1. Generators and volume conductor. The auditory nerve and brainstem nuclei produce current dipoles; the head spreads the resulting field to the scalp, attenuating it strongly with distance.
    2. Skin. The outer dead layer (stratum corneum) has a high resistance and behaves partly like a capacitor. Skin preparation removes or penetrates it.
    3. Electrolyte (gel or paste). Makes ionic contact between skin and metal, fills hair and uneven surfaces, and keeps the contact stable when the patient moves slightly.
    4. Electrode. Converts ionic current in the gel into electronic current in the wire through an electrochemical reaction at the metal surface.
    5. Leads. Carry microvolt signals to the head box (preamplifier). They can act as antennas for electric and magnetic fields and generate artefact when they move.
    6. Isolated differential preamplifier. High input impedance, high common-mode rejection, gain, and an isolation barrier between the patient and mains-powered equipment. Some systems measure electrode impedance through the same inputs.
    7. Filters, ADC, artefact rejection and averager. Covered in 6.2 and 4.4. What reaches them is already fixed by the first six links: filters and averaging can remove noise, but they cannot recover signal that was never picked up.
    ComponentFunctionWhat can go wrong
    Electrode (Ag/AgCl disc, gold cup, disposable hydrogel)Transduces ionic to electronic current with a stable, low-noise interfacePolarisation and drift (poor metal or worn chloride layer), dried gel, detached edge, corrosion on reusable cups
    Electrolyte gel, paste or hydrogelWets the skin, lowers interface impedance, holds the electrodeToo little (high impedance), too much (bridges to a neighbouring electrode), dried out after long sessions
    Skin preparation (abrasive gel, alcohol wipe)Removes oil and dead skin to reduce impedance and skin potential variationInadequate preparation, over-abrasion and skin injury in neonates, residual alcohol under the gel
    Electrode leads and snap or clip connectorsCarry the signal to the head boxBroken conductor, intermittent clip, loops that pick up magnetic fields, lead movement artefact
    Head box (electrode input box, preamplifier)Labelled input sockets, first gain stage, impedance checkLeads in wrong sockets (swapped channels or inverted waveform), head box far from patient, loose multi-pin connector
    Isolated input stagePatient safety and common-mode rangeIsolation fault (a service issue), saturation with large offsets
    Insert earphones and their tubingDeliver the stimulus away from the electrodesTransducer body placed near leads (stimulus artefact), kinked tube (no response)

    3. Types of signals involved: wanted response, noise and interference

    An electrode pair picks up everything that makes a voltage difference between its two sites. It helps to classify these signals by their source, their size and whether they are common-mode or differential, because the remedy depends on the class.

    SignalTypical size at the electrodesFrequency contentModeMain defence
    Click ABR, high level (waves I, III, V)about 0.1 to 0.7 µVenergy mainly about 100 to 1500 Hz; slower components in infantsdifferentialoptimal montage to maximise it
    ABR near threshold (wave V)about 0.05 to 0.2 µVmore low-frequency energydifferentiallow noise and many sweeps
    ASSR at 80 to 100 Hz modulation rate, near thresholdtens of nanovolts, typically under 0.1 µVa single spectral line at the modulation ratedifferentiallow noise, longer recordings
    Background EEG in the ABR pass-bandabout 1 to 5 µV RMS in sleep; more awakebroadband, falls with frequencydifferentialaveraging; natural sleep
    EMG from jaw, neck and scalp musclesa few to tens of µVbroadband, strong above 100 Hzdifferentialrelaxation, electrode site, artefact rejection
    ECG (cardiac)tens of µV at the head, more at neck and chestmostly below 40 Hz, but sharp QRS edges pass the high-pass filtermainly common-mode, partly differentialmontage on the head, high-pass filter, rejection
    Mains interference on the bodyabout 0.1 mV to tens of mV, occasionally more (environment dependent)50 Hz and harmonics (100, 150 Hz and higher)common-mode (converted to differential by imbalance)balanced low impedance, ground, CMRR, distance
    Magnetic pickup in lead loopsnanovolts to microvolts50 Hz and harmonics, switching frequenciesdifferentialtwist and shorten leads
    Stimulus artefactcan reach tens to hundreds of µV for the first 1 msfollows the stimulus waveformdifferentialinsert earphones, lead routing, alternating polarity
    Electrode offset and drift (half-cell, skin potential)millivolts DC, slowly varyingbelow about 1 HzdifferentialAg/AgCl, stable gel, high-pass filter

    These sizes are typical illustrations; the actual values depend on the patient, the room and the system. The key point is the ratio: the wanted ABR is typically 10 to 50 times smaller than the single-sweep noise, and many thousands of times smaller than the mains voltage on the body.

    4. Electrode positions and montages (10-20 system)

    Electrode sites are named with the International 10-20 system, originally described by Jasper for EEG. Distances between the nasion (bridge of the nose) and the inion (bump at the back of the skull), and between the two preauricular points, are divided into steps of 10 % and 20 %. Letters give the region: Fp frontopolar, F frontal, C central, P parietal, O occipital, T temporal; z means the midline. Odd numbers are on the left, even numbers on the right. Cz is the vertex, halfway between nasion and inion on the midline. Fz is 20 % of the nasion-inion distance in front of Cz, and Fpz is at 10 % above the nasion, the low forehead. A1 and A2 are the left and right earlobes, M1 and M2 the left and right mastoids (the bony prominence behind the ear). In evoked-potential work the 10-20 names are used loosely; "high forehead" usually means the midline just below the hairline, between Fpz and Fz.

    Figure 6.3b. The vertical (vertex to ipsilateral mastoid) ABR montage for a left-ear stimulus, in top and side views. Active electrode at the vertex Cz (or high forehead Fz), reference on the left mastoid M1 (or left earlobe A1), ground on the low forehead Fpz (or the contralateral mastoid M2). For a right-ear stimulus the reference moves to M2 or A2.

    The vertical (single-channel) ABR montage

    The montage used in most clinics records between the vertex or high forehead and the ipsilateral mastoid or earlobe. It is called vertical because the line joining the two electrodes runs roughly from the top of the head down to the level of the ear, parallel to the dominant orientation of the wave V generator. A typical configuration (follow local protocol and the equipment manual) is:

    • Non-inverting (+): Cz, or high forehead (Fz region). The high forehead is used when hair makes the vertex hard to prepare, and it is routine in infants. The forehead is easier to prepare and gives a slightly smaller wave V than the vertex.
    • Inverting (−): ipsilateral mastoid (M1 for left ear, M2 for right ear) or ipsilateral earlobe (A1 or A2). The earlobe is further from the neck muscles, so it tends to pick up less EMG; the mastoid is bony and holds an electrode well. Both give a clear wave I.
    • Ground (common): low forehead (Fpz), or the contralateral mastoid or earlobe. The BSA recommended procedure for post-newborn and adult ABR, for example, describes vertex (or high forehead in infants) positive, ipsilateral mastoid negative and contralateral mastoid as common.

    When both ears are tested with one channel, the reference is moved between M1 and M2 (or a switch in the head box selects it). Many systems provide separate left and right reference inputs so that the software can pick the ipsilateral side automatically when the test ear changes.

    Two-channel ipsilateral and contralateral recording

    With a two-channel system the non-inverting input of both channels is connected to the same vertex or forehead electrode (often by a jumper in the head box), channel A uses the ipsilateral mastoid as reference and channel B the contralateral mastoid. Only one ear is stimulated at a time; the other ear is masked if required.

    Figure 6.3c. Two-channel ipsilateral and contralateral montage for a left-ear stimulus. Both channels share the vertex (non-inverting) electrode and the ground; channel A uses the ipsilateral mastoid and channel B the contralateral mastoid as reference.

    Why the channels differ:

    • Wave I is generated by the distal auditory nerve of the stimulated ear. Its field is concentrated near that ear, so the ipsilateral mastoid sees a large negative potential and the contralateral mastoid sees very little. Wave I is therefore larger in the ipsilateral channel and small or absent in the contralateral channel.
    • Wave V arises from the lateral lemniscus terminating in the inferior colliculus region, with a field oriented mostly vertically. It is recorded well in both channels. In the contralateral channel wave V is often slightly later and more clearly separated from wave IV, because wave IV is relatively smaller there.
    • Comparing the two channels is a practical identification tool. A peak that is present ipsilaterally and absent contralaterally at about 1.5 to 2 ms is very likely wave I. A peak that is present in both channels at similar latency is likely wave V. When wave IV and V are fused in the ipsilateral channel (a common "IV-V complex"), the contralateral channel often shows the separate wave V peak.
    • A large peak at 1 ms or less that appears in both channels with a sharp onset and reverses with stimulus polarity is more likely stimulus artefact or cochlear microphonic than wave I.

    Horizontal montage

    The horizontal montage records between the two mastoids (M1 to M2) or the two earlobes (A1 to A2). It is sensitive to horizontally oriented fields. Wave I, which is essentially a large negativity at one ear and not the other, is recorded strongly. Wave V, whose field is largely vertical and symmetric, is small because both mastoids see similar potentials and the difference cancels. The horizontal channel is used mainly to enhance wave I, for example when wave I is hard to see in a hearing loss or to confirm wave I in neurological (retrocochlear) testing. Wave I in this channel has the polarity set by which mastoid goes to which input.

    Infant montage

    Newborns and young infants need a modified placement.

    • Active electrode on the high forehead, on the midline just below the hairline, not at the vertex. The anterior fontanelle (the soft membranous gap between the frontal and parietal bones) lies near the vertex region in young infants. Pressing and abrading there is avoided, and the fontanelle area also gives less reliable contact. Some protocols describe this site as Fz; in practice it is the highest part of the forehead that is still on bone.
    • Reference on the ipsilateral mastoid (or earlobe). Some protocols, particularly for single-channel screening and some diagnostic systems, use the nape of the neck (a non-cephalic or midline posterior reference) instead of the mastoid. The nape reference picks up wave V well and allows both ears to be tested without moving electrodes, but wave I is smaller than with a mastoid reference and neck muscle activity is picked up in an awake baby. A study in newborns (Yiğit and colleagues, 2020) compared a mastoid-vertex montage with a nape-vertex-cheek montage and found the nape montage practical and comfortable in single-channel systems, with similar wave latencies.
    • Ground on the contralateral mastoid, the cheek or the shoulder, depending on protocol.
    • Electrodes are usually small disposable pre-gelled neonatal electrodes with a gentle adhesive. Skin preparation is light: a mild abrasive gel with minimal pressure, because neonatal skin is thin and easily damaged, particularly in preterm babies.

    Figure 6.3d. Infant montage (side view, face to the left). The active electrode sits on the high forehead, in front of the anterior fontanelle; the reference is on the ipsilateral mastoid, with the nape of the neck as an alternative in some protocols; the ground goes on the cheek, shoulder or contralateral mastoid.

    ASSR montages

    The ASSR (6.4) is recorded with the same kind of electrodes and amplifier. Two differences matter for the montage. First, when both ears are stimulated simultaneously with multiple carrier frequencies, a single channel should record responses from both ears equally well, so a midline reference such as the nape of the neck (or the inion region) is often used with the vertex or high forehead as the non-inverting site; a mastoid reference would favour one ear. Second, the ASSR is detected as a narrow spectral line, so low noise at the modulation frequency is what matters rather than a clear wave I. Typical ASSR montages are therefore:

    • Single channel: vertex or high forehead (+) to nape of neck or mastoid (−), ground on the forehead, cheek or collarbone, depending on the manufacturer and protocol.
    • Two channels: vertex to left mastoid and vertex to right mastoid, or vertex to nape plus a second vertex-to-mastoid channel. Research systems sometimes combine several channels to improve the detection statistic, because noise that is partly independent across channels can be averaged.

    At 80 to 100 Hz modulation rates the ASSR is generated mainly in the brainstem, and neck muscle activity (EMG) near the nape is a relevant noise source in awake patients; natural sleep is again preferred. The 40 Hz ASSR in awake adults has larger cortical contributions and a more frontal-central field.

    ECochG recording sites (for contrast)

    Electrocochleography (ECochG, covered in 6.5) records the cochlear microphonic, summating potential and compound action potential (equivalent to wave I). Its electrode sites illustrate the proximity rule clearly:

    SiteElectrode typeDistance to cochlea and nerveTypical AP (wave I) sizeNotes
    Transtympanic (promontory)Fine needle through the eardrum onto the promontoryclosestseveral to tens of µVlargest signal, invasive, usually by or with an otologist
    Tympanic membraneSoft-tipped wick or foam electrode on the eardrumclosetypically a few µV or lessnon-invasive but uncomfortable; careful placement needed
    Ear canal (TIPtrode type)Foam insert tip wrapped in gold foil, also delivers soundfurthertypically about 0.2 to 1 µVcomfortable; enhances wave I in ABR
    Mastoid or earlobe (surface)Standard ABR electrodefurthesttypically about 0.1 to 0.5 µVstandard ABR reference

    Each step closer to the generator increases the recorded wave I, because the potential of a dipole source falls roughly with the square of the distance at moderate distances, and the intervening tissues (bone in particular) attenuate the field. This is why an ear-canal electrode can be substituted for the mastoid as the inverting electrode to enhance wave I in an ABR, for example in hearing loss where wave I is small.

    5. Why each electrode goes where it does

    Dipoles and volume conduction

    A population of neurons firing together produces a flow of current into the cells at one end and out at the other. Seen from a distance, this is a current dipole: a source and a sink separated by a short distance, with an orientation. In a conducting medium the dipole creates a potential field that is positive on the side the dipole points towards, negative on the opposite side, and zero along a plane through its middle at right angles to it. The size of the potential at a point falls with distance, roughly with the square of distance for a simple dipole far from the observer, and depends on the angle between the dipole axis and the direction to the point.

    Two consequences follow for electrode placement:

    • An electrode pair records the difference between two points of this field. The largest difference is obtained when one electrode is near the positive pole and the other near the negative pole, that is, when the line between the electrodes is parallel to the dipole axis.
    • If both electrodes lie on the same equipotential line (for example symmetrically on either side of a vertical dipole), the difference is near zero, however large the local potentials are. Placing two electrodes close together also reduces the difference, because they sample nearly the same potential. This is differential cancellation.

    The far-field brainstem potentials are recorded at a distance from generators deep in the head. The field at the scalp is broad and smooth, so the exact position of the electrodes changes amplitude gradually rather than abruptly. This is why modest placement variations (Cz versus Fz, mastoid versus earlobe) are tolerated, while gross errors (both electrodes on the forehead, reference on the wrong ear) are not.

    The generators and their orientation

    • Wave I: the distal part of the auditory nerve near the cochlea. The ear canal, earlobe and mastoid on that side are close to it and see a negative potential at wave I latency. The vertex sees little. In the vertex-to-mastoid channel, wave I is mostly the inverted mastoid negativity.
    • Wave II: the proximal auditory nerve as it enters the brainstem.
    • Wave III: the cochlear nucleus and nearby lower pons structures, with contributions from both sides.
    • Wave V (and IV): the lateral lemniscus as it approaches the inferior colliculus, largely on the side opposite the stimulated ear. The net field of wave V is oriented mostly vertically (from the brainstem towards the vertex), with the vertex positive and the neck and lower head relatively negative. This is why a vertical montage (vertex to mastoid, earlobe or nape) records wave V well and a horizontal montage records it poorly.

    The descriptions of generators are simplifications; each wave has contributions from more than one structure. The point for the clinician is the orientation and location, which fix the best electrode axis.

    Why the mastoid or earlobe reference

    The mastoid and earlobe reference electrodes are not neutral. They are close to the auditory nerve and pick up wave I as a negative deflection, which the inverting input turns into a positive peak. This is useful: it makes wave I larger in the standard montage than it would be with a distant reference. It also means the reference is always "active" for wave I, so moving the reference to the opposite ear or the nape reduces or removes wave I. For wave V, the mastoid is moderately negative, adding a little to the vertex positivity.

    Why the ground away from the recording pair

    The ground electrode should sit on a quiet, bony, easily prepared site that is electrically well connected to the head but is neither on the line between the recording electrodes nor on a large muscle. The low forehead and the contralateral mastoid satisfy these conditions. The ground carries the displacement current that mains fields couple into the body; the voltage across the ground electrode impedance becomes the common-mode voltage seen by both recording inputs. A low-impedance ground therefore reduces the common-mode voltage, and placing it away from the recording pair avoids shunting or distorting the local field. A ground on the chest or collarbone is used in some protocols, but it brings the recording system nearer to the heart and the large neck and shoulder muscles.

    6. The electrode-skin interface

    Electrode materials

    TypeConstructionAdvantagesLimitations
    Silver/silver chloride (Ag/AgCl) disc or cupSilver coated with a layer of silver chloride; used with chloride gel or pasteNon-polarisable: low and stable half-cell potential, low drift and low noise at low frequenciesChloride layer wears and must be re-chlorided or replaced on reusable electrodes
    Gold-plated cupCup filled with conductive paste, attached with paste or tapeDurable, easy to clean, suitable for repeated use and for hair-covered sitesMore polarisable: larger and less stable offset, more slow drift and more movement artefact; relies on good paste
    Disposable pre-gelled foam or hydrogelAg/AgCl sensor with adhesive foam or solid hydrogel, single useConsistent, hygienic, quick, suited to neonates and screeningNeeds flat, hair-free skin; gel can dry if opened early; adhesion falls on oily or sweaty skin
    Ear canal (gold-foil foam insert, TIPtrode type)Foam ear tip wrapped in gold foil, also carries soundClose to the cochlea: enhances wave IImpedance often higher; canal preparation needed
    Subdermal needleFine sterile needle under the skinVery quick, low impedance under anaesthesiaInvasive; used mainly in intraoperative monitoring, not routine clinics

    Half-cell potential and polarisation

    Whenever a metal touches an electrolyte, a chemical reaction at the surface sets up a small DC voltage called the half-cell potential. Its size depends on the metal and the electrolyte and ranges from millivolts to hundreds of millivolts. If the two recording electrodes had identical half-cell potentials, they would cancel in the differential amplifier. In practice they differ by some millivolts (the electrode offset), and this difference changes slowly with temperature, pressure and movement. The high-pass filter removes the steady part, but sudden changes (a lead tug, a head turn) cause large, slow transients that can saturate the amplifier or trigger artefact rejection.

    A polarisable electrode (an idealised noble metal) passes current mainly by charging its surface like a capacitor; its offset changes when current flows or when the electrode moves. A non-polarisable electrode (Ag/AgCl is close to this ideal) passes current by a reversible chemical reaction, so its potential stays stable. This is why Ag/AgCl is the preferred material for low-noise biopotential recording.

    The skin

    The skin has an outer layer of dead, dry cells, the stratum corneum, typically about 10 to 20 µm thick on most body sites. It is a poor conductor and forms the largest part of the electrode impedance on unprepared skin, often tens to hundreds of kilohms. Beneath it the living epidermis and dermis conduct well. The skin also produces its own potential between its layers (the skin potential), which changes with sweating and with pressure on the electrode. Skin preparation lowers impedance and stabilises this potential by:

    1. removing oil and make-up with an alcohol wipe (allowed to dry), or with soap and water;
    2. gently abrading the stratum corneum with an abrasive paste or gel on a cotton bud or gauze, a few firm strokes on adults and light strokes on infants;
    3. wiping away the loose debris and excess abrasive;
    4. applying a conductive gel or paste, or a pre-gelled electrode, so that the electrolyte fills the prepared area.
    SafetyAbrasion breaks the skin barrier. Use single-use applicators, follow local infection-control policy, and take particular care with neonatal and elderly skin. Do not abrade broken or inflamed skin. Aim for adequate impedance, not the lowest achievable number.

    An equivalent circuit

    The electrochemistry can be approximated by a simple circuit. At the metal-electrolyte boundary there is a half-cell potential in series with a charge-transfer resistance in parallel with a double-layer capacitance. The gel adds a series resistance. The skin adds its own potential and another resistor-capacitor pair representing the stratum corneum, and the deeper tissue adds a small series resistance.

    Figure 6.3e. Equivalent circuit of the electrode-skin interface. Ehc: electrode half-cell potential; Rd and Cd: charge-transfer resistance and double-layer capacitance; Rs: gel resistance; Ese: skin potential; Re and Ce: resistance and capacitance of the stratum corneum and epidermis; Ru: deeper tissue. Skin preparation mainly reduces Re, which is why it lowers impedance so much.

    The simplified model is a DC source in series with a resistor Rp in parallel with a capacitor Cp, plus a series resistor Rs. Its impedance magnitude depends on frequency:

    Z(f) = Rs + Rp / (1 + j 2π f Rp Cp) |Z| → Rs + Rp at very low frequency |Z| → Rs at high frequency where Rs is the series (gel and tissue) resistance, Rp the parallel (interface and skin) resistance, Cp the parallel capacitance, f the frequency and j the imaginary unit.

    Worked example (illustrative values for a prepared site): Rs = 1 kΩ, Rp = 20 kΩ, Cp = 2 µF. The product 2π f Rp Cp is 2.51 at 10 Hz. The table gives the computed magnitude and phase:

    Frequency|Z|PhaseComment
    DC21.0 kΩ0°resistive; not measurable with DC in practice because of polarisation
    10 Hz7.8 kΩ−61°typical impedance-meter frequency region
    30 Hz2.9 kΩ−63°also used by some meters
    100 Hz1.3 kΩ−38°lower edge of the neurological ABR band
    1 kHz1.0 kΩ−5°almost purely the series resistance

    The figure a clinician reads on the impedance meter therefore depends on the test frequency. It is a practical index of contact quality, not the exact impedance in the ABR pass-band. Most systems use the same test frequency every time, so readings are comparable from patient to patient on one system, but not necessarily between systems.

    Measuring impedance

    Evoked-potential systems measure electrode impedance by passing a very small alternating current (typically in the nanoampere to low microampere range, at a frequency often around 10 to 30 Hz) through each electrode and measuring the resulting voltage. Alternating current is used because DC would polarise the electrodes and give misleading readings. Depending on the system, the displayed value is the impedance of each electrode to the others (or to the ground) or of each electrode pair. A DC ohmmeter must never be used on patient electrodes; it polarises the electrodes and is not designed for applied-part safety.

    RememberCommon targets are an impedance below about 5 kΩ for each electrode and a difference between electrodes (inter-electrode imbalance) within about 2 kΩ. The BSA procedure for post-newborn and adult ABR, for example, asks for inter-electrode impedances below 5 kΩ and preferably below 2 kΩ, and balanced. Some neonatal hydrogel electrodes and some systems (for example with very high input impedance or amplifiers at the electrode) tolerate higher values. Follow the local protocol and the equipment manual.

    7. Why impedance matters: thermal noise and impedance imbalance

    Thermal (Johnson) noise

    Any resistance at a temperature above absolute zero generates a small random voltage from the thermal motion of charge carriers. Its RMS value over a bandwidth B is:

    vn = √(4 k T R B) where vn is the RMS noise voltage, k = 1.38 × 10^-23 J/K is Boltzmann's constant, T the absolute temperature in kelvin (about 310 K at body temperature), R the resistance in ohms and B the bandwidth in hertz.

    Worked example, bandwidth 3 kHz, T = 310 K:

    • R = 5 kΩ: 4 × 1.38 × 10^-23 × 310 × 5000 × 3000 = 2.57 × 10^-13 V², so vn = 0.51 µV RMS.
    • R = 10 kΩ: vn = 0.51 × √2 = 0.72 µV RMS.
    • R = 1 kΩ: vn = 0.23 µV RMS.

    These values are per sweep. Thermal noise is random, so it averages down like EEG: after 2000 sweeps the 5 kΩ contribution is 0.51 / √2000 = 0.011 µV. Compared with background EEG and EMG of a few microvolts per sweep, thermal noise of the electrodes is a minor contributor at the usual impedance targets. Real electrode interfaces add some excess noise beyond the Johnson value, particularly at low frequencies and with poorly prepared skin or polarisable electrodes, but the main reason for low impedance lies elsewhere: the conversion of common-mode interference into a differential error.

    Impedance imbalance: how common-mode becomes differential

    Mains interference raises the whole body to a common-mode voltage Vcm relative to the amplifier common. Each recording electrode connects the body to an amplifier input through its electrode impedance (Z1 or Z2). Each input has a finite impedance to the common (Zin), which includes the input resistance and, at 50 Hz, the input and cable capacitance. The electrode impedance and the input impedance form a potential divider. If the two electrode impedances are equal, both inputs receive the same fraction of Vcm and the difference is zero. If they are unequal, the fractions differ and a differential voltage appears that the amplifier cannot distinguish from a real signal.

    Figure 6.3f. The impedance-imbalance potential divider. The common-mode voltage on the body reaches each amplifier input through its electrode impedance. Unequal electrode impedances give unequal fractions of Vcm at the two inputs, and the difference is amplified as if it were a real signal.

    V+ = Vcm Zin / (Z1 + Zin) V− = Vcm Zin / (Z2 + Zin) Verr = V+ − V− ≈ Vcm (Z2 − Z1) / Zin (Z1, Z2 << Zin) CMRR_imb = Vcm / Verr ≈ Zin / |Z2 − Z1| where Vcm is the common-mode voltage on the body, Z1 and Z2 the impedances of the non-inverting and inverting electrodes, Zin the common-mode input impedance of each amplifier input, Verr the differential error and CMRR_imb the rejection ratio that the imbalance alone would allow.

    Worked example. Suppose the environment produces a common-mode voltage of 10 mV RMS at 50 Hz on the patient (an example; the real value can range from well under 1 mV in a shielded room to tens of millivolts or more near unshielded wiring). The vertex electrode is well prepared at 2 kΩ, but the mastoid electrode has lifted slightly and reads 12 kΩ, an imbalance of 10 kΩ.

    • With Zin = 10 MΩ: Verr ≈ 0.010 × 10 000 / 10 000 000 = 10 µV RMS. The imbalance alone limits the rejection to 0.010 / 0.000010 = 1000, or 60 dB. The error is about 20 times larger than a 0.5 µV wave V.
    • With Zin = 1 GΩ: Verr ≈ 0.010 × 10 000 / 10^9 = 0.1 µV RMS, a rejection of 10^5 or 100 dB.
    • With Zin = 10 MΩ but the mastoid re-prepared to 3 kΩ (imbalance 1 kΩ): Verr ≈ 1 µV RMS.

    Combining imbalance with the amplifier's own CMRR

    The amplifier itself has a finite CMRR, set by mismatches inside it. Its own error is Vcm / CMRR_amp. In the worst case the two errors add in phase, and the effective rejection is:

    1 / CMRR_eff = 1 / CMRR_amp + 1 / CMRR_imb where all ratios are linear (not dB); CMRR_eff is the overall rejection seen at the electrodes.

    Continuing the example with an amplifier CMRR of 110 dB (a ratio of about 316 000): the amplifier's own error is 10 mV / 316 000 = 0.032 µV. With Zin = 10 MΩ the total is 10 + 0.032 ≈ 10.03 µV, an effective CMRR of about 60 dB. The excellent amplifier specification is irrelevant: the electrodes decide. With Zin = 1 GΩ the total is 0.1 + 0.032 = 0.13 µV, an effective CMRR of about 97.6 dB. The chain is only as good as its weakest link, and at mains frequency the weak link is usually electrode imbalance.

    Key ideaAt 50 Hz the input impedance of a real amplifier is limited by capacitance as well as resistance. A total of 100 pF from input to common (input stage plus cable) has an impedance of 1 / (2π × 50 × 100 pF) ≈ 32 MΩ at 50 Hz, however high the DC input resistance is. Shielded or active cables and amplifiers placed close to the head reduce this capacitance, which is one reason some systems put the preamplifier in a small box near the patient or even at the electrode.

    Try it: impedance imbalance and mains error

    Set the two electrode impedances, the amplifier input impedance and CMRR, and the common-mode mains voltage on the body. The plot shows one sweep of a clean ABR with the resulting 50 Hz differential error added (no averaging).

    Error from imbalance
    Error from amplifier CMRR
    Total 50 Hz error (RMS)
    Effective CMRR
    Verdict (wave V 0.5 microvolt)
    ABR plus 50 Hz errorclean ABR

    Things to try: set Z1 = Z2 and watch the imbalance error vanish even at high impedance (in this simple model; in practice other mechanisms still favour low impedance). Then lower the input impedance to 10 MΩ and unbalance the electrodes by 5 to 10 kΩ. Finally raise the common-mode voltage to 100 mV, as might happen with the patient's bed next to an unshielded extension cable, and note how quickly the error exceeds the ABR.

    Driven right leg and active ground

    The term driven right leg (DRL) comes from electrocardiography, where the right-leg electrode was traditionally the ground. Instead of connecting the ground electrode directly to the amplifier common, a DRL circuit senses the common-mode voltage at the recording inputs, inverts and amplifies it, and drives it back into the body through the ground electrode. This negative feedback actively pulls the body's common-mode voltage towards zero, typically reducing it by a factor equal to the loop gain. Less common-mode voltage means less error from any residual imbalance. The drive current is limited by a series resistor for safety. Some EEG and evoked-potential amplifiers use this principle, often under names such as active ground or driven common; others use a passive isolated common. The electrode that plays this role still goes on a quiet site with good contact, and its impedance still matters, because the feedback can only drive the body through it.

    Why the patient ground is isolated

    Evoked-potential equipment is medical electrical equipment with an applied part: the electrodes make deliberate, low-impedance electrical contact with the patient, often through abraded skin. The general safety standard for such equipment, IEC 60601-1, requires the applied part to be separated from mains and from earth by an isolation barrier, and it limits the leakage current that may flow through the patient in normal use and under single-fault conditions (limits of the order of tens of microamperes, depending on the applied-part classification). The input stage and its common are therefore powered through an isolated supply and the signal crosses the barrier by optical, magnetic or capacitive coupling. The isolated common is not connected to mains earth. This protects the patient if they touch another earthed device or if a fault occurs elsewhere, and it also helps with interference, because the patient is not tied to a noisy earth.

    SafetyNever connect evoked-potential electrodes to an unapproved or home-made amplifier, never connect the patient ground to a building earth, and do not share electrodes between two instruments unless both manufacturers explicitly permit it. In the NICU, the baby may already be connected to other monitors; follow the unit's protocol and the manufacturers' instructions about simultaneous connection.

    8. How placement affects amplitude, polarity, latency, noise and artefact

    Placement changes are the most frequent practical cause of odd ABR waveforms. The table compares the standard vertical montage with common deviations. Values are typical tendencies, not rules; individual heads and systems vary.

    9. Artefacts and interference

    An artefact is any recorded voltage that does not come from the auditory pathway. Interference is the subset that comes from outside the patient (mains, equipment). The remedies differ, so identify the type first. Looking at the raw, unaveraged EEG on the screen (most systems show it continuously) is the fastest diagnostic tool.

    Mains interference: 50 Hz and harmonics

    In India, as in the UK and most of Europe, Asia and Africa, mains electricity is supplied at a nominal 50 Hz (230 V single phase). The Americas and some other regions use 60 Hz. Mains interference appears as a regular sinusoid of 20 ms period (50 Hz) in the raw EEG, often with harmonics at 100, 150 Hz and above that give it a distorted, peaky shape. Rectifiers, dimmers and switching power supplies produce strong harmonics. It enters by three routes:

    1. Capacitive (electric field) coupling to the body. The patient's body forms a capacitor with nearby mains wiring (a few picofarads) and with earth (hundreds of picofarads). A small displacement current flows through the body and out through the ground electrode. For example, 3 pF to a 230 V, 50 Hz line gives a current of 2π × 50 × 3 × 10^-12 × 230 ≈ 0.22 µA; flowing through a 10 kΩ ground electrode this produces a common-mode voltage of about 2.2 mV. This is the Vcm of section 7. Remedy: low ground impedance, distance from mains cables, and balanced recording electrodes.
    2. Capacitive coupling directly to a lead. A lead running beside a mains cable collects a displacement current that flows through its own electrode impedance. For example 0.1 pF of coupling gives about 7.2 nA; through a 5 kΩ electrode that is 36 µV of differential interference, and through 1 kΩ about 7 µV. Remedy: keep leads away from power cables, keep electrode impedance low, use short and shielded leads.
    3. Magnetic induction into lead loops. A changing magnetic field through the loop formed by the two recording leads and the head induces a voltage directly in series with the signal. Because it is differential, CMRR does nothing against it.

    Figure 6.3g. Magnetic pickup in electrode leads. The two recording leads and the head form a loop. A 50 Hz magnetic field B through the loop induces a voltage proportional to the enclosed area A. Twisting the leads together, keeping them short and routing them close to the skin makes the area small and makes adjacent twists cancel.

    V_induced = 2π f B A where f is the frequency of the field (50 Hz), B the magnetic flux density through the loop (RMS, in tesla) and A the loop area in square metres; V_induced is the RMS voltage in series with the signal.

    Worked example: ambient magnetic fields in a clinic room are often around 0.1 µT and can reach 1 µT or more near transformers, power supplies and wiring. With the vertex and mastoid leads taking separate paths, the loop might enclose 10 cm × 20 cm = 0.02 m². At 0.1 µT: 2π × 50 × 10^-7 × 0.02 = 0.63 µV. At 1 µT: 6.3 µV, far larger than the ABR. Twisting the leads to an effective area of about 1 cm² (10^-4 m²) reduces the 1 µT case to 0.031 µV. Distance also helps: the field from equipment falls rapidly as you move away from it.

    Other interference sources

    SourceHow it appearsWhat to do
    Fluorescent tubes with magnetic ballasts, dimmers50 Hz and 100 Hz with sharp harmonicsswitch off or move away; use incandescent or well-filtered LED lighting in the test room
    Switching power supplies, laptop chargers, LED drivershigh-frequency bursts, sometimes aliasing into the band, irregular spikesrun the laptop from battery during recording if the manufacturer allows; unplug chargers near the patient
    Mobile phones and wireless devicesintermittent bursts synchronous with transmission (a buzzing pattern)switch phones off or to flight mode, including the parent's and the clinician's
    Nearby power cables, extension boards, adjustable bedssteady 50 Hzkeep all cables at least about 1 m from the patient and the leads where practical; do not run leads along power cords
    NICU equipment: incubators, radiant warmers, infusion pumps, monitors, phototherapy lights50 Hz, harmonics, and pump motor or heater switching transientsturn off non-essential devices only with nursing agreement; move the head box and leads away; consider testing when the baby can be out of the incubator safely
    Transducer (earphone) electromagnetic fieldstimulus artefact at time zerosee stimulus artefact below

    Stimulus artefact

    The earphone is driven by a current of milliamperes and produces electric and magnetic fields at the instant of each stimulus. The electrodes and leads pick these up as a sharp deflection at 0 ms that can last into the first millisecond and obscure wave I, or even saturate the amplifier. With a bone vibrator the artefact is usually larger, because the transducer sits on the mastoid next to the electrodes. Remedies:

    • Use insert earphones. The transducer sits some distance away (on the shoulder or clothing) and the sound travels through a tube. The acoustic delay of the tube (typically about 0.8 to 0.9 ms, see 6.2) also separates the electrical artefact in time from wave I.
    • Keep transducer cables and electrode leads apart, and do not lay the transducer body on or next to the electrode leads.
    • Use alternating stimulus polarity. The electrical artefact follows the stimulus polarity, so it cancels in the average of rarefaction and condensation sweeps. The cochlear microphonic cancels too; the neural response largely remains. When auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder is suspected, record rarefaction and condensation separately and compare them with a trial in which the insert tube is clamped (sound blocked): a microphonic reverses with polarity and disappears with the tube clamped, whereas a pure electrical artefact remains with the tube clamped.
    • For bone conduction, place the vibrator on the mastoid and move the reference electrode to the earlobe, or use a more distant reference where the protocol permits; keep the vibrator lead away from the electrode leads.

    Physiological artefacts

    ArtefactOriginAppearanceRemedy
    EMG (muscle)Jaw (masseter, temporalis), neck (trapezius, sternocleidomastoid), scalp muscles; swallowing, sucking, cryingbroadband high-frequency noise, bursts, frequent rejections; the post-auricular muscle can produce a reflex response at about 12 to 15 ms in tense patientsrelaxation, pillow under the neck and head, jaw slightly open, recline, natural sleep; earlobe or mastoid rather than neck reference in awake patients
    Eye movements and blinksThe eye is an electrical dipole (corneo-retinal potential); blinks and movements change the field at frontal siteslarge slow deflections, especially with forehead electrodeseyes closed and relaxed, sleep; the high-pass filter and artefact rejection deal with most of the rest
    ECGHeart; stronger at the neck and chesta regular sharp complex at the heart rate, larger with neck, shoulder or chest electrodes, and in infantskeep electrodes on the head; a non-integer stimulus rate prevents build-up; rejection
    MovementHead turns, lead pulls, electrode pressure changeslarge slow transients, saturationsecure leads with tape, avoid tension, support the head
    Sweat and drying gelSkin potential changes, gel drying over long sessionsslow drift, rising impedancecool room, re-check impedance during long tests, replace dried electrodes

    Line-synchronous stimulus rates

    If the stimulus rate divides exactly into the mains frequency or one of its harmonics, the mains waveform has the same phase at the start of every sweep. It then adds coherently in the average like a real response, instead of averaging towards zero. At 50 Hz, rates of 10, 12.5 or 25 per second are exactly synchronous with the mains; 20 per second repeats the 100 Hz harmonic in phase every sweep. Clinical protocols therefore use non-integer rates such as 11.1, 19.1, 21.1, 27.7, 37.7 or 39.1 per second (and similar values adjusted for 60 Hz countries). The BSA ABR procedure, for example, lists rates such as 11.1 per second for neurological ABR and 45.1 to 49.1 per second for click threshold testing, and notes that the rate must not be harmonically related to the 50 Hz mains.

    Exam tipMains that is not synchronised with the stimulus is still harmful: it enlarges the noise in every sweep, triggers artefact rejection and can saturate the amplifier. A non-integer rate prevents coherent build-up; it does not replace good electrode practice.

    Notch filters

    Most systems offer a 50 Hz (or 60 Hz) notch filter, a narrow band-stop filter at mains frequency. It can rescue a recording in a hostile environment, but it has costs. A narrow notch rings: a transient input produces a decaying 50 Hz oscillation in the output that can resemble a slow wave. It also removes genuine response energy near 50 Hz, which matters for low-frequency tone-burst ABRs and infant ABRs (which have substantial low-frequency content), and for any ASSR analysis near that frequency. Some recommended procedures advise against a notch filter for 500 Hz tone-burst testing. Use it as a last resort after fixing the electrodes and environment, record that it was used, and compare with norms obtained under the same filter settings.

    10. How the response is detected: SNR, averaging and test time

    After amplification and filtering, the response is recovered by stimulus-locked averaging (4.4). If each sweep contains the response s(t) plus random noise with RMS value σ, the noise remaining in the average of N sweeps, called the residual noise, is:

    RN = σ / √N N = (σ / RN_target)² T = N / (R × (1 − p)) where RN is the residual noise (µV RMS), σ the single-sweep noise (µV RMS), N the number of accepted sweeps, RN_target the residual noise you want to reach, T the test time in seconds, R the stimulus rate per second and p the fraction of sweeps rejected.

    The squared relationship is the practical heart of this topic. Halving the single-sweep noise cuts the number of sweeps needed by a factor of four, and therefore the time at each level by a factor of four. Good skin preparation, balanced impedances, twisted leads and a sleeping patient are the most effective ways to shorten a test.

    Worked example with a residual-noise target of 0.05 µV (an illustrative figure; the BSA procedure for example uses 25 to 40 nV criteria for response judgements) and a stimulus rate of 39.1 per second, ignoring rejections:

    Recording condition (illustrative)Single-sweep noise σSweeps neededTime at 39.1/sTime at 11.1/s
    Good preparation, asleep2 µV RMS(2 / 0.05)² = 160041 s2.4 min
    Average4 µV RMS64002.7 min9.6 min
    Poor (imbalance, EMG)8 µV RMS25 60010.9 min38.4 min

    A threshold search needs several levels per frequency and two replications at the lowest levels, so the difference between the first and last rows is the difference between completing an infant's assessment in one sleep and not completing it.

    Estimating residual noise and response quality

    • Plus-minus (±) reference. Alternate sweeps are added and subtracted. The response cancels in the difference, leaving an estimate of the noise in the average. Many systems display a residual-noise value in nanovolts or microvolts derived from this or a similar method.
    • Replication. Two independent (often interleaved) averages A and B are compared. Their difference, (A − B)/2, estimates the residual noise; their correlation indicates whether a response is present.
    • Fsp. Elberling and Don (1984) defined a statistic, Fsp, as the ratio of the variance of the averaged waveform over a time window (signal plus residual noise) to the estimated variance of the residual noise, which is obtained from the sweep-to-sweep variance at a single fixed time point divided by N. In the absence of a response Fsp is near 1. Its sampling distribution approximates an F distribution, so a criterion value can be chosen for a desired confidence; a value around 3.1 is often quoted for about 99 % confidence under the original assumptions. Fsp rises as averaging continues if a response is present, which lets the clinician stop as soon as the criterion is met.
    • Weighted averaging. Some systems weight each block of sweeps by the inverse of its noise variance (sometimes called Bayesian weighting), so noisy blocks contribute less. This lowers residual noise when noise is non-stationary, for example when a baby stirs.
    • ASSR detection. The ASSR is judged in the frequency domain: the spectral amplitude at the modulation frequency is compared with the noise in neighbouring frequency bins (an F-test) or the phase coherence across epochs is tested. The same √N law applies: lower EEG noise shortens the time to a significant detection. See 6.4.

    Try it: residual noise, sweeps and test time

    Three recording conditions with illustrative single-sweep noise of 2, 4 and 8 µV RMS. Move the target line and the stimulus rate; the readouts give sweeps and time per average for each condition.

    Good preparation
    Average
    Poor
    good preparation, 2 µV RMS per sweepaverage, 4 µVpoor, 8 µVtarget

    12. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment and consumables

    The recording interface described here is found in all clinical ABR and ASSR systems, for example systems from Interacoustics (Eclipse platform), Natus (Bio-logic Navigator Pro, and systems from the former Otometrics/MADSEN range), GSI (Grason-Stadler), Intelligent Hearing Systems (SmartEP), MAICO, Inventis and Vivosonic (Integrity, which places part of the amplifier near the electrode). Systems differ in the head-box layout, the number of channels, whether the ground is passive or actively driven, the impedance test frequency, and whether impedance is shown per electrode or per pair.

    Consumables include disposable pre-gelled Ag/AgCl electrodes (for example the Ambu Neuroline family and similar neonatal electrodes from several manufacturers), reusable Ag/AgCl or gold cup electrodes with conductive paste (for example Ten20 paste from Weaver and Company), abrasive skin-preparation gels (for example NuPrep, also from Weaver and Company), and ear-canal electrodes of the TIPtrode type from Etymotic Research.

    RememberExamples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation. Naming a product is not an endorsement, and electrodes and leads should be those approved for use with the specific instrument.

    13. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation basics

    Applications of good montage and interface practice

    • Threshold ABR and ASSR in infants. Diagnostic follow-up after newborn screening is usually done in natural sleep with a limited time window. Low noise from careful preparation and lead routing directly increases the number of frequencies and levels that can be completed.
    • Neurological (site-of-lesion) ABR. Interpeak latencies (I to III, III to V, I to V) and interaural differences require a clear wave I on both sides. A two-channel ipsi/contra montage, low impedance and, if needed, an ear-canal electrode or horizontal channel help to identify wave I reliably.
    • Auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder. Separating the cochlear microphonic from stimulus artefact depends on correct lead routing, insert earphones and the tube-clamp control trial.
    • Bone-conduction ABR. The vibrator is close to the electrodes; montage adjustments (earlobe reference, lead separation) reduce artefact.
    • Intraoperative and NICU recording. Electrically hostile environments make balanced low impedance, short twisted leads and a good ground essential.

    Interpretation basics that depend on the interface

    • Always check the montage and polarity convention before reading latencies. An inverted waveform with normal timing points to a lead error.
    • Absent wave I in the ipsilateral channel with a clear wave V may be due to hearing loss, but first exclude a wrong reference (contralateral mastoid selected, reference on the nape) and a large stimulus artefact.
    • A "response" that does not replicate, or that appears with the same shape at all levels including very low ones, may be mains or artefact. Check the raw EEG and the replications.
    • A flat average with very low residual noise and very low impedance on all channels can indicate gel bridging or a short circuit, not an absent response.
    • Judging "no response" requires a documented low residual noise, not only a flat trace. A flat but noisy average is inconclusive.

    Advantages of surface electrodes

    • Non-invasive, quick and inexpensive
    • Suitable for sleeping infants
    • Standardised sites give comparable norms
    • Far-field recording sees the whole brainstem pathway

    Limitations

    • Very small signals: long averaging needed
    • Sensitive to skin, hair, sweat, movement and muscle tension
    • Susceptible to environmental interference
    • Wave I small at the mastoid in hearing loss; closer electrodes may be needed

    14. Practical troubleshooting

    A systematic sequence saves time. Work from the patient connection outwards, and look at the raw EEG before starting (and while) averaging.

    Figure 6.3h. Troubleshooting flowchart. Decisions (amber) are checked in order from the patient connection outwards; actions (blue) are taken when a check fails, after which the recording is re-checked.

    SymptomLikely causeAction
    Flat line on one or all channels, no EEG visibleLead unplugged, wrong socket, channel switched off, broken lead, head box not connected; gel bridge shorting two electrodesCheck each lead from electrode to socket; check channel set-up and gain; look at impedance (very low on both may mean a bridge); replace suspect lead
    Trace pinned at the top or bottom (saturation)Electrode off, dried gel, large offset from polarised or mixed-metal electrodesRe-check impedance; replace electrode; use matching electrode types on one patient
    Large regular 50 Hz (and harmonics)High or unbalanced impedance, poor ground, leads near mains cables, lead loops, nearby devicesRe-prepare to balanced low impedance; check ground; twist and shorten leads; move or unplug devices; move bed away from walls with wiring; notch filter only as last resort
    One channel much noisier than the otherThe electrode unique to that channel (e.g. contralateral mastoid) is poor, or its lead is near a sourceCheck impedance of that electrode; re-prepare; re-route its lead
    Excessive rejections, broadband noiseEMG from tense jaw or neck, movement, awake infant, reference on neck muscle, rejection level too strict for the patientImprove relaxation: recline, pillow under neck, jaw slightly open; wait for sleep; move reference to earlobe or mastoid; consider the protocol's rejection setting
    Waveform inverted (troughs where peaks expected)Active and reference leads swapped; display polarity changedCheck leads at the head box against the montage; check display convention; re-record
    No wave I (wave V present)Reference on contralateral side or nape; conductive or high-frequency hearing loss; stimulus artefact obscuring itCheck the ipsilateral reference is selected; add ear-canal electrode or horizontal channel; increase level; use alternating polarity
    Huge artefact at time zero lasting into wave ITransducer near electrode leads; supra-aural or bone transducer; single polarityUse inserts; separate transducer from leads; alternate polarity; for bone conduction, re-route leads and consider earlobe reference
    Slow wandering baseline and driftSweat, drying gel, movement, polarisable electrodesCool room, fresh electrodes, secure leads; check high-pass filter setting
    Regular spikes at about 1 per secondECG, especially with neck, shoulder or chest electrodesKeep electrodes on the head; non-integer rate; rejection
    "Response" at all levels, including no-stimulus controlLine-synchronous rate, electrical artefactChange to a non-integer rate; run a no-stimulus (or tube-clamped) control average

    Patient-state tips

    • Infants: schedule the test at a usual sleep time; keep the baby slightly hungry and awake before arrival where the local protocol advises; prepare the electrodes while the baby is being fed; test in natural sleep. Swaddling and a quiet, dim room help.
    • Adults and older children: recline in a comfortable chair or lie down, with a pillow or neck roll so the neck muscles do not hold up the head. Ask the patient to let the jaw hang slightly open and to keep the eyes closed. Many adults fall asleep, which improves the recording.
    • Avoid talking to the patient during averaging; pause the average when the patient moves or swallows.
    • Sedation or general anaesthesia may be considered for some children when natural sleep fails, under local medical policy.

    Room and cable tips

    • Keep the head box close to the patient's head so that leads are short, and route leads together, twisted or bundled, away from the transducer cables and power cords.
    • Keep power strips, chargers, lamp cables and monitors at least about 1 m from the patient and the leads where practical.
    • Switch off fluorescent lights with magnetic ballasts and dimmers if interference appears; check whether a nearby room's equipment is the source by watching the raw EEG while items are switched off one at a time.
    • Ask everyone in the room to switch phones off or to flight mode.
    • If the clinic has a shielded or electrically quiet room, test the most difficult cases there.

    Demonstration

    Equipment: an ABR/ASSR system with at least two channels and an impedance meter, insert earphones, disposable and reusable electrodes, abrasive gel, alcohol wipes, conductive paste, tape, a volunteer adult, a mains extension cable and a phone charger (as interference sources), a tape measure for 10-20 positions, and this page for the interactive demos.

    1. Measure and mark Cz (half the nasion-inion distance and half the distance between preauricular points) and Fz on the volunteer; identify M1, M2, A1, A2 and Fpz. Students observe how "high forehead" differs from Cz.
    2. Place one electrode on unprepared skin and one on prepared skin. Read the impedance of each; show the difference (often several-fold).
    3. Apply the standard vertical two-channel montage (Cz or Fz active, M1 and M2 references, Fpz ground). Measure all impedances, bring them below about 5 kΩ and balanced within about 2 kΩ.
    4. Show the raw EEG. Ask the volunteer to clench the jaw, then relax; lift the head off the pillow, then relax. Students observe EMG bursts and the rejection counter.
    5. Lay the mains extension cable (switched on, with a load such as a lamp) next to the leads; separate the leads to form a large loop. Observe 50 Hz in the raw EEG. Twist the leads and move the cable 1 m away; observe the reduction.
    6. Record a click ABR at a high level (for example 70 to 80 dB nHL) with alternating polarity: compare wave I in the ipsilateral and contralateral channels, and the IV-V complex.
    7. Swap the active and reference leads at the head box and record again; students see the inverted waveform.
    8. Move the insert transducer body next to the electrode leads and use a single polarity; observe the stimulus artefact. Restore inserts away from leads and alternate polarity.
    9. Deliberately unbalance the impedances (lift one electrode edge) and repeat the mains test. Then use the u6-3-imbalance demo on this page to relate what was seen to the potential-divider calculation.
    10. Use the u6-3-noise demo to estimate how long each condition would take to reach a residual noise of 0.05 µV, and compare with the recorded residual-noise values.

    Current developments

    • Amplifiers placed at or near the electrode (active electrodes) and very high input impedance front ends, which reduce the effect of electrode impedance and cable pickup and make lighter skin preparation possible.
    • Dry and semi-dry electrodes developed for wearable EEG, being explored for hearing screening and for in-ear EEG recording, with the challenge of higher and less stable impedance.
    • Better statistical response detection and noise estimation (weighted averaging, frequency-domain and multi-channel detectors), which shortens test time when the noise is non-stationary.
    • Automated quality monitoring in software: continuous impedance display, automatic mains detection and prompts to the tester when noise rises.
    • Ear-canal and in-ear electrode designs for combined stimulation and recording, including research on recording evoked responses from hearing-device earpieces. These remain largely research tools.

    Practical activity and assessment

    1. In pairs, locate and mark Cz, Fz, Fpz, M1, M2, A1 and A2 on each other using the 10-20 measurements. Photograph the marks (with consent) and label them.
    2. Apply a vertical two-channel ABR montage. Record impedances before preparation, after an alcohol wipe, and after abrasive preparation for each site. Tabulate the values.
    3. Record raw EEG for 30 s in three states: relaxed with eyes closed, jaw clenched, and head lifted off the pillow. Note the artefact rejection rate in each.
    4. Record a high-level click ABR (alternating polarity) with the correct montage and with the leads swapped. Mark waves I, III and V in the correct recording and explain the swapped result.
    5. Compare the ipsilateral and contralateral channels: measure wave I and wave V amplitudes and latencies in each.
    6. Using the residual noise shown by the system after 1000 and 4000 sweeps, check whether the ratio is close to 2 (the √N law).
    7. Deliverable: a two-page report containing the impedance table, annotated waveforms, a short explanation of each observed difference in terms of dipoles, differential amplification and impedance imbalance, and a one-paragraph troubleshooting plan for a noisy NICU recording.

    Assessment: correct site identification, impedance targets reached, correct identification of waves and of the inverted recording, correct application of the equations, and a realistic troubleshooting plan.
