    6.4

    Auditory steady-state response (ASSR)

    Supplementary2 hoursPractical P9

    The auditory steady-state response (ASSR) is the brain's electrical response to a continuous, rhythmically modulated sound. Because the response repeats at a known rate, it appears as a single spectral line at that rate in the EEG spectrum, and a computer can decide statistically whether it is present. This makes the ASSR a frequency-specific, objective way to estimate hearing thresholds, including in infants and in severe to profound losses where the ABR runs out of stimulus level.

    What it is

    An evoked potential recorded with the same electrodes and amplifier as the ABR, but elicited by tones that are amplitude-modulated, frequency-modulated or both (or by trains of narrow-band chirps) at a steady rate, typically around 40 Hz or 80 to 100 Hz. The response is a small sinusoid, often only tens of nanovolts, locked in phase to the modulation.

    Why it is required

    Clinicians need threshold estimates at 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz in each ear for people who cannot respond behaviourally, above all infants referred from newborn screening. The ASSR gives these with a statistical detection criterion, can test several frequencies in both ears at the same time, and can use stimulus levels up to about 110 to 120 dB HL, which helps to separate severe from profound loss when planning hearing aids or cochlear implants.

    How it works

    The stimulus generator produces carrier tones, each modulated at its own rate. The EEG is recorded in epochs whose length is an exact number of modulation cycles, averaged, and converted to a spectrum with the fast Fourier transform (FFT). The amplitude in the frequency bin at the modulation rate is compared with the amplitudes in neighbouring bins (noise) using an F-test, and the consistency of phase is tested across epochs. A significant result means the ear and auditory pathway responded to that carrier at that level.

    Key ideaThe ABR asks "is there a waveform at the right latency?" and a human reads the trace. The ASSR asks "is there more energy at exactly fm than in the surrounding noise?" and a statistical test answers. Both use the same scalp electrodes, differential amplifier and averaging principles described in 4.4 and 6.2; electrode practice and impedance are covered in 6.3.

    1. Basic working principle

    Transient and steady-state responses

    An evoked potential is transient when each stimulus is presented after the response to the previous stimulus has died away. The click ABR at 20 to 40 stimuli per second is transient: wave V appears about 5.5 to 6 ms after each click and the system has returned to baseline well before the next click 25 to 50 ms later. Transient responses are analysed in the time domain: we look at the waveform and measure peak latencies and amplitudes.

    If stimuli are repeated so fast that the response to one stimulus overlaps the responses to the next ones, the individual responses merge into a periodic waveform with the same period as the stimulation. After an initial build-up, this waveform stays constant in amplitude and phase for as long as the stimulation continues. Regan, who studied such responses in the visual system, described a steady-state evoked potential as one whose discrete frequency components remain constant in amplitude and phase over an infinitely long time. In practice "steady" means over the recording time, typically minutes.

    A periodic signal contains energy only at its repetition frequency and the harmonics of that frequency. A steady-state response to a stimulus modulated at 90 Hz is therefore concentrated at 90 Hz (with smaller components at 180 Hz, 270 Hz and so on). This is what makes frequency-domain analysis so efficient: all the response energy lands in one or a few known bins of the spectrum, while background EEG noise is spread over all frequencies.

    Transient response (ABR)

    • Stimulus: brief click, tone burst or chirp, then silence.
    • Response dies away before the next stimulus.
    • Analysed in the time domain: peaks, latencies, amplitudes.
    • Detection traditionally by visual inspection with replication; objective measures (for example Fsp and residual noise) are available on many systems.
    • Frequency specificity limited by the short stimulus: a brief tone burst has a wide spectrum.

    Steady-state response (ASSR)

    • Stimulus: continuous tone modulated at a steady rate, or a fast regular train of chirps.
    • Responses overlap and form a periodic signal at the modulation rate.
    • Analysed in the frequency domain: amplitude and phase at fm.
    • Detection by a statistical test with a stated false-positive rate.
    • Frequency specificity good: the spectrum of a modulated tone is narrow and centred on the carrier.

    Where the response comes from: the carrier and the envelope

    The ASSR stimulus has two parts. The carrier is a pure tone, for example 1000 Hz, which determines which place on the basilar membrane is excited. The modulation is a slow periodic change, for example of amplitude at 90 Hz, which is what the brain follows. The inner hair cells act as half-wave rectifiers: they release transmitter mainly during one half of each carrier cycle. Rectification followed by the low-pass behaviour of the synapse and neural membranes recovers the envelope of the sound, in the same way that a diode and capacitor demodulate an AM radio signal. Auditory nerve fibres therefore fire with a probability that rises and falls at the modulation rate, and neurons in the brainstem and cortex that can follow that rate phase-lock to it. The summed activity of these neurons produces a scalp potential at fm.

    DefinitionThe carrier frequency fc tells you which part of the cochlea (and therefore which audiometric frequency) is being tested. The modulation frequency fm tells you where in the EEG spectrum to look for the response. The response is at fm, never at fc; the scalp EEG cannot follow a 1000 Hz or 4000 Hz carrier in any useful way.

    Because the response frequency depends only on the modulation and not on the carrier, several carriers can be presented together, each with its own modulation rate. Each response appears at its own spectral line and can be tested separately. This is the basis of the multiple-stimulus ASSR described in section 3.

    Modulation rate and generators: 40 Hz versus 80 to 100 Hz

    Different parts of the auditory pathway can follow different rates of modulation. Neurons in the auditory cortex follow slower modulations well but cannot phase-lock to fast ones; brainstem neurons can follow faster rates. The rate chosen therefore selects which generators dominate the recording.

    • Around 40 Hz. Galambos, Makeig and Talmachoff (1981) described a large 40 Hz response in awake adults. Around 40 Hz the successive waves of the middle-latency response (Na, Pa, Nb, Pb, spaced roughly 25 ms apart) overlap in phase and add constructively, and there are substantial contributions from thalamus and auditory cortex. In awake adults the 40 Hz ASSR is typically several times larger than the 80 Hz response and gives thresholds close to behavioural thresholds. Its disadvantages are that it falls considerably in amplitude during sleep and sedation, and it is small and unreliable in infants and young children because the cortical generators are immature.
    • Around 80 to 100 Hz. At these rates the response is generated mainly in the brainstem (with contributions from regions such as the cochlear nucleus, superior olive and inferior colliculus). It is much smaller than the awake 40 Hz response, typically of the order of tens of nanovolts near threshold, but it is only slightly affected by sleep and sedation and is present and reliable in infants. The EEG background is also lower at 80 to 100 Hz than at 40 Hz, so the signal-to-noise ratio is workable. This is why infant and sleeping-patient protocols use rates of roughly 80 to 100 Hz.

    Responses to rates between these ranges (roughly 50 to 70 Hz) are small in sleep and are rarely used clinically; very low rates (below about 20 Hz) produce responses dominated by cortical activity that is strongly dependent on attention and state.

    Clinical linkA sleeping infant tested at 40 Hz may show no responses at all, even with normal hearing, because sleep and immaturity reduce the cortical contribution. The same infant tested at about 90 Hz typically shows clear responses. Match the modulation rate to the patient state: roughly 80 to 100 Hz for infants and sleeping or sedated patients; 40 Hz is an option for awake, relaxed adults, for example in medico-legal assessment.

    Why a steady-state response can be detected objectively

    The FFT of an averaged EEG epoch divides the signal into frequency bins. Background EEG noise is a random process, so the noise in any one bin has a random amplitude and a random phase. A true ASSR has a fixed amplitude and a fixed phase relative to the modulation. Two properties of a real response can therefore be tested statistically: its amplitude is larger than the noise in the neighbouring bins (F-test), and its phase is consistent from one epoch to the next (phase coherence). Both tests give a probability that the observed result could arise from noise alone, and a threshold such as p < 0.05 sets the false-positive rate. This replaces the subjective reading of waveforms with a decision rule that every tester applies in the same way.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    An ASSR system is normally a software module on an AEP platform. It shares the transducers, patient isolation, electrode leads, preamplifier and ADC with the ABR module (see 6.2), and differs in the stimulus generator and in the analysis software. The block diagram below shows the full chain for a multiple-stimulus system.

    Figure 6.4a. Signal flow in a multiple-stimulus ASSR system. The stimulus DAC and the recording ADC run from one clock, so the modulation frequencies stay locked to the FFT bins.

    Walk-through, block by block:

    1. Carrier oscillators. Digital sine generators produce the carrier tones, typically at 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz for each ear. Many systems adjust each carrier slightly so that it also contains a whole number of cycles in the stimulus buffer, which avoids discontinuities at buffer boundaries.
    2. Modulators. Each carrier is multiplied by its own envelope (amplitude modulation) and/or its instantaneous frequency is varied (frequency modulation) at its own modulation rate fm. In chirp-based systems this block is replaced by a generator that repeats a narrow-band chirp at the chosen rate.
    3. Summation and DAC. The modulated carriers for one ear are added and sent to one DAC channel; the other ear has its own channel. Summing up to four carriers raises the peak level, so the software must leave headroom to avoid clipping.
    4. Attenuator and transducer. A calibrated attenuator sets the level. Insert earphones are standard for air conduction because they increase interaural attenuation and move the transducer away from the electrodes. A bone-conduction vibrator or loudspeakers (for aided sound-field testing) may also be used.
    5. Electrodes and differential amplifier. Identical in principle to the ABR: a non-inverting electrode on the vertex or high forehead, an inverting electrode at the nape or mastoid, and a ground. The isolated differential amplifier rejects common-mode interference (see 6.3).
    6. Filter and ADC. The pass-band differs from the transient ABR: the high-pass corner is lower (for example about 1 to 30 Hz) because the response lies at 40 Hz or 80 to 100 Hz, and the low-pass corner is set to a few hundred hertz. A steep anti-aliasing filter before the ADC is essential (see limitations: aliased stimulus artefact). Filter basics are in 1.8.
    7. Common clock. The DAC and ADC are driven from the same clock, or synchronised, so that exactly the intended number of modulation cycles fits into each recording epoch. Without this lock the response would drift across FFT bins.
    8. Epoch buffer and artefact rejection. The continuous EEG is cut into epochs (for example about 1 s long). Epochs whose peak amplitude exceeds a rejection limit (typically a few tens of microvolts) are discarded, because movement and muscle bursts would inflate the noise.
    9. Averaging. Accepted epochs are averaged, either as time waveforms or as complex spectra; for a linear average the two routes give the same result. Some systems also weight epochs by their noise level.
    10. FFT and statistics. The averaged epoch is transformed to the frequency domain; the amplitude and phase at each fm are extracted and tested against the noise estimate.
    11. Level control and threshold. When a response is detected at one level, the software or clinician lowers the level for that carrier; when it is absent, the level is raised. The lowest level with a significant response is the physiological threshold, which is converted to an estimated hearing level using correction values.

    Components

    ComponentFunctionWhat can go wrong
    Stimulus generator (software)Creates carriers and envelopes with exact ratesWrong protocol (40 Hz rates in a sleeping infant); modulation rates too close together
    DAC and output amplifierConverts the summed stimuli to voltageClipping when many carriers are added at high level, producing distortion and spread of energy
    AttenuatorSets stimulus level in calibrated stepsCalibration out of date; wrong transducer selected in software
    Insert earphonesAir-conduction delivery, high interaural attenuationFoam tip not inserted deeply, collapsed canal in neonates, blocked tube
    Bone vibratorBone-conduction deliveryLarge electromagnetic artefact that can alias to fm; limited maximum output
    Electrodes and leadsPick up scalp potentialHigh or unbalanced impedance, lead near transducer cable (artefact pick-up)
    Isolated differential amplifierAmplifies the difference between electrodes, rejects mainsPoor common-mode rejection from unbalanced impedance, saturation after movement
    Anti-aliasing filter and ADCBand-limits and samples the EEGInadequate filtering lets stimulus artefact alias into the response bins
    Analysis softwareEpochs, rejection, FFT, F-test, phase statistics, stopping rulesToo-lenient criterion or repeated testing without correction inflates false positives
    Correction tablesConvert physiological thresholds to estimated HLUsing corrections from another system or stimulus type

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    The ASSR stimulus must do two jobs: stimulate a narrow region of the cochlea (frequency specificity) and carry a strong, regular periodicity that the auditory pathway follows (response size). Several stimulus families trade these off in different ways. The response the system records is always a sinusoid-like EEG component at the modulation or repetition rate, plus smaller harmonics.

    Amplitude-modulated (AM) tones

    A sinusoidal AM tone is a carrier whose amplitude rises and falls sinusoidally:

    s(t) = A [1 + m sin(2π fm t)] sin(2π fc t) where A is the carrier amplitude, m the modulation depth (0 to 1, usually expressed as 0 to 100 %), fm the modulation frequency and fc the carrier frequency.

    Modulation depth is the fraction by which the envelope varies: with m = 1 (100 %) the envelope swings from zero to 2A; with m = 0.5 it swings from 0.5A to 1.5A. Response amplitude grows with depth, so clinical systems use 100 % AM or close to it.

    Using the identity sin(a) sin(b) = ½[cos(a − b) − cos(a + b)], the product term splits into two tones:

    s(t) = A sin(2π fc t) + (mA/2) cos(2π (fc − fm) t) − (mA/2) cos(2π (fc + fm) t) The spectrum has three lines: the carrier at fc and two sidebands at fc ± fm, each of amplitude mA/2.

    Figure 6.4b. Stimulus spectrum of a 1000 Hz carrier with 100 % AM at 90 Hz. The acoustic energy lies between 910 and 1090 Hz, which is why the ASSR is frequency specific. The EEG response, in contrast, appears at 90 Hz.

    Worked example (AM). Carrier fc = 1000 Hz, fm = 90 Hz, m = 1:

    • Components at 1000 − 90 = 910 Hz, 1000 Hz and 1000 + 90 = 1090 Hz.
    • Sideband amplitude = m/2 = 0.5 of the carrier amplitude, that is 20 log10(0.5) = −6.0 dB relative to the carrier.
    • Power: the carrier power is proportional to 1, each sideband to 0.52 = 0.25, so the sidebands carry 0.5 / 1.5 = 33 % of the total power.
    • Total bandwidth = 2 fm = 180 Hz, which is 18 % of the carrier frequency. For a 4000 Hz carrier modulated at 101 Hz, the lines are at 3899, 4000 and 4101 Hz, a bandwidth of only about 5 % of the carrier.

    Compare this with a brief tone burst used for frequency-specific ABR: a 2-1-2 cycle 1000 Hz tone burst lasts 5 ms and its main spectral lobe spreads over several hundred hertz either side of 1000 Hz. The AM tone is far more frequency specific, although at high levels the cochlear excitation still spreads beyond the stimulus spectrum (upward spread of excitation), as for any tone.

    Frequency-modulated (FM) tones

    In FM the carrier frequency swings above and below fc at the rate fm. The FM depth is usually given as the peak-to-peak frequency swing as a percentage of the carrier. With 20 % depth on a 1000 Hz carrier, the frequency moves between 900 and 1100 Hz; the peak deviation is Δf = 100 Hz.

    s(t) = A sin(2π fc t − β cos(2π fm t)) β = Δf / fm where β is the modulation index and Δf the peak frequency deviation. The instantaneous frequency is fc + Δf sin(2π fm t).

    An FM tone has sidebands at fc ± fm, fc ± 2fm and so on, with amplitudes given by Bessel functions Jn(β). Worked example: Δf = 100 Hz and fm = 90 Hz give β = 1.11. Then J0 = 0.71 (carrier, −2.9 dB), J1 = 0.47 (first sidebands at 910 and 1090 Hz, −6.5 dB), J2 = 0.14 (second sidebands at 820 and 1180 Hz, −17.1 dB) and J3 = 0.03 (−31.6 dB). Carson's rule estimates the bandwidth as 2(Δf + fm) = 2(100 + 90) = 380 Hz. The envelope of a pure FM tone is flat; the response arises because, as the frequency sweeps, the peak of excitation moves back and forth along the basilar membrane, and each place is excited periodically at fm.

    Mixed modulation (MM)

    Mixed modulation applies AM and FM simultaneously at the same fm, for example 100 % AM with 20 to 25 % FM. When the phases are aligned so that the amplitude peaks at the same moment as the frequency reaches its maximum (or minimum), the neural activity evoked by the two forms of modulation adds, and the response is typically larger than with AM alone. The price is a slightly wider stimulus spectrum. Many research and clinical protocols that use sinusoidally modulated tones have used MM for this reason.

    Exponential envelope (AM2)

    An AM tone with an exponential envelope raises the sinusoidal envelope to a power, making the envelope peaks sharper and the troughs longer:

    env(t) = [(1 + sin(2π fm t)) / 2]N where N is the exponent. N = 1 is ordinary 100 % AM; N = 2 is called AM2.

    For N = 2 the envelope expands to 3/8 + ½ sin(θ) − ⅛ cos(2θ) with θ = 2πfmt, so the spectrum gains a second pair of sidebands at fc ± 2fm (amplitude 1/16 relative to 3/8 for the carrier). The more abrupt onset of each envelope cycle synchronises neurons better and typically increases the response, at a small cost in frequency specificity.

    Chirp-based stimuli

    A chirp is a short sweep whose frequency changes over a few milliseconds so that its low-frequency components are presented slightly earlier than the high-frequency ones. The timing compensates for the travelling-wave delay along the cochlea: basal (high-frequency) places respond almost immediately, apical (low-frequency) places several milliseconds later. When each frequency region is stimulated at the right moment, many nerve fibres fire together and the response is larger than for a click or an unmodified tone burst. For ASSR, some commercial systems present narrow-band chirps, each covering roughly one octave around 500, 1000, 2000 or 4000 Hz, repeated at a steady rate near 40 Hz or 90 Hz. Such a train has a line spectrum at the repetition rate and its harmonics within the chosen octave. The EEG response then contains the repetition rate and several harmonics, and detection algorithms may combine information from more than one harmonic. The design details of these stimuli and their detection algorithms are manufacturer specific.

    RememberStimulus families differ in response size and frequency specificity, and the correction values that convert physiological thresholds to estimated hearing levels are specific to the stimulus, the recording system and the protocol. Never apply correction values measured with one stimulus type to another.

    Multiple simultaneous stimuli

    Because each response appears at its own modulation frequency, several carriers can be presented at once. The widely described multiple-stimulus technique presents up to four carriers per ear (typically 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz) in both ears together, eight stimuli in total, each modulated at a different rate within the 80 to 100 Hz range (or around 40 Hz). A single EEG channel records all eight responses, and the FFT separates them.

    Figure 6.4c. Illustrative EEG spectrum in a dichotic multiple-stimulus recording: eight responses, one per carrier and ear, each at its own modulation frequency (red: right ear, blue: left ear). The modulation rates are an example arrangement only; each system uses its own set.

    The modulation frequencies are separated for several reasons:

    • Each response needs its own FFT bin. With 1 s epochs the bins are about 1 Hz apart. Rates a few hertz apart put each response in a distinct bin with clean bins between them.
    • Noise estimation. The F-test estimates noise from bins near the signal bin. Those noise bins must not contain other responses, so the bins at the other modulation rates are excluded from the noise estimate, and rates should not be so crowded that too few clean bins remain.
    • Leakage and drift. Even with exact bin alignment, very small timing errors or non-stationarity smear a response into adjacent bins; spacing protects neighbouring responses.
    • Harmonics and interference. Rates are chosen so that harmonics and difference frequencies do not coincide with other rates, and so that they avoid mains frequencies and their harmonics (50, 100, 150 Hz in 50 Hz countries such as India; 60 and 120 Hz where the mains is 60 Hz).

    Carriers are usually spaced an octave or more apart so that the regions they excite on the basilar membrane overlap little. At moderate levels the responses to multiple carriers are similar in amplitude to the responses to each carrier alone, so testing eight at once saves time. At high levels (roughly above 60 to 75 dB SPL per carrier) the excitation patterns spread and the carriers begin to mask or suppress each other, particularly lower carriers affecting responses to higher ones, and responses become smaller than for single stimuli. Some systems therefore reduce the number of simultaneous carriers, or test at high levels one frequency at a time. Presenting different rates to the two ears allows both ears to be recorded in the same channel; at 80 to 100 Hz the binaural interactions are small.

    Try it: ASSR stimulus builder

    Choose a carrier and modulation rate, then add AM depth, FM depth or both (mixed modulation). Watch the envelope in the time waveform and the sidebands spread around the carrier in the spectrum.

    Lower sideband
    Upper sideband
    First sideband level
    FM index β
    Bandwidth (−30 dB)
    Waveform / spectrumEnvelope and fc ± fm markers

    Things to try: with AM only, the first sidebands sit exactly 6 dB below the carrier at 100 % depth and 12 dB below at 50 % depth (m/2 = 0.25). Set AM to zero and FM to 20 %: the envelope becomes flat, but more sideband pairs appear. Choose AM2 and note the extra sidebands at fc ± 2fm.     4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Transducers and levels

    • Air conduction. Insert earphones with foam tips are standard. They provide interaural attenuation of the order of 50 dB or more at many frequencies (less at high levels or with shallow insertion), reduce the need for masking, keep the transducer's magnetic field away from the electrodes and put a delay of roughly 0.8 to 1 ms in the tube, which separates acoustic stimulus from electrical artefact in time. Maximum output with inserts is typically around 110 to 120 dB HL at mid frequencies, somewhat lower at 4000 Hz and depending on the system.
    • Bone conduction. A bone vibrator on the mastoid (or on the temporal bone in infants, held with an elastic band at a controlled force) allows BC ASSR, for example to show a conductive component. The maximum usable level is limited, commonly around 50 to 60 dB HL at 500 Hz and higher at other frequencies, because above that the electromagnetic artefact from the vibrator can produce spurious responses. BC ASSR levels and corrections in infants differ from adults because the infant skull transmits low-frequency vibration more efficiently.
    • Sound field. Loudspeakers can deliver the stimulus to an aided or implanted ear. Aided ASSR has been studied, but hearing-aid processing (compression, noise reduction, frequency lowering) alters modulated stimuli, so aided cortical testing (6.5) or real-ear verification is usually preferred for fitting decisions.

    Calibration

    Steady modulated tones can be calibrated in dB SPL with a sound level meter and coupler, as for audiometric tones (see 4.1). Manufacturers typically express levels in dB HL, using reference values derived from behavioural thresholds for the modulated tones in normally hearing listeners, or in dB nHL for chirp trains. The unit printed on the result (dB HL, dB nHL, dB eHL) must always be checked before comparing with an audiogram.

    Level sequence

    A typical infant protocol starts at a moderate level (for example 50 to 60 dB HL) for all eight stimuli, then moves each carrier down after a detection and up after a no-response, in 10 dB steps, refining to 5 dB near threshold if time permits. Multiple-stimulus systems adjust each carrier independently, so one ear or frequency may be finished while others continue. Test levels, step sizes and stopping rules follow the local protocol and the equipment manual.

    Electrode montage and patient state

    Figure 6.4d. A common single-channel ASSR montage (head seen from above, nose at the top). A midline inverting electrode at the nape records responses from both ears in one channel during dichotic testing; a mastoid inverting electrode is also used. Details of skin preparation, impedance targets and alternatives are in 6.3.

    Impedances are typically kept below about 5 kΩ and balanced within about 2 kΩ, as for the ABR. Keep electrode leads away from the transducer cables and bone vibrator, and braid or twist them together, because the stimulus can couple electromagnetically into the leads.

    Patient state matters more for the ASSR than for the ABR because the test depends on a low noise level for many minutes:

    • Infants under about 4 to 6 months are tested in natural sleep after a feed; older infants and children often need sedation or general anaesthesia according to local policy. The 80 to 100 Hz response is relatively robust to sleep and sedation.
    • Adults lie or recline comfortably with neck supported, eyes closed; for 80 to 100 Hz testing sleep is welcome. For 40 Hz testing the adult must be awake but relaxed, since sleep reduces the 40 Hz response.
    • Muscle tension (neck, jaw) and movement raise the noise level sharply; reposition the head and neck before starting rather than extending the test time.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Step 1: epochs whose length fits the modulation

    The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) of an epoch of N samples taken at sampling rate fs gives values at a set of equally spaced frequencies called bins:

    X[k] = Σn=0..N−1 x[n] e−j2πkn/N fk = k fs / N = k / T Δf = 1 / T where x[n] is the n-th sample, k the bin index, T = N / fs the epoch duration in seconds and Δf the frequency resolution (bin spacing).

    The FFT is simply a fast algorithm for the DFT (Fourier analysis is introduced in Unit 1). A sinusoid that completes a whole number of cycles in the epoch falls exactly on one bin, and all its energy appears there. A sinusoid that does not fit a whole number of cycles leaks into neighbouring bins, which would both reduce the apparent response and contaminate the noise estimate. ASSR systems therefore choose each modulation frequency to be an exact multiple of 1/T.

    Worked example. With fs = 1000 Hz and N = 1024 samples, T = 1.024 s and Δf = 1 / 1.024 = 0.977 Hz. A nominal 90 Hz modulation lies at 90 × 1.024 = 92.16 bins, which is not a whole number. The system instead uses bin 92, that is fm = 92 / 1.024 = 89.84 Hz. Similarly a nominal 80 Hz rate becomes 82 / 1.024 = 80.08 Hz. This is why printed modulation rates on ASSR systems are often unusual numbers with decimals.

    Longer epochs give finer resolution: with T = 2.048 s, Δf = 0.49 Hz. Finer resolution does not by itself improve the signal-to-noise ratio for the same total recording time; it spreads the same noise power over more, narrower bins, while a stable sinusoid stays in one bin, so the per-bin SNR depends on total recording time rather than on how it is cut into epochs.

    Step 2: artefact rejection and averaging

    Each epoch is checked against an amplitude limit (typically a few tens of microvolts peak); epochs containing movement, swallowing or muscle bursts are rejected. Epochs are often grouped into sweeps (for example 16 epochs of about 1 s, giving a sweep of roughly 16 s) for display and for running statistics. Accepted epochs are averaged. Because the response is phase-locked, its amplitude stays the same in the average, while the random noise amplitude falls in proportion to the square root of the number of epochs:

    noise(M) = noise(1) / √M where noise(1) is the noise amplitude in the response bin for a single epoch and M is the number of averaged epochs.

    Worked example. Suppose the noise amplitude in the 90 Hz bin is 320 nV for a single 1.024 s epoch. After 64 epochs (about 66 s) it is 320 / 8 = 40 nV; after 256 epochs (about 262 s, 4.4 min) it is 20 nV; after 1024 epochs (about 17.5 min) it is 10 nV. Halving the noise costs four times the recording time. A near-threshold 80 to 100 Hz response of perhaps 20 to 30 nV therefore needs a residual noise of about 10 nV to be detected reliably, which is why threshold searching takes many minutes per level and why a quiet, sleeping patient matters so much.

    Step 3: amplitude and phase at fm

    From the complex FFT value X[km] at the modulation bin:

    A = (2 / N) √(Re² + Im²) φ = atan2(Im, Re) where A is the peak amplitude of the response sinusoid (in nV after scaling for amplifier gain), and φ its phase relative to the start of the epoch. Re and Im are the real and imaginary parts of X[km].

    The phase carries timing information. Because the modulation is continuous, the phase delay between the stimulus envelope and the response can be converted to an apparent latency; for 80 to 100 Hz responses this is typically around 7 to 10 ms in adults, consistent with brainstem generators, and longer for 40 Hz responses. Apparent latency is ambiguous by whole cycles, so it is used mainly in research.

    Step 4: the F-test (amplitude compared with neighbouring noise)

    Noise in the EEG spectrum varies smoothly with frequency, so the bins close to fm estimate how much noise the signal bin would contain if there were no response. The F-test compares the power in the signal bin with the mean power in L neighbouring noise bins:

    F = As² / [ (1/L) Σi=1..L Ai² ] degrees of freedom: (2, 2L) where As is the amplitude in the signal bin, Ai the amplitudes in the L noise bins (other response bins excluded) and the result is compared with the F distribution with 2 and 2L degrees of freedom.

    Why these degrees of freedom? Each FFT bin has two independent components, the real and the imaginary part. If the EEG in that frequency region is random with a Gaussian distribution, each part is a Gaussian random number with zero mean, and the power (the sum of the two squares) follows a chi-squared distribution with 2 degrees of freedom. The signal bin contributes 2 degrees of freedom; L noise bins contribute 2L. The ratio of two such scaled chi-squared variables follows the F distribution. The p-value is the probability of obtaining an F this large or larger if the signal bin contains only noise.

    A useful property: for 2 numerator degrees of freedom, the p-value has a simple closed form,

    p = (1 + F / L)−L valid for the F distribution with (2, 2L) degrees of freedom.

    Worked example. Take L = 120 noise bins (60 either side of fm), so the degrees of freedom are (2, 240). The critical F for p = 0.05 is 3.03 and for p = 0.01 is 4.69. In amplitude terms the response must exceed the RMS noise amplitude by √3.03 = 1.74 times (4.8 dB) to reach p < 0.05.

    • Response 40 nV, noise RMS amplitude 15 nV: F = (40 / 15)² = 7.11, p = (1 + 7.11/120)−120 ≈ 0.001. Response present.
    • Response 20 nV, same noise: F = (20 / 15)² = 1.78, p ≈ 0.17. No significant response at this stage; continue averaging or record "no response" if the noise is already at the stopping floor.
    Common error"No significant response" is not the same as "no response". If the residual noise is still high (for example 30 nV), a genuine 20 nV response cannot reach significance. A no-response result is meaningful only when the noise has been reduced to the protocol's floor (commonly around 10 nV for 80 to 100 Hz responses) or the maximum test time has been reached. Always report the noise level with a no-response result.

    Step 5: phase coherence and circular statistics

    Phase angles live on a circle: 350° and 10° are only 20° apart, so ordinary means and standard deviations do not apply. Circular statistics treat each epoch's phase θk as a unit vector and average the vectors:

    R = | (1/K) Σk=1..K ejθk | Z = K R² p ≈ e−Z (Rayleigh test, large K) where K is the number of epochs, θk the phase at fm in epoch k, R the phase coherence (0 = random phases, 1 = identical phases) and Z the Rayleigh statistic.

    If phases are random, the unit vectors point in all directions and R is small, of order 1/√K. A real response pulls the phases towards one direction and R grows. The Rayleigh test asks whether the phases are uniformly distributed around the circle. Worked example: for K = 16 epochs, R must exceed √(ln 20 / 16) = √(3.00 / 16) = 0.43 for p < 0.05; for K = 64, R must exceed 0.22. More epochs allow smaller but consistent phase clustering to be detected. A weighted version, the magnitude-squared coherence, weights each epoch's vector by its amplitude.

    Phase coherence uses only the direction of each epoch's vector, so it is less affected by occasional large-amplitude noisy epochs; the F-test uses the amplitude of the average. Both are used; some systems combine them.

    Step 6: Hotelling's T2 (briefly)

    Each epoch's response bin can be treated as a point in a two-dimensional plane (real part, imaginary part). With no response, these points scatter around the origin; with a response, their cloud is shifted away from it. Hotelling's T2 is the multivariate version of the one-sample t-test: it tests whether the mean of this two-dimensional cloud differs from zero, taking the variance and covariance of the two components into account. It can be converted to an F statistic, F = (K − 2) T2 / [2(K − 1)] with (2, K − 2) degrees of freedom, and extended to test several harmonics at once. It is also used for automated detection of cortical responses (see 6.5). The F-test above estimates noise from other frequencies in the same average; T2 estimates noise from the scatter across epochs at the same frequency. They answer the same question from different directions.

    Step 7: multiple testing and stopping rules

    With p < 0.05, one test in twenty will be "significant" by chance when there is no response. In an eight-stimulus recording with no responses, about 8 × 0.05 = 0.4 false detections are expected per assessment of all eight. If the software re-tests the running average every sweep and stops at the first significant result, the false-positive rate rises further. Systems control this with stricter criteria (for example p < 0.01 in some protocols), a requirement for repeated significance, a minimum recording time, or statistical corrections for sequential testing. Stopping rules typically end a recording for a stimulus when a response is detected, when the residual noise reaches a floor, or when a maximum time has elapsed.

    Try it: detecting an ASSR in noise

    A small sinusoidal response at fm = 89.84 Hz is buried in Gaussian EEG noise. Change the response amplitude, the noise level and the number of 1.024 s epochs, and watch the F-test and the phase coherence decide whether a response is present.

    Recording time
    Amplitude at fm
    Noise (RMS of 120 bins)
    F (2, 240)
    p (F-test)
    Phase coherence R
    p (Rayleigh)
    Decision (F-test, p < 0.05)
    Signal bin at fmNoise bins (shaded region) and RMS noiseAmplitude needed for p < 0.05Epoch phases

    Things to try: set the response to 30 nV and the noise to 5 µV, then step the epochs from 8 to 512 and watch the noise floor fall by about half for every fourfold increase (with 5 µV broadband noise the single-epoch noise in one bin is about 2 × 5000 / √1024 ≈ 310 nV, so 512 epochs bring it to about 14 nV). Set the response to 0 nV and press "New noise" repeatedly: roughly one press in twenty will show a false "detected" result, which is exactly what p < 0.05 means. The simulation uses white Gaussian noise; real EEG noise is larger at low frequencies and contains occasional artefacts, so real recordings need longer.

    Recording parameters, noise estimation and test time

    • Sampling rate. Must be well above twice the highest frequency of interest after filtering. Rates of the order of 1 kHz are sufficient for responses up to about 100 Hz and their low harmonics, provided the anti-aliasing filter removes the stimulus frequencies; many systems sample faster and decimate.
    • Epoch length. Chosen so that every modulation frequency completes an integer number of cycles (and often every carrier too). Around 1 s is common, giving roughly 1 Hz bins.
    • Frequency resolution. Δf = 1/T. It must be fine enough that the modulation rates in a multiple-stimulus set occupy separate bins with clean noise bins between them.
    • Artefact rejection. A peak amplitude limit, typically a few tens of microvolts; some systems use noise-weighted averaging instead of hard rejection.
    • Noise estimation. Either from neighbouring bins in the averaged spectrum (F-test approach, reported as residual noise in nV) or from the variability across epochs at the signal frequency (T2 and phase approaches).
    • Test time. Depends on the noise level and how close the stimulus is to threshold. At suprathreshold levels responses are often detected within one or two minutes; near threshold several minutes per level are typical. A full eight-threshold assessment in a sleeping infant often takes in the order of 30 to 60 minutes of recording, varying widely with state and hearing loss.

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    • Interacoustics Eclipse with its ASSR module: the manufacturer describes binaural testing of four frequencies per ear at the same time (eight simultaneous presentations), a choice of 40 Hz or 90 Hz rates, CE-Chirp family stimuli, statistical response detection and correction factors to estimated HL.
    • Grason-Stadler (GSI) Audera Pro: a clinical EP/OAE platform with ASSR among its test modalities.
    • Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP-ASSR: the ASSR module of the SmartEP evoked potential platform.
    • Natus Bio-logic MASTER: historically an important multiple-stimulus ASSR system on the Bio-logic platform, based on the MASTER (multiple auditory steady-state response) technique developed by John and Picton.
    • Vivosonic Integrity V500: an AEP system with an ASSR option; the manufacturer emphasises amplification at the electrode to reduce noise, which may help testing of quiet but awake patients.
    RememberExamples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation. Listing a system here is not an endorsement. Stimulus types, detection algorithms and correction values differ between systems, so thresholds from different systems are not interchangeable without each system's own normative data.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation basics

    From physiological threshold to estimated hearing level

    The ASSR threshold is the lowest stimulus level at which a significant response is detected. It is not the behavioural threshold. The response near threshold is small, and the finite recording time means a response can exist below the level at which it becomes statistically detectable. ASSR thresholds therefore typically lie above behavioural thresholds, and the difference must be corrected to give an estimated hearing level (eHL).

    Corrections are derived from studies in which the same people have both ASSR and behavioural audiometry. Two approaches are used:

    • Fixed correction: subtract a frequency-specific value from the ASSR threshold, for example a larger value at 500 Hz than at 2000 Hz.
    • Regression: fit a line relating behavioural threshold to ASSR threshold, HLest = a × TASSR + b, for each frequency. Where the slope a is greater than 1, the correction shrinks as hearing loss increases.
    HL_est = a × T_ASSR + b where T_ASSR is the ASSR threshold in dB HL, and a and b are regression coefficients that are specific to the frequency, stimulus, equipment, recording protocol and population.

    Worked example with illustrative coefficients only (a = 1.2, b = −24; not for clinical use): an ASSR threshold of 40 dB HL gives 1.2 × 40 − 24 = 24 dB eHL, a correction of 16 dB; an ASSR threshold of 80 dB HL gives 1.2 × 80 − 24 = 72 dB eHL, a correction of only 8 dB. This reproduces the general pattern reported in the literature:

    • In sensorineural hearing loss, ASSR thresholds at 80 to 100 Hz typically lie roughly 10 to 15 dB above behavioural thresholds at 1000 to 4000 Hz, and the difference shrinks as the loss becomes more severe. One explanation is loudness recruitment: in a cochlear loss the response grows steeply above threshold, so it becomes detectable closer to the behavioural threshold.
    • In normal hearing and mild losses the difference is larger (often 20 dB or more), so the ASSR tends to overestimate a mild loss or to suggest a mild loss in a normally hearing ear.
    • At 500 Hz the difference is larger and more variable than at higher frequencies, because low-frequency EEG noise is higher and neural synchrony to low-frequency carriers is poorer.
    • Infant corrections differ from adult corrections because of maturation, ear-canal size (higher SPL in a small canal for the same dial level) and different noise levels.
    Common errorApplying a correction table from one manufacturer, stimulus type or age group to another. Corrections are regression-based and equipment-specific. Use the corrections supplied and validated for the specific system and protocol, and report both the physiological threshold and the estimated HL.

    Comparison: ABR and ASSR

    FeatureABR (click, tone burst, chirp)ASSR
    Response typeTransient waveformSteady-state periodic response
    Analysis domainTime (peaks and latencies)Frequency (amplitude and phase at fm)
    DetectionVisual, with replication; objective aids on some systemsStatistical (F-test, phase coherence, T2)
    StimulusClicks, brief tone bursts, chirps at about 20 to 50/sContinuous AM/FM/MM tones or chirp trains at 40 or 80 to 100 Hz
    Frequency specificityModerate for tone bursts; poor for clicksGood (narrow stimulus spectrum)
    Frequencies per runOne frequency, one earUp to four per ear, both ears
    Maximum levelLimited for brief stimuli (often about 90 to 100 dB nHL)Higher, typically up to about 110 to 120 dB HL
    Neural diagnosisYes: latencies, interpeak intervals, waveform morphology, CM in ANSDNo site-of-lesion information in routine use
    Auditory neuropathyIdentified by absent or abnormal ABR with present CMCannot identify; thresholds may be misleading
    Accuracy in normal and mild hearingTone-burst ABR reasonably accurate with correctionsTends to overestimate thresholds
    Severe to profound lossOften no response at maximum levelCan show residual hearing at high levels (beware artefact)
    Patient stateSleep preferredSleep required for 80 to 100 Hz; awake for 40 Hz
    Skill neededHigh: waveform interpretationLower for detection; high for troubleshooting and interpretation

    In practice the two tests are complementary. Most infant diagnostic protocols include click ABR (both polarities) to exclude auditory neuropathy and assess neural integrity, and use either tone-burst ABR or ASSR for frequency-specific thresholds, often adding ASSR at high levels when tone-burst ABR responses are absent.

    Clinical applications

    • Infants referred from newborn screening. Frequency-specific threshold estimates in each ear during natural sleep, used to confirm hearing loss and to start amplification early. Scenario: a 3-month-old referred bilaterally; click ABR shows normal neural morphology with raised thresholds; multiple-stimulus 90 Hz ASSR gives thresholds of about 60 to 70 dB eHL, sloping to 80 dB eHL at 4000 Hz, on which the first hearing-aid prescription is based.
    • Hearing-aid fitting target estimation. ASSR-estimated audiograms (with the correction clearly stated) feed prescriptive methods such as DSL or NAL for infants, together with real-ear-to-coupler difference measurements. Targets are revised as behavioural audiometry (VRA) becomes possible.
    • Cochlear implant candidacy. Because ASSR can present stimuli at up to about 110 to 120 dB HL, it can show whether there is measurable residual hearing in severe to profound loss, which helps decisions about hearing-aid trial, bimodal fitting or implantation. Responses at very high levels must be checked for artefact.
    • Medico-legal and non-organic hearing loss. In adults with suspected exaggerated thresholds (compensation claims, occupational noise cases) the ASSR gives an objective frequency-specific estimate. The 40 Hz response in awake adults is attractive here because thresholds are close to behavioural ones and the patient does not need to sleep.
    • Difficult-to-test populations. Children and adults with developmental disability, under sedation or general anaesthesia (80 to 100 Hz).
    • Research uses include temporal processing (envelope following responses), binaural processing and aided responses; these are research tools unless validated for clinical decisions.

    Advantages

    • Frequency-specific stimuli with narrow spectra.
    • Objective statistical detection with a known false-positive rate; less dependent on tester experience.
    • High output levels for severe to profound losses.
    • Several frequencies in both ears simultaneously, saving time at moderate levels.
    • Automated level stepping and stopping rules.

    Limitations, errors and troubleshooting

    ProblemCauseWhat to check or do
    Spurious "responses" at high AC levels or at modest BC levels in a patient with no hearingElectromagnetic or acoustic stimulus artefact picked up by electrodes and aliased to fm (see below)Use systems with adequate anti-aliasing and sampling; alternate stimulus polarity where available; route leads away from transducers; treat responses above about 50 dB HL BC or about 95 to 100 dB HL AC with caution; check that responses behave physiologically (phase, growth with level)
    No responses at 40 Hz in an infant or sleeping adultState dependence of the cortical-dominated 40 Hz responseUse 80 to 100 Hz rates for sleeping patients and infants
    Estimated mild loss in a normally hearing earOverestimation near normal thresholds; limited recording timeConfirm with OAE, tone-burst ABR or later behavioural audiometry; report corrections used
    Test not completedPatient woke; high noise from muscle or movementSchedule for natural sleep; reposition; consider sedation per policy; prioritise frequencies and ears
    High residual noise at all frequenciesHigh or unbalanced electrode impedance, mains interference, restless patientRe-prepare skin (6.3), check leads and ground, switch off nearby equipment
    Response at one rate in every recording, even with earphones removedMains harmonic or equipment interference at that frequencyCheck modulation rates avoid mains harmonics; run a no-stimulus or disconnected-transducer control
    Good thresholds but the child does not respond behaviourallyAuditory neuropathy spectrum disorder: ASSR may not reflect behavioural thresholdsAlways include click ABR with both polarities (CM), OAE and acoustic reflexes
    Thresholds raised at low frequencies onlyHigher low-frequency noise, larger 500 Hz correction, middle-ear effusionCheck tympanometry (high-frequency probe in infants), compare with BC
    Discrepant results between systemsDifferent stimuli, algorithms and correctionsUse system-specific norms; do not mix correction tables

    Aliased stimulus artefact: a worked example

    Consider a 1000 Hz carrier modulated at 90 Hz. Its spectrum contains 910, 1000 and 1090 Hz. If some of this stimulus reaches the recording (electromagnetic field from the transducer or bone vibrator, or current in the leads) and the EEG is sampled at 1000 Hz without adequate anti-aliasing filtering, each component is folded to |f − n fs|: 1090 Hz aliases to 1090 − 1000 = 90 Hz, and 910 Hz aliases to 1000 − 910 = 90 Hz. Both sidebands land exactly at the modulation frequency, producing a perfectly phase-locked "response" that passes every statistical test, even though it is not neural. Such artefactual responses were demonstrated for ASSR recordings with bone conduction at modest levels and air conduction at high levels (Small and Stapells, 2004). Modern systems reduce the problem with steep anti-aliasing filters, higher sampling rates and stimulus designs such as alternating polarity, but at the highest levels caution is still needed.

    Interpretation basics

    • Report for each ear and frequency: the physiological threshold, the estimated HL, the correction used, the residual noise and whether a no-response result reached the noise floor or maximum level.
    • Check consistency across frequencies and with other tests (OAE, tympanometry, click ABR). A single isolated response at a high level with no responses at adjacent frequencies may be artefact.
    • For conductive components, compare BC and AC ASSR only within the levels at which the BC artefact is known to be absent for that system.
    • Treat ASSR thresholds as estimates to be refined by behavioural audiometry as soon as the child can perform VRA.

    Current developments

    • Chirp-based ASSR stimuli that compensate for cochlear travel-time delay, aiming at larger responses and shorter test times.
    • Improved detection statistics: use of multiple harmonics, Hotelling-type multivariate tests, noise-weighted averaging and sequential test designs that control false positives when the running average is re-tested.
    • Aided and electrically evoked ASSR in hearing-aid and cochlear implant users, largely in research; stimulus artefact from the device is a key obstacle.
    • Envelope-following responses to speech and to complex modulations, studied as markers of temporal processing and possible hidden hearing loss; research tools at present.
    • Amplifiers placed at or near the electrode and better artefact handling, aimed at testing infants who are awake or only lightly asleep.

    Demonstration

    Equipment: an AEP system with an ASSR module, insert earphones, bone vibrator, disposable electrodes, skin preparation gel, an impedance meter (built into most systems), a volunteer adult and a projector showing the system screen; the two browser demos in this topic.

    1. Show the stimulus builder demo. Build a 1000 Hz carrier with 100 % AM at 90 Hz and point out the sidebands at 910 and 1090 Hz and their level of −6 dB. Add FM to show mixed modulation, then AM2.
    2. Prepare the volunteer with a Cz (or Fz) to nape montage and a forehead ground; show impedance values below about 5 kΩ and balanced (see 6.3).
    3. Run a multiple-stimulus 40 Hz recording with the volunteer awake, eyes closed, at 60 dB HL. Show responses emerging within one or two minutes, and the displayed p-values and residual noise.
    4. Switch to an 80 to 100 Hz protocol at the same level. Students observe that the responses are smaller and take longer to reach significance in an awake adult.
    5. Ask the volunteer to clench the jaw for 20 s. Observe rejected epochs and a rise in residual noise.
    6. Remove the insert earphones from the ears but leave them near the electrode leads, and run at high level. Discuss whether any detected "response" could be artefact. Repeat with the leads moved away and twisted together.
    7. Use the F-test demo with a 0 nV response and repeated noise samples to show the false-positive rate of about 5 %.
    8. Display a sample report and discuss conversion from physiological threshold to eHL with the system's correction table.

    Students should observe: the response sits at fm, not at the carrier; state affects the 40 Hz response; noise falls with the square root of recording time; statistics make the detection decision.

    Practical activity and assessment

    1. In pairs, prepare a partner for ASSR recording and document electrode sites and impedances.
    2. Record 40 Hz ASSR thresholds for 1000 and 4000 Hz in one ear, using 10 dB steps and a stopping rule from the local protocol. Record the residual noise at each level.
    3. Obtain behavioural thresholds for the same ear with the audiometer and compute the ASSR minus behavioural difference.
    4. Using the F-test demo, find the recording time at which a 30 nV response is detected on at least 8 out of 10 noise samples at 5 µV noise. Explain the result with the √M rule.
    5. Deliverable: a two-page report with a labelled montage diagram, a table of levels, p-values and residual noise, the ASSR-behavioural differences, and a paragraph on why ASSR thresholds need system-specific corrections.
