    6.5

    Other electrophysiological tests: ECochG, MLR, CAEP/ALR, P300 and MMN, VEMP, electrically evoked responses

    Supplementary3 hoursPractical P9

    Beyond the ABR and ASSR, the same evoked-potential equipment records responses from the cochlea, the auditory cortex, cognitive processes, the vestibular reflexes and the electrically stimulated auditory nerve. Each test changes one or more of the same few settings (stimulus, electrode sites, filter band, time window, number of sweeps and patient state) to target a different generator. This topic walks through each test in turn using the same eight points as the rest of Unit 6.

    What it is

    A family of evoked-potential tests: electrocochleography (ECochG), the auditory middle latency response (MLR), cortical auditory evoked potentials (CAEP, also called the auditory late response, ALR), the cognitive P300 and mismatch negativity (MMN), vestibular evoked myogenic potentials (cervical cVEMP and ocular oVEMP), and responses to electrical stimulation through a cochlear implant (electrically evoked ABR and the electrically evoked compound action potential, ECAP).

    Why it is required

    They answer questions the ABR cannot: is there endolymphatic hydrops or auditory neuropathy (ECochG); is sound reaching the auditory cortex with a hearing aid (aided CAEP); is the auditory system discriminating sounds (MMN, P300); are the otolith organs and vestibular nerves working (VEMP); is the auditory nerve responding to a cochlear implant (ECAP, EABR).

    How it works

    All the tests use stimulus-locked averaging of a differential recording, as for the ABR (4.4, 6.2). The generator of interest determines the latency window (from under 1 ms for the ECAP to 600 ms for the P300), which in turn sets the filter band, stimulus rate, electrode positions and the patient state required. VEMPs are different in one respect: they are muscle (myogenic) responses, so the recording measures a change in ongoing muscle activity rather than a neural field potential.

    Common framework: latency, filters and rate

    A useful rule links the latency of a response to the settings needed to record it. Early responses are brief and fast-changing, so they contain high frequencies and need a wide filter reaching up to about 1500 to 3000 Hz; they recover quickly, so stimuli can be fast. Late responses are slow waves lasting tens to hundreds of milliseconds, so the useful energy lies below about 30 Hz and the filter can stop there; their generators take a long time to recover (refractoriness), so stimuli must be spaced about a second apart. Cognitive responses also depend on attention, so the patient state becomes part of the protocol.

    Figure 6.5a. Approximate latency ranges of the responses covered in this topic, on a logarithmic time axis (typical adult values; each lab uses its own norms). Teal boxes are auditory neural responses, blue are electrically evoked or cognitive responses, amber is myogenic (vestibular).

    TestMain generatorTypical filter bandTypical stimulus ratePatient state
    ECochGCochlear hair cells (CM, SP), distal auditory nerve (AP)about 5 to 3000 Hz (low high-pass to keep SP)about 7 to 11/sRelaxed or asleep
    MLRThalamus and primary auditory cortexabout 10 to 200 Hzbelow about 11/sAwake and relaxed in adults; sleep stage matters in children
    CAEP (P1-N1-P2)Auditory cortex (supratemporal plane)about 1 to 30 Hzabout 0.5 to 1/sAwake, quiet, alert (watching silent video)
    P300Distributed cortical network (temporo-parietal and frontal)about 0.1 to 30 Hzabout 1/sAwake, attending, task
    MMNAuditory cortex and frontal regionsabout 0.1 to 30 Hzabout 1 to 2/sAwake, attention elsewhere
    cVEMP / oVEMPSaccule and inferior vestibular nerve / utricle and superior vestibular nerve, via reflex to muscleabout 10 to 1000 Hz (varies)about 5/sTonic muscle contraction (neck) or upward gaze
    EABR / ECAPBrainstem / auditory nerve (electrical stimulation)EABR about 100 to 3000 Hz; ECAP wide bandEABR about 20 to 50/sAnaesthesia, asleep or awake

    Values are typical and hedged; follow the local protocol and the equipment manual. Multichannel EEG recording for cortical and cognitive responses is described in 4.5; electrode sites and impedance practice in 6.3.

    Electrocochleography (ECochG)

    1. Basic working principle

    ECochG records the electrical activity of the cochlea and auditory nerve in the first few milliseconds after a sound, using an electrode placed as close as possible to the cochlea. Three components are measured:

    • Cochlear microphonic (CM): an alternating voltage that follows the waveform of the stimulus, generated mainly by receptor currents in the outer hair cells. It reverses when the stimulus polarity reverses.
    • Summating potential (SP): a direct-current shift lasting for the duration of the stimulus, arising from asymmetric (non-linear) hair-cell transduction, with contributions from inner and outer hair cells. It is usually negative when recorded from the ear canal or promontory.
    • Compound action potential (AP): the summed synchronous firing of auditory nerve fibres, a negative peak N1 (the same event as ABR wave I) at about 1.5 ms for a high-level click, followed by a smaller N2.

    Because these are near-field potentials, amplitude falls rapidly with distance: with a transtympanic needle on the promontory the AP may be tens of microvolts; with an ear-canal electrode it is typically below one to a few microvolts. The closer the electrode, the fewer sweeps are needed and the more reliable the SP measurement.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5b. ECochG signal flow. Averaging responses to rarefaction (R) and condensation (C) stimuli separately allows the stimulus-following CM to be separated from the SP and AP.

    The hardware is an ordinary AEP system with a special electrode and adjusted filters. Electrode options, from least to most invasive: extratympanic ear-canal electrodes (for example a gold-foil-covered foam insert tip, or a small electrode against the canal wall), tympanic electrodes whose soft conductive tip rests on the tympanic membrane, and transtympanic needle electrodes passed through the tympanic membrane to the promontory by an otologist, usually under local or general anaesthesia. The inverting electrode is commonly on the contralateral mastoid or earlobe and the ground on the forehead, although montages vary between clinics. The high-pass filter must be low (a few hertz, or DC coupling) so that the steady SP is not removed by the filter.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli: clicks (100 µs), usually at 80 to 95 dB nHL, in alternating polarity to cancel the CM and stimulus artefact when measuring SP and AP; tone bursts (commonly 500 to 2000 Hz, with a plateau of several milliseconds) to show the SP as a sustained shift during the tone; and separate single-polarity runs when the CM is of interest. Generated signals: CM (follows the stimulus, microvolts or less at the ear canal), SP (DC shift, typically a fraction of a microvolt at the ear canal), AP N1 at about 1.5 ms.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    The patient lies relaxed or asleep. The ear canal is cleaned and, for tympanic electrodes, the tip is moistened with conductive gel and advanced under otoscopic control until it touches the tympanic membrane; the patient usually reports a scratchy sound or mild discomfort. The insert earphone foam or a sealing plug holds the electrode lead in place. Stimuli are delivered at about 7 to 11 per second, with a 10 ms analysis window, and 500 to 2000 sweeps depending on electrode type (more for canal electrodes).

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Signals are averaged and replicated. The SP is measured from the baseline (before the stimulus) to the shoulder on the leading edge of the AP; the AP from the baseline to the N1 trough. Their ratio is computed:

    SP/AP ratio = SP amplitude / AP amplitude CM = (R − C) / 2 neural + SP = (R + C) / 2 where R and C are the averaged responses to rarefaction and condensation stimuli. The subtraction removes polarity-independent components; the addition removes polarity-dependent ones (CM and most stimulus artefact).

    Worked example: SP = 0.35 µV and AP = 1.20 µV give SP/AP = 0.29. If the SP were 0.65 µV with the same AP, the ratio would be 0.54, above a typical extratympanic click upper limit of about 0.4 to 0.5 used by many clinics. Some clinics also compute an SP/AP area ratio over the waveform, which is reported to improve sensitivity for Menière's disease.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Stimulus100 µs click, alternating; tone bursts 500 to 2000 HzAlternating cancels CM for SP/AP measurement
    Levelabout 80 to 95 dB nHLSP is small; high levels needed
    Rateabout 7 to 11/sFaster rates reduce AP more than SP
    Filterabout 5 Hz (or DC) to 3000 HzLow high-pass preserves the DC-like SP
    Analysis windowabout 10 msCovers SP, AP N1 and N2
    Sweepsabout 500 to 2000, replicatedMore for canal electrodes
    AP N1 latency (high-level click)about 1.3 to 1.8 msEquals ABR wave I
    AP amplitudecanal: about 0.5 to a few µV; TT: tens of µVDepends strongly on electrode distance
    SP/AP ratio upper limit (click)about 0.4 to 0.5 (extratympanic, clinic norms)Above this suggests a hydrops pattern

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    ECochG is usually a protocol on a general AEP system, for example Interacoustics Eclipse, GSI Audera Pro, Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro or Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP, used with suitable ear-canal, tympanic or transtympanic electrodes. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • Menière's disease and endolymphatic hydrops: an enlarged SP relative to the AP (raised SP/AP ratio) is associated with hydrops. Sensitivity is moderate and varies with disease stage and fluctuation, so a normal ratio does not exclude Menière's disease. A raised SP/AP ratio has also been reported in superior semicircular canal dehiscence.
    • Auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder (ANSD): a present (often large) CM with absent or abnormal AP and ABR. Separate rarefaction and condensation runs show the CM inverting with polarity, which distinguishes it from a neural response. A run with the sound tube clamped confirms that an inverting response is not electrical stimulus artefact.
    • Enhancing wave I: when ABR wave I is poorly formed (hearing loss, noise), an ear-canal electrode enlarges it so that I to V intervals can be measured.
    • Intraoperative monitoring: of cochlear and auditory nerve function during surgery near the nerve, and intracochlear ECochG through a cochlear implant electrode during insertion (a developing application).

    Limitations: small SP at the ear canal and large inter-subject variability; electrode placement affects amplitude and ratio; transtympanic recording is invasive and needs a medical practitioner; results must be interpreted with clinic norms for the specific electrode and stimulus.

    Auditory middle latency response (MLR or AMLR)

    1. Basic working principle

    The MLR is a sequence of waves between roughly 12 and 60 ms after a click or tone burst, following the ABR. Its main components are labelled by polarity and order: Na (negative, about 15 to 20 ms), Pa (positive, about 25 to 35 ms), Nb (about 35 to 45 ms) and Pb (about 50 to 60 ms; Pb corresponds to the P1 or P50 of the cortical response). Na is thought to arise from subcortical structures (thalamus and upper midbrain), Pa mainly from the primary auditory cortex in Heschl's gyrus with thalamocortical contributions. Because it reflects the thalamocortical auditory pathway, the MLR tests the part of the system above the brainstem, and its successive waves spaced about 25 ms apart underlie the 40 Hz ASSR (6.4).

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5c. MLR recording chain. A vertex channel measures the overall response; channels over each hemisphere allow an "electrode effect" (asymmetry between hemispheres) to be assessed.

    The same AEP platform is used with a longer window (commonly 80 to 100 ms), lower filter band and slower stimulus rate than for the ABR. Two or more channels are useful: a vertex channel (Cz) and hemispheric channels (C3 and C4, or temporal sites T7/T8, previously called T3/T4), each referred to the earlobe or mastoid. Some protocols use a non-cephalic reference to avoid the post-auricular muscle response.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli are clicks or tone bursts (for frequency-specific MLR, tone bursts of 500 to 2000 Hz with rise and fall times of a few milliseconds) at moderate to high levels. Generated signals: the Na-Pa-Nb-Pb complex with amplitudes of the order of 0.5 to 2 µV; the ABR appears as a small ripple in the first 10 ms if the filter is wide enough.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Adults lie awake and relaxed with the neck supported, or in light sleep; deep sleep and many sedative and anaesthetic agents reduce the Pa component. Rate is kept below about 11 per second (for example 7.1/s) because the cortical generators need time to recover, and slower still (about 1 to 4 per second) in young children, whose Pa is small and strongly affected by sleep stage. Insert earphones are preferred. The patient should keep jaw, neck and scalp muscles relaxed.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Responses are averaged and replicated in each channel. Latencies of Na and Pa and the Na-Pa peak-to-peak amplitude are measured. The electrode effect compares Pa amplitude over the two hemispheres: a markedly smaller Pa over one hemisphere, for both ears, suggests a lesion of the auditory cortex or thalamocortical pathway on that side. The ear effect compares responses to left and right ear stimulation. The post-auricular muscle (PAM) reflex, a large myogenic response at about 12 to 15 ms from the muscle behind the ear, can contaminate recordings with a mastoid reference; it grows with neck tension and is reduced by relaxation, earlobe or non-cephalic references and by checking replication.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Stimulusclick or tone burst, about 60 to 70 dB nHL for neurodiagnosisRobust response without excessive PAM
    Ratebelow about 11/s (adults); slower in childrenAvoids refractory reduction of Pa
    Filterabout 10 to 200 Hz (some use a higher upper limit)Passes the 20 to 40 Hz energy of the MLR
    Windowabout 80 to 100 msCovers Na to Pb
    Sweepsabout 500 to 1000, replicatedResponse of about 1 µV
    Latencies (adult)Na 15 to 20 ms; Pa 25 to 35 ms; Nb 35 to 45 ms; Pb 50 to 60 msDelay suggests thalamocortical dysfunction
    Na-Pa amplitudeabout 0.5 to 2 µVCompare hemispheres rather than use absolute limits

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    MLR is a standard protocol on AEP platforms such as Interacoustics Eclipse, GSI Audera Pro, Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro and Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP; multichannel EEG systems are used in research. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • Assessment of the thalamocortical auditory pathway in suspected central auditory processing disorder and neurological lesions (electrode effect), as part of a test battery.
    • Low-frequency threshold estimation in awake adults, historically, where tone-burst ABR at 500 Hz is weak.
    • Monitoring of depth of anaesthesia (research and some specialised monitors), because Pa is suppressed by anaesthetics.
    • Cochlear implant research: electrically evoked MLR.

    Limitations: state dependence and poor reliability in young children; PAM and other muscle artefacts; wide normal variability of amplitude, so the test relies on comparisons between hemispheres and ears rather than absolute values.

    Cortical auditory evoked potentials (CAEP / ALR): P1-N1-P2

    1. Basic working principle

    The cortical auditory evoked potential, also called the auditory late response (ALR) or slow vertex response, is the obligatory response of the auditory cortex to the onset of a sound (and to changes within it). In adults it consists of P1 (about 50 to 80 ms), N1 (about 80 to 150 ms, commonly near 100 ms) and P2 (about 150 to 250 ms), with a peak-to-peak N1-P2 amplitude of several microvolts. It is generated in the supratemporal plane of the auditory cortex with contributions from association areas. "Obligatory" means it occurs whenever the sound is detected by the cortex, without the patient having to perform a task, although attention and arousal change its size.

    The CAEP changes with maturation. In infants and young children it is dominated by a large positive peak P1 with a latency of up to about 200 to 300 ms in the first months, decreasing through childhood; the adult P1-N1-P2 pattern emerges gradually and is typically mature by the teenage years. The P1 latency is therefore used as a biomarker of auditory cortical maturation, for example after cochlear implantation.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5d. Aided CAEP set-up. The stimulus passes through the patient's own hearing aid (or implant processor), so the response shows whether the aided sound reaches the auditory cortex.

    Hardware is an AEP or EEG system able to record slow potentials (high-pass around 0.1 to 1 Hz, long windows) plus, for aided testing, a calibrated sound-field loudspeaker. A typical single-channel montage is Cz (non-inverting) to mastoid or earlobe (inverting) with a forehead ground; multichannel recordings add Fz, Pz and lateral sites (see 4.5). An eye-movement (EOG) channel above or beside the eye helps to reject blink artefacts, which are large in this frequency band.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli are longer than for the ABR: tone bursts of about 40 to 100 ms with rise and fall times of about 10 ms, or natural speech tokens. For aided testing, short speech sounds with different spectral emphasis are widely used, for example /m/ (low-frequency), /g/ (mid-frequency) and /t/ (high-frequency), presented at levels representing soft, average and loud speech (for example 55, 65 and 75 dB SPL). The generated signal is the P1-N1-P2 complex, a slow wave of several microvolts.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    The patient must be awake: sleep changes the CAEP substantially. Children sit on a parent's lap or in a chair watching a silent video or quiet toys, which keeps them still and alert without attending to the sounds. Stimuli are presented slowly, with an onset-to-onset interval of about 1 to 2 s, because the N1 generator has a long refractory period. For unaided threshold estimation, stimuli are delivered by insert earphones; for aided testing the hearing aid is fitted and set to the user's everyday programme, and the loudspeaker is placed about 1 m in front of the head at ear level. Level is calibrated at the listening position without the patient present.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Epochs from about 100 ms before to 500 ms (or more) after onset are baseline-corrected, checked against a rejection limit (for example about ±100 to 150 µV, or an EOG criterion), and averaged, typically 50 to 200 accepted epochs per condition in two replications. Detection can be visual (by an experienced examiner, with replication) or statistical. One statistical approach divides the post-stimulus window into time bins, takes the mean voltage in each bin for every epoch, and applies Hotelling's T2 test to ask whether the multivariate mean of these bin values differs from zero; this approach was used in an aided CAEP system (HEARLab) developed by the National Acoustic Laboratories. Latencies and amplitudes of P1, N1 and P2 are measured when a response is present.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Stimulitone bursts 40 to 100 ms; speech tokens such as /m/, /g/, /t/Frequency and speech-feature specific audibility
    Onset-to-onset intervalabout 1 to 2 sAllows N1 recovery; larger response
    Aided presentation levelsfor example 55, 65, 75 dB SPL at 1 m, 0°Soft, average and loud speech audibility
    Filterabout 1 to 30 Hz (0.1 Hz high-pass for research)Passes slow cortical waves, reduces muscle noise
    Windowabout −100 to 500 ms (to 700 ms in infants)Includes baseline and P2 (or infant P1)
    Accepted epochsabout 50 to 200 per conditionResponse is several µV
    Adult latenciesP1 50 to 80 ms; N1 80 to 150 ms; P2 150 to 250 msMature cortical response
    N1-P2 amplitude (adult)about 3 to 10 µVVaries with level, attention, arousal
    Infant P1 latencyup to about 200 to 300 ms, decreasing with ageMaturation marker

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Late responses are protocols on AEP platforms such as Interacoustics Eclipse, GSI Audera Pro, Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro and Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP. HEARLab, developed by the National Acoustic Laboratories (Australia) for aided cortical testing, has been used clinically in several countries. Research laboratories use multichannel EEG systems. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • Aided cortical testing in infants and young children. Present responses to /m/, /g/ and /t/ at conversational levels show that aided speech sounds reach the auditory cortex. This supports hearing-aid fitting and counselling for infants who cannot yet give reliable behavioural responses, and can support cochlear implant referral when responses remain absent despite appropriately verified amplification.
    • Auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder. In ANSD the ABR is absent or grossly abnormal, but cortical responses may be present because they depend less on precise neural synchrony; their presence has been associated with better speech perception outcomes.
    • Threshold estimation in awake adults (cortical electric response audiometry), for example in medico-legal and non-organic loss assessment. Thresholds are typically within about 10 to 15 dB of behavioural thresholds in cooperative adults.
    • Cortical maturation after cochlear implantation. P1 latency falls towards age norms after implantation; studies by Sharma and colleagues associated early implantation (in roughly the first three and a half years) with P1 latencies reaching the normal range.

    Limitations and interpretation: the child must be awake and settled; an absent response does not prove that a sound is inaudible, because of noise, state and maturation; hearing-aid processing (compression attack time, noise reduction and the aid's own noise floor) changes the onset of short stimuli and the signal-to-noise ratio at the output, so the aided CAEP can reflect the signal-to-noise ratio more than the absolute level; cochlear implant stimulation produces electrical artefacts that must be removed. Always interpret CAEP results with real-ear verification and behavioural observation.

    Cognitive potentials: P300 and mismatch negativity (MMN)

    1. Basic working principle

    Both tests use an oddball paradigm: a frequent "standard" sound is interrupted at random by a rare "deviant" sound that differs in frequency, duration, intensity or phonetic content. The brain responds to the deviant with components that are absent or smaller for the standard.

    • P300 (P3b). When the listener actively attends to the sounds and detects the rare target (for example by counting targets or pressing a button), a large positive wave appears at about 250 to 400 ms, largest over the parietal midline (Pz). It reflects the conscious evaluation of a task-relevant event and is generated by a distributed network including temporo-parietal and frontal regions. A related frontal component, the P3a, occurs to unexpected novel sounds even without a task.
    • Mismatch negativity (MMN). When the listener ignores the sounds (reading or watching a silent video), the deviant still produces an extra negativity compared with the standard, peaking at about 100 to 250 ms, largest at fronto-central sites (Fz). It is seen best in the difference wave: the average response to deviants minus the average response to standards. The MMN is thought to reflect an automatic comparison between the incoming sound and a memory trace of the regularity set up by the standards, with generators in the auditory cortex and frontal regions. With a nose reference, the MMN inverts in polarity at the mastoids, which supports an auditory-cortex source.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5e. Oddball paradigm and processing. The stimulus computer sends an event code with every sound so that the EEG epochs can be sorted into standard and deviant averages.

    The hardware is an AEP system with a long-latency protocol and an oddball stimulus option, or a multichannel EEG system with stimulus presentation software that sends event codes (triggers) at each sound onset. The event code identifies the stimulus type, so the software can sort and average epochs separately. Electrode sites include Fz, Cz and Pz, with mastoid references and an EOG channel; research recordings often use 32 to 64 or more channels (see 4.5).

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli are tones or speech sounds of about 50 to 100 ms. Typical P300 contrasts are easy to detect (for example 1000 Hz standards and 2000 Hz targets). Typical MMN contrasts are smaller: frequency deviants of about 5 to 10 %, duration deviants, or speech contrasts such as /da/ versus /ga/, because the MMN's size and latency index how discriminable the contrast is. Generated signals: the P300 is large (about 5 to 20 µV); the MMN is small (about 0.5 to 5 µV) and lies on top of the obligatory N1-P2 response.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Sounds are presented through inserts or loudspeakers at a comfortable level (for example 60 to 75 dB SPL), with deviant probability about 10 to 20 % in a pseudo-random order (often with at least one or two standards between deviants). For the P300 the patient counts or presses a button for targets, and the stimulus-onset interval is about 1 to 2 s. For the MMN the patient ignores the sounds, the interval is shorter (about 0.5 to 1 s), and many more trials are needed: typically at least 100 to 200 accepted deviants.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Epochs are sorted, artefact-rejected (with EOG correction or rejection) and averaged per stimulus type. The P300 is measured as the largest positive peak between about 250 and 500 ms in the target average at Pz. The MMN is measured in the difference wave (deviant minus standard) as the most negative peak or mean amplitude in a window of roughly 100 to 250 ms at Fz. Because deviants are also physically different from standards, part of the difference can come from obligatory N1 differences; better designs compare the deviant with the same sound presented as a standard in another block ("identity MMN") or reverse roles between blocks. Significance can be tested with permutation methods or t-tests on mean amplitudes, but individual MMNs are often hard to confirm.

    MMN(t) = avg_deviant(t) − avg_standard(t) residual noise ≈ √(σd²/Nd + σs²/Ns) where σd and σs are the single-trial noise levels and Nd, Ns the numbers of accepted deviant and standard epochs; the second line is the residual noise of the difference wave.

    Worked example: with single-trial noise of 10 µV, 150 deviants and 850 standards, the residual noise of the difference wave is √(100/150 + 100/850) = √(0.667 + 0.118) = √0.785 = 0.89 µV. A 2 µV MMN would be only about twice the residual noise, which shows why the MMN is hard to detect reliably in a single person and why the number of deviants, not standards, limits the test.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Deviant probabilityabout 10 to 20 %Rarity creates the response
    Stimulus-onset intervalP300 about 1 to 2 s; MMN about 0.5 to 1 sBalance between recovery and test time
    Filterabout 0.1 to 30 HzSlow potentials
    Windowabout −100 to 600 ms (P300); −100 to 400 ms (MMN)Includes baseline and component
    Accepted trialsP300: 30 to 60 targets; MMN: 100 to 200 or more deviantsMMN needs many more trials
    P300 latency (young adult)about 250 to 400 ms, typically near 300 msIncreases with age and cognitive decline
    P300 amplitudeabout 5 to 20 µV at PzSmaller with inattention, task difficulty
    MMN latency / amplitudeabout 100 to 250 ms / 0.5 to 5 µV at FzLarger and earlier for easier contrasts

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Clinical AEP platforms such as Interacoustics Eclipse, GSI Audera Pro, Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro and Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP offer long-latency and oddball protocols; research uses multichannel EEG systems with dedicated stimulus software. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • P300: cognitive evaluation of auditory processing, attention and memory; changes with ageing, dementia and some neurological and psychiatric conditions; used in research on central auditory processing disorder and on outcomes with hearing aids and implants.
    • MMN: objective index of auditory discrimination that does not need attention or a behavioural response, attractive for infants, people with limited cooperation and coma research; studied for speech-contrast discrimination in hearing-aid and implant users.
    • Advantages: they assess processing beyond detection; the MMN needs no task.
    • Limitations: large variability between individuals; the MMN is small and often not reliably identifiable in a single person, so most validated findings are group results; both depend on arousal and medication; the P300 needs cooperation. These are best treated as research or supplementary tools rather than stand-alone diagnostic tests.

    Vestibular evoked myogenic potentials (VEMP): cVEMP and oVEMP

    1. Basic working principle

    The otolith organs (saccule and utricle) are sensitive to loud sound and to bone vibration, as well as to linear acceleration. A loud, brief sound or vibration activates otolith afferents, which trigger short-latency reflexes to muscles. The VEMP records the resulting change in muscle activity with surface electrodes.

    • Cervical VEMP (cVEMP): recorded from the sternocleidomastoid (SCM) muscle on the same side as the stimulated ear. The pathway is mainly saccule, inferior vestibular nerve, vestibular nuclei, medial vestibulospinal tract, accessory nerve nucleus, SCM. The response is a brief inhibition of the ongoing muscle contraction, seen in the average as a biphasic wave p13-n23 (positive at about 13 ms, negative at about 23 ms). Because it is a modulation of ongoing activity, there is no response if the muscle is relaxed.
    • Ocular VEMP (oVEMP): recorded from the inferior oblique muscle beneath the eye opposite to the stimulated ear. The pathway is mainly utricle, superior vestibular nerve, vestibular nuclei, crossed vestibulo-ocular pathway, oculomotor nucleus, inferior oblique. The response is a brief excitation, the n10 (negative at about 10 ms), followed by p15. The patient looks upwards to bring the inferior oblique close to the electrode and to activate it.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5f. VEMP recording chain. The cVEMP depends on the level of tonic muscle contraction, so the system measures the rectified EMG and shows it to the patient and examiner.

    Electrode sites:

    • cVEMP: non-inverting electrode over the upper third or middle of the belly of the SCM on the stimulated side; inverting electrode on the sternoclavicular junction or upper sternum; ground on the forehead (or another neutral site). Some clinics record both SCMs simultaneously.
    • oVEMP: active electrode on the skin just below the lower eyelid, centred beneath the pupil, on the side opposite the stimulated ear; reference about 1 to 2 cm below it (or on the chin, depending on the protocol); ground on the forehead or sternum.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Air-conducted stimuli: short 500 Hz tone bursts (the otoliths respond best to low frequencies, roughly 400 to 1000 Hz), for example with 2 ms rise, 2 ms plateau and 2 ms fall, or clicks, at high levels (typically about 95 to 100 dB nHL, which corresponds roughly to 125 to 135 dB peak-equivalent SPL). Bone-conducted stimuli: 500 Hz tone bursts through a powerful bone vibrator, a mini-shaker, or a reflex hammer tap at the forehead (Fz) or mastoid; bone conduction is useful when a conductive hearing loss would block air-conducted stimuli. Generated signals: the cVEMP p13-n23 is large (tens to hundreds of microvolts, depending on muscle contraction); the oVEMP n10 is small (a few microvolts with air conduction, larger with bone conduction).

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    For cVEMP the SCM must be contracted steadily during the recording. The patient either turns the head away from the stimulated ear while seated, or lies supine and lifts the head (with or without turning), which contracts both SCMs. The examiner watches an EMG meter and asks the patient to maintain the target level; runs are short (for example 100 to 200 stimuli at about 5 per second) with rests between, because the position is tiring. For oVEMP the patient sits or lies and looks upwards, typically about 20 to 30° above horizontal, at a fixed target, keeping the head still. Levels are high, so the total sound exposure should be kept low: use the minimum number of runs, avoid unnecessary repetition, check for conductive loss first (air-conducted VEMPs are reduced or absent with an air-bone gap) and follow local safety guidance.

    5. How the response is detected and processed: EMG normalisation

    The average is formed in a window of about −10 to 50 ms. Peaks p13 and n23 (cVEMP) or n10 and p15 (oVEMP) are identified and replicated. The cVEMP amplitude increases roughly in proportion to the background muscle activity, so a weak contraction on one side can mimic a weak vestibular response. EMG normalisation corrects for this: the system computes the mean rectified EMG in a pre-stimulus window, and the corrected amplitude is the raw peak-to-peak amplitude divided by that value. Some systems instead accept only sweeps with EMG inside a target range, or show the EMG level so the examiner can match both sides.

    A_norm = A_p13-n23 / EMG_mean-rectified AR (%) = 100 × (A_larger − A_smaller) / (A_larger + A_smaller) where A is the peak-to-peak amplitude (raw or normalised) on each side and AR the asymmetry ratio.

    Worked example: left raw cVEMP 150 µV with mean rectified EMG 100 µV (normalised 1.50); right raw 90 µV with EMG 60 µV (normalised 1.50). Raw AR = 100 × (150 − 90) / (150 + 90) = 25 %, but the normalised AR is 0 %: the apparent asymmetry was due to weaker contraction on the right. If instead the right had been 90 µV with 100 µV EMG (normalised 0.90), the normalised AR would be 100 × (1.50 − 0.90) / 2.40 = 25 %. Upper limits of AR are commonly around 30 to 40 %, depending on the clinic's norms.

    The VEMP threshold (lowest level with a replicable response) is also useful: it is abnormally low in superior semicircular canal dehiscence (a third-window lesion), and the oVEMP amplitude is often markedly enlarged in that condition.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Stimulus500 Hz tone burst (for example 2-2-2 ms) or click; BC tone burst or tapOtolith sensitivity is best at low frequencies
    AC levelabout 95 to 100 dB nHL (about 125 to 135 dB peSPL)High levels needed; limit exposure
    Rateabout 5/sFaster rates reduce amplitude
    Filterabout 10 to 1000 Hz (oVEMP sometimes 1 to 1000 Hz)Passes the myogenic wave
    Windowabout −10 to 50 msIncludes pre-stimulus EMG window
    Sweepsabout 100 to 200 per run, replicatedLarge cVEMP needs few sweeps
    cVEMP latenciesp13 about 11 to 15 ms; n23 about 20 to 26 msProlonged latency suggests central (for example brainstem) involvement
    oVEMP latenciesn10 about 9 to 12 ms; p15 about 14 to 17 msUtricular and superior nerve pathway
    Asymmetry ratio upper limitabout 30 to 40 %Above suggests unilateral loss (or enhancement) of otolith function
    Tonic EMG targetsystem and protocol specificKeeps amplitude comparable between sides

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    VEMP protocols with EMG monitoring are available on AEP platforms such as Interacoustics Eclipse, GSI Audera Pro and Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro; bone-conducted VEMP needs a vibrator capable of high force output. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • Superior semicircular canal dehiscence: low VEMP thresholds and enlarged oVEMP amplitudes.
    • Vestibular neuritis: absent oVEMP with preserved cVEMP suggests superior vestibular nerve involvement; the reverse suggests inferior nerve involvement.
    • Menière's disease: reduced or absent cVEMP, or altered frequency tuning, in some patients.
    • Vestibular schwannoma and central lesions: reduced responses or prolonged latencies.
    • Children with sensorineural hearing loss or cochlear implant candidates: assessment of otolith function.

    Advantages: fast, non-invasive, side-specific assessment of the otolith organs, which caloric and head-impulse tests (canal tests) do not assess. Limitations: dependence on muscle contraction (use normalisation); reduction with age and with conductive loss; high sound levels; wide normal ranges. Interpret VEMPs within a vestibular test battery (see 6.6 for VNG and vHIT).

    Electrically evoked responses: EABR and ECAP (neural response telemetry)

    1. Basic working principle

    When the auditory nerve is stimulated electrically, through a cochlear implant electrode or a needle on the promontory or round window, the nerve fibres fire directly without hair-cell transduction or synaptic delay. Two responses are recorded:

    • Electrically evoked compound action potential (ECAP): the summed response of the auditory nerve fibres near the stimulating electrode, recorded from a neighbouring intracochlear electrode of the implant itself. It has a negative peak N1 at about 0.2 to 0.4 ms and a positive peak P2 at about 0.5 to 0.8 ms. Implant manufacturers use their own names for ECAP recording systems: Neural Response Telemetry (NRT, Cochlear), Neural Response Imaging (NRI, Advanced Bionics) and Auditory nerve Response Telemetry (ART, MED-EL).
    • Electrically evoked ABR (EABR): the brainstem response to electrical stimulation, recorded with scalp electrodes as for the acoustic ABR. Waves are labelled eII, eIII and eV (wave eI is usually hidden in the stimulus artefact). Because the cochlear delays are bypassed, latencies are about 1 to 1.5 ms shorter than for acoustic clicks: eV typically about 3.5 to 4.5 ms.

    The cochlear implant itself, its electrode array, stimulation strategies and the telemetry link are described in 3.6.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.5g. ECAP recording uses the implant's own electrodes and telemetry; EABR adds an evoked potential system with scalp electrodes, triggered by the implant interface.

    For ECAP, the clinical programming software instructs the implant to deliver a stimulus on one electrode and to record the voltage on another electrode a short distance along the array (for example two electrodes away), using a recording amplifier inside the implant. The digitised recording is sent back across the skin by the radio-frequency link (back-telemetry). No scalp electrodes or separate amplifier are needed. For EABR, the implant (or a promontory stimulator) delivers the stimulus and sends a trigger pulse to a standard evoked potential system, which records from scalp electrodes; the non-inverting electrode is commonly Cz and the inverting electrode is placed on the side away from the implant (contralateral mastoid or neck) to reduce the artefact.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli are charge-balanced biphasic current pulses, typically with phase durations of about 25 to 100 µs, at current levels expressed in the manufacturer's clinical units (which map to microamperes). ECAP measurements use single pulses at low repetition rates (tens of pulses per second) and are repeated at several levels to form an amplitude growth function. EABR uses pulses at about 20 to 50 per second. Generated signals: ECAP N1-P2 amplitudes from tens of microvolts up to about a millivolt (large because the recording electrode is inside the cochlea); EABR wave eV amplitude typically a fraction of a microvolt to about 1 µV.

    4. How the stimulus is presented to the patient

    Intraoperatively, after electrode insertion, the audiologist measures impedances and ECAPs on selected electrodes while the patient is under general anaesthesia; EABR may also be recorded intraoperatively. Postoperatively, ECAPs can be measured with the patient awake and sitting quietly; stimuli are presented at levels up to, but not beyond, comfortable loudness, starting low and increasing. For an awake EABR, the patient should be relaxed or asleep. Electrical stimuli are delivered only through the manufacturer's clinical software, within the charge limits built into the system.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The main difficulty is stimulus artefact: the stimulating current produces a voltage at the recording electrode far larger than the neural response, and it overlaps the response in time. Techniques include:

    • Forward-masking subtraction: a masker pulse puts the nearby fibres into their refractory period so that a following probe pulse evokes artefact only; recordings of probe alone, masker plus probe, masker alone and a no-stimulus baseline are combined (A − B + C − D) so that artefacts cancel and the neural response remains.
    • Alternating polarity: averaging responses to cathodic-first and anodic-first pulses reduces the artefact, which inverts with polarity.
    • Template or model subtraction, recording delay and amplifier gain settings, and blanking of the amplifier during the pulse.

    For EABR, a high sampling rate, blanking and alternating polarity help; the artefact often obscures wave eI and sometimes eII. The ECAP threshold (the lowest level with a visible N1-P2, or a value extrapolated from the amplitude growth function) is determined for several electrodes. In many recipients ECAP thresholds fall between the behavioural threshold (T) and comfort (C or M) levels used in the map, but the correlation for individual electrodes is only moderate.

    6. Important technical specifications and parameters

    ParameterTypical valueClinical meaning
    Pulsebiphasic, about 25 to 100 µs per phaseCharge per phase sets the neural response
    Recording electrode offsetabout 2 electrodes from the stimulating electrode (varies)Close enough for a large response, far enough to reduce artefact
    ECAP N1 / P2 latencyabout 0.2 to 0.4 ms / 0.5 to 0.8 msVery early: direct nerve activation
    ECAP amplitudetens of µV to about 1 mVGrowth with current reflects neural survival and electrode position
    EABR rateabout 20 to 50/sAs for acoustic ABR
    EABR filterabout 100 to 3000 HzBrainstem response band
    EABR eV latencyabout 3.5 to 4.5 msShorter than acoustic wave V
    SweepsECAP: tens to about 100 per level; EABR: about 500 to 2000ECAP is large; EABR is small and artefact-prone

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    ECAP is measured with each implant manufacturer's clinical fitting software and programming interface: Cochlear (Custom Sound software with NRT), Advanced Bionics (NRI within its fitting software) and MED-EL (ART within its fitting software). EABR uses a standard evoked potential system (for example Interacoustics Eclipse, Natus Bio-logic Navigator Pro or Intelligent Hearing Systems SmartEP) triggered by the implant interface or by a dedicated electrical stimulator. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    • Intraoperative confirmation that the device works and the auditory nerve responds, alongside impedance telemetry.
    • Initial programming in young children who cannot give reliable responses: ECAP thresholds give a starting profile across electrodes, refined by behavioural observation. Electrically evoked stapedius reflex thresholds are another objective aid.
    • Monitoring over time and checking suspected device problems or electrode migration (with spread-of-excitation measures).
    • EABR for candidacy and special cases: promontory or intracochlear test-electrode EABR in cochlear nerve deficiency, and in auditory brainstem implant surgery to guide placement of the electrode paddle on the cochlear nucleus.

    Limitations: an absent ECAP does not prove that the nerve cannot be stimulated (artefact, measurement settings, and some patients with good outcomes lack measurable ECAPs); ECAP thresholds do not predict map levels precisely; EABR is time-consuming and artefact-prone. Interpret results together with behavioural measures, impedances and imaging.

    Comparing the waveforms

    Try it: typical waveforms of the other evoked potentials

    Choose a test to see an illustrative, idealised waveform with labelled peaks on its own time axis. Positive is plotted upwards for every test. Waveforms are schematic, not patient data; amplitudes and latencies are typical adult values.

    Time window
    Key measure
    RememberSelect a test.

    The clinical variants show: an enlarged SP (raised SP/AP ratio) for ECochG, a reduced Pa for MLR, an immature infant-type P1 for CAEP, a delayed and smaller P300, a smaller and later MMN for a harder contrast, a reduced cVEMP on one side, and an enlarged oVEMP of the kind seen with superior canal dehiscence.

    Current developments

    • Intracochlear ECochG recorded through the cochlear implant electrode during insertion, studied as a way to monitor and preserve residual hearing in real time.
    • Automated statistical detection of cortical responses and aided CAEP testing, with growing attention to hearing-aid processing effects on the stimuli.
    • Speech-evoked and complex-sound responses (for example frequency-following responses) as research measures of neural encoding of speech.
    • Bone-conducted and frequency-tuned VEMPs, and combined otolith and canal testing (with vHIT) for more complete vestibular profiles.
    • Automated ECAP measurements across the whole array and objective fitting aids for cochlear implants, including spread-of-excitation and recovery functions; these supplement, and do not replace, behavioural fitting.
    • Wireless and low-noise amplifiers and quick-application electrodes that make cortical recordings easier in infants and outside laboratories; many remain research tools.

    Demonstration

    Equipment: an AEP system with ECochG, MLR, late-response, oddball and VEMP protocols; insert earphones and bone vibrator; ear-canal electrodes (for example foil-covered insert tips); disposable surface electrodes; skin preparation; an EMG display; a sound-field loudspeaker; a volunteer; if available, a cochlear implant manufacturer's demonstration software or recorded ECAP examples. Use the waveform demo above to preview each response.

    1. Show the latency map (Figure 6.5a) and the waveform demo. Ask students to predict the filter and rate for each test from its latency.
    2. ECochG: place an ear-canal electrode and record alternating-polarity click responses at about 90 dB nHL. Identify SP and AP N1 and compute SP/AP. Record rarefaction and condensation separately and show the CM inverting. Clamp the sound tube to show that a real CM disappears while electrical artefact would not.
    3. MLR: change the window to 100 ms, the filter to about 10 to 200 Hz and the rate to about 7/s. Record from Cz and C3/C4. Ask the volunteer to tense the neck to show post-auricular muscle contamination.
    4. CAEP: with the volunteer awake and watching a silent video, record responses to a 1000 Hz tone burst at about one per second. Then ask the volunteer to close the eyes and relax deeply and note the change in amplitude.
    5. P300 and MMN: run an oddball sequence. First passive (reading) to obtain the MMN difference wave; then active (counting targets) to obtain the P300 at Pz. Compare the number of trials needed.
    6. cVEMP: place electrodes on the SCM and sternum, show the EMG meter, and record with the head turned away for a limited number of sweeps; demonstrate normalisation by recording once with a weak and once with a strong contraction. Keep the number of loud runs to a minimum.
    7. oVEMP: place infraorbital electrodes and record during upward gaze, with bone-conducted stimulation if available; show that the response is under the eye opposite the stimulated ear and becomes smaller when the gaze returns to straight ahead.
    8. ECAP: show recorded ECAP traces and an amplitude growth function from implant software or published examples, and explain artefact subtraction.

    Students should observe that the same amplifier and averaging principle, with different settings, yields responses from the cochlea to the cortex, and that patient state and muscle activity are decisive for the later and myogenic responses.

    Practical activity and assessment

    1. For each test in this topic, fill in a protocol card: stimulus, level, rate, electrode montage, filter, window, sweeps, patient state and the main measure. Check your values against the equipment manual and local protocol.
    2. In pairs, record a cVEMP bilaterally with EMG monitoring. Compute raw and EMG-normalised asymmetry ratios and explain any difference.
    3. Record a CAEP in a partner at two stimulus levels and measure N1 and P2 latency and N1-P2 amplitude.
    4. Using the waveform demo, sketch each waveform with its time axis and label each peak with its generator.
    5. Deliverable: the completed protocol cards, the VEMP and CAEP results with calculations, and a half-page case discussion choosing tests for (a) a child with suspected auditory neuropathy, (b) an adult with episodic vertigo and fluctuating low-frequency hearing loss, and (c) an infant fitted with hearing aids whose parents ask whether the aids are helping.
