    6.6

    Other audiological assessment instruments

    Supplementary3 hoursPractical P9Practical P8

    Beyond the audiometer and the evoked-potential systems, an audiology clinic depends on a family of instruments that probe the middle ear, the cochlear amplifier, speech understanding, infant hearing, the ear canal, hearing-aid performance and the vestibular system. This topic walks through each of them with the same eight questions, so that a student can compare how they generate a stimulus, capture a response and turn it into a clinical decision.

    What it is

    A set of eight instrument groups: immittance and wideband tympanometry, otoacoustic emissions, speech and sound-field audiometry, paediatric behavioural set-ups (VRA and play audiometry), newborn and school screening devices, video otoscopy, real-ear and hearing-aid test systems, and vestibular instruments (VNG/VOG, caloric irrigators and vHIT).

    Why it is required

    No single test describes an ear. The audiogram shows sensitivity; immittance shows middle-ear mechanics; OAEs show outer hair cell function; speech tests show functional hearing; screening devices find hearing loss early; real-ear measurement shows what a hearing aid actually delivers; vestibular tests assess the other half of the inner ear. A cross-check of several instruments is the basis of reliable diagnosis.

    How it works

    Most of these instruments share a small set of building blocks: a digitally generated stimulus, a miniature receiver or loudspeaker, a sealed probe or a sound field, a sensor (probe microphone, camera, electrodes or motion sensor), an analogue front end and ADC, and signal processing (averaging, Fourier analysis, statistical detection) that compares the response with normative data.

    RememberBasic notes on the middle-ear analyser are in 4.2, on the OAE analyser in 4.3 and on AEP instrumentation in 4.4. ABR, electrodes and ASSR are covered in 6.2, 6.3 and 6.4, VEMP in 6.5, and the audiometer in 6.1. Here each instrument is treated compactly; follow the links for the underlying detail.

    Shared engineering ideas

    Four concepts recur in every block below.

    • Level scales. Sound in a sealed ear canal is expressed in dB SPL (re 20 µPa). Short transients such as clicks are specified in peak-equivalent SPL (dB peSPL): the SPL of a continuous sine whose peak equals the peak of the transient. Screening ABR levels are in dB nHL, relative to normal behavioural threshold for that transient. Behavioural sound-field and speech levels are in dB HL.
    • Probe microphones and in-situ calibration. Because the ear canal volume varies from under 0.5 ml in a newborn to over 1.5 ml in an adult, a probe that delivers a fixed voltage produces different SPLs in different ears. Probe instruments therefore measure the SPL they produce in each ear (the in-ear check) and adjust the drive.
    • Averaging. A response that is time-locked to the stimulus adds coherently over N repetitions, while random noise adds as the square root of N. The signal-to-noise ratio in amplitude improves by √N, that is by 10 log10(N) dB.
    • Fourier analysis. With a sampling rate fs and an analysis block of N samples, the fast Fourier transform (FFT) gives frequency bins spaced Δf = fs/N apart. Instruments choose stimulus frequencies that fall exactly on bins so that the response is measured in one bin and the noise in its neighbours.
    SNR gain (dB) = 10 log10(N) Δf = f_s / N_FFT where N is the number of averaged, artefact-free repetitions, f_s the sampling rate and N_FFT the FFT length.

    Worked example: averaging 1000 frames improves the amplitude SNR by √1000 = 31.6 times, which is 30 dB. With fs = 48 kHz and NFFT = 4096, the bin spacing is 48 000 / 4096 = 11.72 Hz.

    6.6.1 Immittance audiometry and wideband tympanometry

    1. Basic working principle

    Acoustic immittance is the general term for acoustic admittance Y (how easily sound flows into the ear) and its reciprocal, impedance Z. A probe sealed in the canal delivers a low-frequency tone of known volume velocity and measures the resulting SPL with a probe microphone. For a given volume velocity, the SPL in the canal is inversely proportional to the admittance of everything beyond the probe: a stiff system (small admittance) builds up a high SPL, a compliant one a lower SPL. Instruments typically keep the probe SPL constant with an automatic gain control and read admittance from the drive needed. A pump varies the air pressure in the sealed canal from positive to negative. At extreme pressures the tympanic membrane is stretched and stiff, so the probe sees mainly the canal air volume; when canal pressure equals middle-ear pressure the membrane is most mobile and admittance peaks. The plot of admittance against pressure is the tympanogram. The acoustic reflex is measured by presenting a loud activator sound (in the same or the opposite ear) and detecting the small drop in admittance caused by contraction of the stapedius muscle.

    |Y_cavity| = 2π f V / (ρ c²) where f is the probe frequency, V the air volume, ρ ≈ 1.2 kg/m³ the density of air and c ≈ 343 m/s the speed of sound, so ρc² ≈ 1.42 × 105 Pa.

    Worked example: at 226 Hz a 1 cm³ cavity has |Y| = 2π × 226 × 10−6 / (1.42 × 105) = 1420 × 10−6 / 1.42 × 105 = 1.0 × 10−8 m³/(Pa·s), which equals 1.0 acoustic mmho in the cgs units used clinically. This is why, at 226 Hz, admittance in mmho and equivalent volume in cm³ (ml) are numerically interchangeable. At 1000 Hz the same cavity has an admittance 1000/226 = 4.4 times larger, and mass and resistance of the middle ear matter more, so the volume shortcut no longer applies.

    Probe-tone choice: 226 Hz is standard from about 6 months of age. In younger infants the ear canal wall is compliant and the middle ear behaves differently, so 226 Hz tympanograms can look normal even with effusion; a 1000 Hz probe tone is recommended for infants under about 6 months. Multifrequency tympanometry sweeps the probe frequency to find the middle-ear resonant frequency (typically near 800 to 1200 Hz in adults).

    Wideband tympanometry and absorbance: a broadband click or chirp (roughly 226 to 8000 Hz) is delivered and the instrument computes, at each frequency, the reflectance R(f), the ratio of reflected to incident sound pressure. Energy absorbance is A(f) = 1 − |R(f)|², the fraction of incident power absorbed by the middle ear. Measured at ambient pressure or across a pressure sweep, it shows how the middle ear accepts energy across the whole speech range, not only at one probe frequency. The source (probe) must be characterised in several calibration cavities beforehand so that incident and reflected components can be separated. Wideband measures are available on some clinical systems; normative data and interpretation criteria are still developing, and they supplement rather than replace standard tympanometry.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6a. Immittance meter. The probe carries three tubes (receiver, microphone, pressure); the activator reaches the canal through the probe (ipsilateral) or through an earphone on the other ear (contralateral).

    The probe assembly contains a miniature receiver for the probe tone, a probe microphone and an air tube to the pump; an ipsilateral activator is delivered through the same probe (often a second receiver). The manometer measures canal pressure so the controller can sweep it at a set rate. The processor computes admittance (often as its components, susceptance B and conductance G, at 1000 Hz), compensates for the ear canal volume and extracts the measures.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Probe tones: 226 Hz (standard), 678 Hz or 1000 Hz (infants, multifrequency). Activator signals: pure tones at 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz and broadband noise, typically 0.5 to 1 s long. Wideband stimulus: repeated clicks or chirps. The measured output is admittance (mmho) versus pressure (daPa), admittance change during an activator, and for wideband systems reflectance or absorbance versus frequency and pressure.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    A soft tip of suitable size seals the probe in the canal; the seal must be airtight for the pressure sweep. The pressure is swept typically from +200 to −400 daPa. Reflexes are measured at the tympanometric peak pressure so the membrane is most mobile. Contralateral activators use a supra-aural or insert earphone on the other ear; ipsilateral activators come through the probe, often alternated in time with the probe tone to avoid intermodulation artefacts.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The tympanogram is recorded as the pressure sweeps. Ear canal volume (ECV, also Vea) is read at the positive tail (+200 daPa); the compensated static admittance Ytm is the peak minus the tail; tympanometric peak pressure (TPP) is the pressure at the peak; tympanometric width is the pressure interval at half the compensated peak. For reflexes the admittance is sampled before and during the activator; a decrease larger than a criterion (commonly about 0.02 to 0.03 mmho), time-locked and repeatable, counts as a reflex. The reflex threshold is the lowest activator level that meets the criterion. Reflex decay testing holds the activator 10 dB above reflex threshold for 10 s at 500 and 1000 Hz and measures whether the amplitude falls by half or more. See the tympanogram demo in 4.2.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    Probe tone226 Hz at about 85 dB SPL; 1000 Hz for young infantsLow enough level not to elicit a reflex
    Pressure rangeabout +200 to −400 (to −600) daPaCovers typical middle-ear pressures
    Sweep ratecommonly about 50 to 600 daPa/s (often near 200)Fast sweeps shift TPP and change peak height
    Adult ECVroughly 0.6 to 1.5 ml (larger in some adults); children smallerLarge ECV with a flat trace suggests perforation or patent grommet
    Adult Ytmroughly 0.3 to 1.7 mmho (226 Hz)Low: stiffness (otosclerosis); high: flaccid TM or discontinuity
    TPProughly −100 to +50 daPa normalNegative: Eustachian tube dysfunction
    Reflex activator rangeipsi up to about 100 to 110 dB HL; contra up to about 120 dB HLLimits the maximum stimulus; loud activators need care
    Reflex criterionabout 0.02 to 0.03 mmho changeSmaller changes are indistinguishable from noise
    Wideband stimulus rangeabout 226 to 8000 HzAbsorbance across the speech range

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Interacoustics (Titan platform, which offers wideband tympanometry, and AT235), GSI (TympStar Pro), MAICO (touchTymp), Natus MADSEN (Zodiac), Amplivox (Otowave handheld tympanometers). Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: detecting effusion (flat trace, normal ECV), perforation or patent ventilation tube (flat trace, large ECV), Eustachian tube dysfunction (negative TPP), stiffness and discontinuity (low or high Ytm), and site-of-lesion patterns from ipsilateral and contralateral reflexes (middle ear, cochlea, VIII nerve, brainstem, facial nerve). Reflex thresholds at reduced sensation levels suggest cochlear loss; absent reflexes with normal middle ears and mild loss raise suspicion of retrocochlear or neural involvement. Advantages: objective, quick, no active response required. Limitations: needs a seal and a quiet, still patient; a 226 Hz probe misleads in young infants; tympanometry does not measure hearing. Wideband absorbance may add sensitivity for effusion and otosclerosis, but interpretation criteria are less established.

    6.6.2 Otoacoustic emission (OAE) instruments

    1. Basic working principle

    Outer hair cells amplify basilar-membrane motion nonlinearly. Part of this energy travels back through the middle ear and appears as a faint sound in the sealed canal: the otoacoustic emission. Transient-evoked OAEs (TEOAEs) are recorded after clicks; distortion-product OAEs (DPOAEs) are recorded during two simultaneous tones f1 and f2 (f2 > f1), at intermodulation frequencies, of which 2f1 − f2 is the largest in humans. Present emissions indicate functioning outer hair cells and a middle ear that transmits sound in both directions. See 4.3 for physiology and basic analysis.

    f1 = f2 / r (r = f2/f1, commonly about 1.22) f_DP = 2 f1 − f2 (cubic difference tone) f_DP2 = 2 f2 − f1 (upper DP, much smaller) where f1 and f2 are the primary tone frequencies and r the primary ratio.

    Worked example: f2 = 4000 Hz, r = 1.22: f1 = 4000 / 1.22 = 3279 Hz, fDP = 2 × 3279 − 4000 = 2557 Hz, and 2f2 − f1 = 4721 Hz. The emission is generated mainly near the f2 place on the basilar membrane, so a DP-gram is plotted against f2 even though the measured frequency is lower.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6b. OAE instrument. The outgoing path delivers clicks or two primaries; the return path digitises, rejects noisy frames, averages, and analyses the emission against the noise floor.

    The probe is the critical component: two receivers and a low-noise microphone in a housing a few millimetres across, with sound tubes that are easily blocked by wax or vernix. The ADC must handle a stimulus of 60 to 85 dB SPL and an emission near 0 dB SPL simultaneously, which calls for high resolution and careful gain staging.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    TEOAE: clicks of about 80 to 86 dB peSPL (or tone bursts), in linear or nonlinear mode. DPOAE: pairs of pure tones, commonly L1/L2 = 65/55 dB SPL, f2/f1 ≈ 1.22, at several f2 frequencies (DP-gram) or at several levels (DP input/output function). The generated output is a waveform and band spectrum (TEOAE) or DP level and noise floor per frequency (DPOAE).

    Nonlinear (derived) averaging for TEOAEs: the click sequence contains three clicks of one polarity followed by one click of opposite polarity and three times the amplitude (about 9.5 dB higher, since 20 log103 = 9.54 dB). When the four responses are summed, any linear component (the ringing of the stimulus in the canal and probe) cancels exactly: 1 + 1 + 1 − 3 = 0. The emission is compressive, so the large click does not produce three times the emission. If one click produces an emission of 1.0 (arbitrary units) and the large click only 1.8, the sum retains 3 × 1.0 − 1.8 = 1.2 units. The nonlinear mode therefore removes stimulus artefact at the cost of some response amplitude; it is standard for screening.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    A probe with a disposable tip is sealed in the canal. The instrument first plays a probe-fit (in-ear calibration) signal, checks the canal response for leaks and blockage, and adjusts the drive to reach the target SPL. Some systems calibrate in forward pressure level (FPL), which reduces errors from standing waves at high frequencies. The patient sits or sleeps quietly; newborns are tested asleep after a feed.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    Frames are high-pass filtered, checked against a noise-rejection threshold, and averaged into two interleaved buffers. For TEOAEs the first few milliseconds are windowed out, the reproducibility (correlation of buffers A and B) and the band SNRs are computed. For DPOAEs the FFT bin at 2f1 − f2 gives the emission level and the mean of neighbouring bins gives the noise floor; the SNR is their difference in dB. A response is accepted when it exceeds the protocol's SNR criterion (often about 6 dB) and a minimum absolute level.

    Try it: DPOAE frequencies and DP-gram

    Move f2, the f2/f1 ratio and the primary levels; the calculator shows the primaries and the distortion-product frequencies. The DP-gram shows a simulated emission (points) against a simulated noise floor for the chosen ear condition. The pass criteria are an example only: SNR at least the selected value and DP level at least −10 dB SPL, with an overall pass when at least 3 of the 4 frequencies f2 = 2, 3, 4 and 5 kHz pass.

    f1
    2f1 − f2
    2f2 − f1
    DP level / noise at this f2
    SNR at this f2
    Screening result (example rule)

    DP levelNoise floorNoise + criterionSelected f2 / primaries

    The model in the demo is for teaching: the DP level is highest near r ≈ 1.22 and when L1 follows the "scissors" rule L1 = 0.4 L2 + 39 dB proposed from DPOAE growth studies; it falls with cochlear hearing loss at the f2 place and is attenuated twice (inward and outward) by a conductive loss. The noise floor is higher at low frequencies, where body and room noise are larger.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    TEOAE stimulusabout 80 to 86 dB peSPL clicks, nonlinear modeRobust emission with artefact cancellation
    TEOAE window and bandsabout 2.5 to 20 ms; half-octave or octave bands about 1 to 4 kHzEarly window removes ringing
    DPOAE ratio and levelsf2/f1 ≈ 1.2; L1/L2 commonly 65/55 dB SPLNear-optimal DP in adults
    DP-gram rangef2 about 1 to 8 kHz (higher with special probes)Frequency-specific OHC status
    Emission level (normal)roughly −10 to +20 dB SPLNeeds averaging to exceed the noise floor
    SNR criterioncommonly about 3 to 6 dB or moreTrade-off between false refer and missed loss
    Test timetens of seconds to a few minutes per earSuitable for screening

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Otodynamics (ILO and Otoport families), Interacoustics (Titan, Eclipse OAE modules, OtoRead), GSI (Corti, Novus), MAICO (ERO-SCAN), Natus MADSEN (Capella), Intelligent Hearing Systems (SmartOAE). Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: newborn hearing screening, paediatric cross-check, identifying auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder (present OAEs with absent or abnormal ABR), ototoxicity monitoring (DPOAEs at high f2 often change before the audiogram), functional hearing loss, and differentiating cochlear from neural losses. Advantages: objective, fast, frequency-specific (DPOAE), no sedation. Limitations: OAEs are usually absent when the cochlear loss exceeds roughly 30 to 40 dB HL, so they do not estimate threshold; any conductive problem (effusion, even vernix) can abolish them; noise and poor probe fit cause false refers. Interpretation: "present" means an emission meets the protocol criteria; "absent" is non-specific and must be read with tympanometry and other tests.

    6.6.3 Speech audiometry and sound-field equipment

    1. Basic working principle

    Speech audiometry presents calibrated speech material and scores what the listener repeats or identifies. The speech recognition threshold (SRT) is the level at which about 50 % of easy words (spondees or equivalent) are repeated correctly; the speech detection or awareness threshold is the level at which speech is just detected; the word recognition score (WRS) is the percentage of words correct at a comfortable suprathreshold level; speech-in-noise tests estimate the signal-to-noise ratio needed for 50 % correct (SNR-50). The instrument is the speech channel of a diagnostic audiometer, fed by recorded material or monitored live voice, routed to earphones or to loudspeakers in a calibrated sound field.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6c. Speech channel (top) and a typical sound-field arrangement (plan view). Speech is usually presented from 0°; noise from 0°, 180°, ±45° or ±90° depending on the test. The level is calibrated at the reference point with the listener absent.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Recorded word lists, sentences and digits in the patient's language; a calibration tone (usually 1 kHz) recorded at the start of each track at the same VU level as the speech; speech-shaped noise, multi-talker babble or competing speech for masking and speech-in-noise tests; warble tones or narrowband noise for sound-field threshold testing. Pure tones are avoided in the sound field because room reflections create standing waves.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    Under earphones, speech is routed like pure tones, and contralateral speech-shaped masking is used when the presentation level minus IA could reach the non-test cochlea. In the sound field the listener sits at the reference point, typically about 1 m from the loudspeakers with the loudspeaker axis at ear height, facing 0°. Recorded material is strongly preferred to monitored live voice, because a talker's level, rate and pronunciation vary between sessions and clinics, and published norms apply only to the recorded version.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The listener repeats words aloud, points to pictures (closed-set tests for children) or types or taps responses in computerised tests. The tester scores each item; software can run adaptive procedures (level or SNR changes after each response) and compute SRT or SNR-50. Scores are interpreted against the confidence limits for the list length: a 25-word list has much wider test-retest limits than a 50-word list, because a score is a binomial proportion.

    SE(p) = √( p (1 − p) / n ) where p is the proportion correct and n the number of scored items.

    Worked example: a WRS of 80 % on 25 words has SE = √(0.8 × 0.2 / 25) = √0.0064 = 0.08, that is 8 percentage points; on 50 words SE = √(0.16/50) = 0.057, about 5.7 points. Two scores of 80 % and 68 % on 25-word lists may therefore not differ significantly.

    Speech channel calibration: the calibration tone is set to 0 VU on the audiometer meter so that the speech dial reads correctly. Speech dB HL is referenced to a speech reference level; in ANSI practice for earphones this is about 12.5 dB above the 1 kHz pure-tone RETSPL of the same earphone. For an illustrative supra-aural earphone with a speech reference near 20 dB SPL, speech at 40 dB HL corresponds to about 60 dB SPL in the coupler (VU-equivalent level). Sound-field calibration: with the listener absent, a sound level meter microphone is placed at the reference point, pointing as the standard for that meter and field type requires, and the level of each loudspeaker is measured for the calibration signal (warble tones, narrowband noise or speech noise) and adjusted to the reference values for sound-field listening. The level should also be checked a little in front of, behind and to each side of the reference point: it should vary by only a few dB over the region occupied by the head. In the free field the inverse square law applies: moving the head from 1.0 to 0.9 m changes the level by 20 log10(1.0/0.9) = 0.9 dB, while a movement of the same size near a wall reflection can change it more.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    Speech channel frequency responseflat within a few dB over roughly 125 to 6000 Hz (or wider)Preserves speech cues
    Speech level rangeabout −10 to 100 dB HL under earphones; lower maximum in sound fieldSRT, WRS and MCL/UCL measures
    VU meterstandard averaging meter, 0 VU referenceCorrect dial reading requires 0 VU input
    Loudspeaker distancecommonly about 1 m (or more)Far enough to reduce near-field error
    Level uniformity at head positiona few dB over the head regionHead movement should not change the result
    Room reverberation and noiselow reverberation; ambient below sound-field MPANLReverberation degrades speech scores
    Maximum sound-field outputoften about 90 dB HL (model dependent)Limits aided testing of severe losses

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Two-channel diagnostic audiometers with speech and sound-field outputs (Interacoustics, GSI, MAICO, Natus MADSEN, Inventis), external sound-field amplifiers and calibrated loudspeakers, recorded speech materials for the relevant languages (including lists developed for Indian languages) and computerised speech-in-noise tests such as QuickSIN, the Hearing in Noise Test and digits-in-noise tests. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: cross-checking pure-tone thresholds (SRT normally agrees with PTA3 within about 10 dB; a much better SRT suggests a functional loss), measuring functional speech understanding, predicting benefit and verifying outcome with hearing aids and cochlear implants (aided sound-field testing), counselling, and detecting retrocochlear signs (for example very poor WRS for the degree of loss, or rollover at high levels). Advantages: face validity and direct relation to communication. Limitations: dependence on language, vocabulary and cognition; sound-field testing is not ear-specific unless one ear is masked or plugged; room acoustics and loudspeaker position affect results.

    6.6.4 Paediatric behavioural set-ups: VRA and play audiometry

    1. Basic working principle

    Young children cannot follow the instructions of conventional audiometry, but they can learn a response. In visual reinforcement audiometry (VRA), suitable from a developmental age of about 6 months to about 2 to 2.5 years, a sound is presented and a head turn towards its source is rewarded by a brief visual display (an illuminated animated toy or a short video) next to the loudspeaker. After conditioning, the child turns on hearing the sound in anticipation of the reward. In conditioned play audiometry (CPA), suitable from about 2.5 to 5 years, the child performs a play action (dropping a block in a box, putting a peg in a board) each time a sound is heard. Levels found this way are minimum response levels (MRLs) in young infants, which are often somewhat above adult thresholds and approach them with age.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6d. VRA as a control loop: sound, head turn, observer judgement, reinforcement. A distracter keeps the child's gaze at midline between trials, and control (no-sound) trials scheduled from the audiometer estimate the false-response rate.

    A typical room: the child sits on a parent's lap (or in a high chair) in the centre of the booth, loudspeakers at about 90° (or 45°) on each side at about 1 m, each with a reinforcer mounted next to or above it; a second tester (the distracter) sits in front with quiet toys; the audiologist operates the audiometer from the control room through a window or with a camera view. Parents wear headphones with music or masking, or are asked not to react, so that they do not cue the child. Computer-controlled systems switch reinforcers from the audiometer, log each trial, schedule control trials and may use video clips as reinforcers.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Warble tones or narrowband noise at 500, 1000, 2000 and 4000 Hz (frequency-specific, and interesting to children), speech or speech-like signals for detection, and bone-conducted tones. The output is a set of MRLs per frequency and transducer.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    Conditioning begins with a clearly audible stimulus paired with the reinforcer, or presented and followed by the reinforcer after a spontaneous look, until the child turns reliably. Testing then proceeds with a bracketing rule (commonly down 10 or 20 dB after a response, up 10 dB after no response). As soon as the child accepts them, insert earphones (or the child's own earmoulds with inserts) are used to obtain ear-specific results, and a bone vibrator for BC. In CPA the child is taught the play task with a loud stimulus, and earphones are used from the start where possible.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The observer judges each head turn within a response window of a few seconds after stimulus onset; only a turn within that window is reinforced. Control trials without sound, interleaved at random, estimate the probability of a chance turn. If the child turns on control trials, the results are unreliable. Responses are recorded as MRLs and interpreted against age-appropriate expectations; they are combined with OAE, tympanometry and ABR results (the cross-check principle).

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    Loudspeaker positionsabout 90° (or 45°) each side, about 1 m, at child's ear heightClear localisation target for the head turn
    Sound-field calibrationdB HL at the child's head position with the child absentMRLs in dB HL comparable across visits
    Reinforcer durationa few seconds (for example about 2 to 4 s)Long enough to reward, short enough to avoid habituation
    Response windowa few seconds after stimulus onsetLimits chance responses
    Control trial proportiona minority of trials, randomly placed (local protocol)Estimates false responses
    Step sizes10 to 20 dB down, 10 dB up (commonly)Few trials before habituation

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Diagnostic audiometers with sound-field outputs and reinforcer control (for example from Interacoustics, GSI, MAICO and Inventis), dedicated reinforcer units (animated toys in smoked-glass boxes, LCD video reinforcers) and VRA control software from several suppliers. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: frequency-specific behavioural thresholds in infants and toddlers, confirmation of electrophysiological estimates, aided sound-field testing, monitoring of progressive loss. Advantages: behavioural (tests the whole auditory system), frequency-specific, no sedation. Limitations: needs a developmental age of about 6 months or more and adequate head control and vision; habituation limits the number of trials; sound-field testing is not ear-specific; tester and parent cueing are risks. Interpretation: MRLs are reported with the transducer, reliability and state of the child; a "normal" sound-field result does not exclude a unilateral loss.

    6.6.5 Newborn and school screening devices

    1. Basic working principle

    A screening device answers one question with a pass or refer outcome: is there enough evidence of a response to conclude that hearing is probably adequate? Newborn screeners are automated OAE devices (TEOAE or DPOAE) and automated auditory brainstem response (AABR) devices. AABR presents clicks or chirps at a fixed low level (commonly about 35 dB nHL) and uses a statistical detector to decide whether a brainstem response is present; because it tests the pathway up to the brainstem, it detects auditory neuropathy, which OAE screening misses. School and adult screening uses screening audiometers (fixed-level pure-tone sweeps) and often tympanometry.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6e. Generic automated screener. The detector stops the test as soon as a response is statistically confirmed (pass) or a maximum number of sweeps is reached without one (refer).

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    OAE screeners: nonlinear clicks for TEOAE or f1/f2 pairs at several f2 frequencies for DPOAE. AABR: clicks or broadband chirps (chirps compensate for cochlear travelling-wave delay and give larger responses) at about 35 dB nHL, at rates of several tens per second, often alternating polarity. Screening audiometers: pure tones at fixed levels, for example 20 dB HL at 1, 2 and 4 kHz (some protocols add 500 Hz or use 25 dB HL). Output: pass or refer per ear, with quality data (noise, impedance, test time).

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    OAE: probe tip in the canal. AABR: adhesive earcups or insert couplers, and three disposable electrodes, commonly high forehead (non-inverting), nape of neck or mastoid (inverting) and shoulder or cheek (common), after light skin preparation. The infant should be asleep or settled; testing after a feed helps. School screening: supra-aural or insert earphones in the quietest available room, one child at a time, with a demonstration first.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    AABR detectors compare the averaged EEG with a template of the expected newborn response, or apply statistical tests (for example tests based on the variance ratio of averaged and single sweeps, or binomial tests on the polarity of samples at many time points). The test continues until the probability that the observed pattern arises from noise falls below a preset significance level (pass) or a maximum sweep count is reached (refer). The noise reduction follows the √N rule: EEG noise of about 10 µV RMS averaged over 4000 sweeps falls to 10 / √4000 = 10 / 63.2 = 0.16 µV, which is comparable to a newborn ABR at screening levels (a few tenths of a microvolt). OAE screeners apply the SNR and band criteria described in 6.6.2. Screening audiometry is scored by the tester: a failure to respond at any frequency in either ear leads to rescreening and then referral.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    AABR stimulus levelcommonly about 35 dB nHLPass indicates hearing better than roughly that level in the click range
    AABR electrode impedancebelow the manufacturer's limit (screeners often accept higher values than diagnostic ABR)High impedance adds noise and prolongs the test
    Maximum test durationa few minutes per ear (device dependent)Refer on time-out, not only on absent response
    Detector significance levelset by the manufacturer (very low false-pass probability)Controls the risk of missing a hearing loss
    OAE screener pass ruleSNR and level criteria in several bands or frequenciesMust match the validated protocol
    Screening audiometer levels20 or 25 dB HL at 1, 2, 4 kHz (plus 500 Hz in some protocols)Identifies losses greater than the screening level

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Natus (ALGO AABR screeners, AccuScreen and Echo-Screen combined OAE/AABR screeners), MAICO (MB 11 BERAphone, easyScreen, ERO-SCAN), Interacoustics (Sera, OtoRead), GSI (Novus), Otodynamics (Otoport). Screening audiometers from Amplivox, MAICO, Interacoustics and others; smartphone and tablet-based screening systems with calibrated headphones are also in use. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: universal newborn hearing screening (typically OAE for well babies, with AABR for babies from neonatal intensive care because of the risk of auditory neuropathy), rescreening, school entry screening, occupational monitoring. The Joint Committee on Infant Hearing benchmarks (screening by 1 month, diagnosis by 3 months, intervention by 6 months) guide programme design. Advantages: quick, automated, usable by trained screeners. Limitations: a pass does not exclude mild or frequency-specific loss, late-onset or progressive loss; OAE screening misses neural disorders; refer results are common in the first days of life because of canal debris and middle-ear fluid. Interpretation: "refer" means "needs further testing", never "has a hearing loss"; every refer must lead to follow-up, and children with risk factors need surveillance even after a pass.

    6.6.6 Video otoscopy

    1. Basic working principle

    A video otoscope is an otoscope in which the eye is replaced by a miniature camera. Light is carried to the tip of the speculum by optical fibres or produced by LEDs near the tip; the illuminated ear canal and tympanic membrane are imaged by a lens onto an image sensor (usually CMOS), and the digitised image is shown on a monitor, computer or tablet and can be stored. Handheld models integrate the display; others connect to a computer by USB or wirelessly.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6f. Video otoscope. The image path replaces the examiner's eye; the processor handles exposure, focus and white balance, and the display allows the patient and others to see the image.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    The "stimulus" is white light from the LED source. The output is a live video stream (commonly around 30 frames per second) and still images or clips, usually stored as standard image or video files and, in hospital systems, attached to the patient record.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    The examiner selects the largest speculum that fits comfortably, pulls the pinna to straighten the canal (up and back in adults, back and down in infants), braces the hand against the head so that a sudden movement cannot drive the tip into the canal, and advances under view on the screen. Some systems use a foot pedal or button to capture images without moving the probe.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The sensor converts the image into pixels; the processor corrects exposure, colour (white balance) and sometimes focus, and compresses the stream. Interpretation is visual: the examiner (or a remote specialist in store-and-forward tele-audiology) assesses canal skin, cerumen, foreign bodies, discharge, and the tympanic membrane's colour, translucency, position (retracted or bulging), landmarks (handle of malleus, light reflex), perforations and ventilation tubes. Image-classification algorithms for otoscopic images are an active research area; they are not a substitute for clinical examination.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    Image resolutionfrom VGA to full HD or several megapixelsFine detail of the TM and small perforations
    Field of viewwide enough to show the whole TM at working distanceLandmarks visible in one frame
    Depth of field and focusa few millimetres to more; fixed or adjustable focusCanal and TM sharp at the same time
    Illuminationwhite LED, adjustable brightnessToo bright washes out colour; too dim adds noise
    Speculum sizesabout 2.5 to 5 mm (paediatric to adult)Fit and comfort
    InterfaceUSB or wireless; image exportIntegration with records and tele-audiology

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Natus (Aurical Otocam 300), Welch Allyn digital otoscopes, and a range of handheld and smartphone-attachment video otoscopes. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: documenting the ear before and after cerumen management, pre-impression otoscopy (checking the canal and the position of the otoblock), checking before immittance and REM, patient education and counselling, referral documentation, tele-audiology and training. Advantages: shared view, permanent record, magnification. Limitations: images are two-dimensional and colour depends on the camera and white balance; a video otoscope does not test TM mobility (use pneumatic otoscopy or tympanometry); infection control of reusable parts; examiner training is still essential.

    6.6.7 Real-ear measurement and hearing-aid test box

    1. Basic working principle

    Real-ear measurement (REM, also probe-microphone measurement) measures the sound pressure level near the eardrum with a thin silicone probe tube connected to a microphone, with and without the hearing aid, while a calibrated signal plays from a loudspeaker. A reference microphone near the ear entrance measures the incoming level and holds it constant (the modified pressure method with concurrent equalisation). The result shows what the hearing aid delivers in that individual ear, which differs from what the fitting software predicts because of ear canal size, earmould or dome acoustics, venting and microphone location. The hearing-aid test box measures the aid in a small sound-treated chamber on a standard coupler (commonly 2 cm³), for quality control, verification against the manufacturer's specification and troubleshooting.

    REIG(f) = REAR(f) − REUR(f) RECD(f) = SPL_real_ear(f) − SPL_2cc(f) Predicted real-ear SPL = coupler SPL + RECD where REUR is the real-ear unaided response, REAR the real-ear aided response (both SPL at the eardrum for the same input), REIG the real-ear insertion gain, and RECD the real-ear-to-coupler difference measured with the same insert transducer.

    Worked example: with a 65 dB SPL input, the unaided SPL near the eardrum at 2.5 kHz is 82 dB SPL (an ear canal resonance gain of 17 dB) and the aided SPL is 90 dB SPL. The insertion gain is 90 − 82 = 8 dB, although the aided ear is 25 dB above the input. For a child whose RECD at 2 kHz is 10 dB, a hearing aid measured at 100 dB SPL in the 2 cm³ coupler will produce about 110 dB SPL in that child's ear; this is why paediatric fittings verify maximum output with measured or age-appropriate RECDs.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6g. Real-ear system (top) and hearing-aid test box (bottom). In both, a reference microphone regulates the input level, and a measurement microphone (probe tube or coupler) records the output.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Speech-like signals are preferred for modern nonlinear aids because compression, noise reduction and feedback cancellation respond differently to tones and to steady noise. The International Speech Test Signal (ISTS), standardised in IEC 60118-15, is built from recordings of female talkers in several languages, cut into short segments and remixed so that it has the spectral and temporal properties of speech but is unintelligible in any language. It is typically played at 55, 65 and 75 dB SPL (soft, average, loud) and analysed in percentiles of the short-term level distribution (speech mapping). Other signals: pink or speech-shaped noise, pure-tone sweeps (for maximum output measured with a 90 dB SPL input, OSPL90), and composite or modulated signals. Outputs: SPL-o-grams relative to targets from NAL-NL2 or DSL v5, insertion gain, RECD, coupler frequency response, harmonic distortion, equivalent input noise and battery current.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    REM: the patient sits about 0.5 to 1 m from the loudspeaker, usually at 0° or 45° azimuth, with the probe tube inserted to within about 5 mm of the eardrum (commonly about 28 mm from the intertragal notch in adult females and about 30 mm in adult males, verified by otoscopy or by an acoustic method). The probe tube is first calibrated against the reference microphone so its own frequency response is removed. Test box: the aid is attached to the coupler (HA-1 for custom and receiver-in-canal devices with an earmould simulator, HA-2 with tubing for behind-the-ear aids), placed at the reference point in the chamber, and measured with feedback and adaptive features set as required by the standard.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    The probe microphone signal is analysed in fractional-octave bands or by FFT. For speech signals the analyser computes the long-term average spectrum and the 30th and 99th percentiles of short-term levels, displaying them as a band against the listener's thresholds (converted to dB SPL at the eardrum) and prescription targets. The audiologist adjusts gain and output until the measured values are within an acceptable tolerance of the targets (commonly within about 5 dB at key frequencies, per local protocol), and checks that maximum output stays below the patient's uncomfortable level. The test box computes standard values (OSPL90, full-on gain, reference test gain, frequency range, THD, equivalent input noise, battery current) and compares them with the manufacturer's specification sheet.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    Probe tubethin silicone tube about 1 mm outer diameterMust not change the fit or cause feedback
    Probe tip positionwithin about 5 mm of the TMStanding waves cause high-frequency errors if too far
    Input levelsabout 50 to 80 dB SPL speech; 90 dB SPL for OSPL90Checks soft, average, loud and maximum
    Frequency rangeabout 125 to 8000 Hz (some systems higher)Covers the fitting range
    Coupler2 cm³ (HA-1, HA-2); ear simulator for some testsStandard, repeatable load
    Test box ambient isolationenough to keep equivalent input noise measurableRoom noise inflates noise and distorts gain
    Tolerance to target (REM)commonly within about 5 dB (protocol dependent)Defines an adequate fit

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Audioscan (Verifit family), Interacoustics (Affinity), Natus/Otometrics (Aurical), Frye Electronics (FONIX hearing-aid test systems). Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: verification of every hearing-aid fitting against prescriptive targets, paediatric fitting with RECD, verification of open fittings and frequency-lowering features, checking directional microphones and noise reduction, and test-box quality control on delivery, at follow-up and after repair. Advantages: objective, individual, fast. Limitations: probe placement errors, feedback during measurement, patient movement, occluding cerumen, and difficulties with very small canals; coupler results do not describe the individual ear without RECD. Interpretation: the fitting software's "simulated" real-ear curves are predictions; measured real-ear results take precedence.

    6.6.8 Vestibular instruments (overview): VNG/VOG, caloric irrigators and vHIT

    1. Basic working principle

    The vestibular system is assessed mainly through the vestibulo-ocular reflex (VOR), which moves the eyes opposite to head motion to keep vision stable. Instruments therefore measure eye movements. Videonystagmography (VNG), also called video-oculography (VOG), uses infrared cameras in goggles to track the pupil during oculomotor tests (saccades, smooth pursuit, optokinetic and gaze tests), positional and positioning tests (such as the Dix-Hallpike manoeuvre) and caloric tests. Caloric testing warms or cools the ear canal, setting up convection in the endolymph of the lateral semicircular canal and producing nystagmus; comparing the two ears shows whether one lateral canal responds less. The video head impulse test (vHIT) measures the VOR gain for brief, fast, unpredictable head rotations, testing each semicircular canal at high frequencies of head motion. Older electronystagmography (ENG) recorded the corneo-retinal potential with electrodes; VNG has largely replaced it. VEMPs are covered in 6.5.

    2. Major hardware components and signal flow

    Figure 6.6h. VNG/VOG and vHIT. Cameras measure eye position; an inertial measurement unit (IMU) in vHIT goggles measures head velocity; the processor computes slow-phase velocity for nystagmus and VOR gain for head impulses.

    Goggles carry infrared LEDs and one or two infrared cameras (a hot mirror lets the camera see the eye while the patient sees through or the goggles are closed to remove fixation). The pupil appears as a dark disc; software thresholds the image, fits an ellipse, and tracks its centre frame by frame. A calibration with targets (for example at ±10°) converts pixel displacement into degrees. Caloric irrigators (water or air) have a temperature controller, flow control and a timer. vHIT goggles are light and tightly fitted to avoid slippage and include a gyroscope-based IMU.

    3. Types of signals used and generated

    Stimuli: visual targets (light bar or screen) for saccades, pursuit and optokinetic stimulation; head and body positioning; caloric irrigation, typically bithermal, with water at 30 °C and 44 °C (7 °C below and above body temperature) or air at temperatures set by the manufacturer (commonly around 24 °C and 50 °C); passive head impulses for vHIT. Outputs: eye position and velocity traces, nystagmus slow-phase velocity (SPV), saccade latency, velocity and accuracy, pursuit gain, VOR gain and corrective saccades.

    4. How the stimulus is presented

    Calorics: the patient lies supine with the head raised about 30° so the lateral canal is vertical; each ear is irrigated in turn (water commonly for about 30 to 40 s, air for about 60 s) and nystagmus is recorded with fixation removed, with rest intervals between irrigations. vHIT: the examiner stands behind the patient, who fixates a target about 1 m away, and delivers small (about 10 to 20°), brief, unpredictable head rotations with peak velocities of roughly 150 to 250°/s in the plane of the canal pair under test.

    5. How the response is detected and processed

    For nystagmus the software differentiates eye position to get velocity, identifies and removes fast phases, and reports the slow-phase velocity; for calorics the peak SPV (averaged over a few seconds at the culmination) of each irrigation enters the Jongkees formulas. For vHIT the gain is the ratio of eye velocity to head velocity (as a ratio of areas under the velocity curves, or at a fixed time after impulse onset), and the traces are inspected for covert and overt corrective saccades.

    UW (%) = [(RW + RC) − (LW + LC)] / (RW + RC + LW + LC) × 100 DP (%) = [(RW + LC) − (LW + RC)] / (RW + RC + LW + LC) × 100 VOR gain = eye velocity / head velocity where RW, RC, LW and LC are peak slow-phase velocities (deg/s) for right warm, right cool, left warm and left cool irrigations; UW is unilateral weakness (positive values mean the left is weaker) and DP directional preponderance.

    Worked example: RW = 20, RC = 18, LW = 10 and LC = 8 deg/s. The sum is 56 deg/s. UW = (38 − 18) / 56 × 100 = 35.7 %, a left weakness above a typical laboratory limit of about 20 to 25 %. DP = (28 − 28) / 56 × 100 = 0 %. In vHIT, a head impulse with peak velocity 200 deg/s producing an eye velocity of 90 deg/s gives a gain of 0.45, well below typical normal values for the lateral canal (commonly about 0.8 or more), consistent with a hypofunctioning canal.

    6. Important technical specifications

    ParameterTypical value or rangeClinical meaning
    VNG frame ratecommonly about 30 to 100+ frames/sAdequate for nystagmus and pursuit
    vHIT frame rateabout 250 frames/s or moreCaptures eye velocity during a 100 to 200 ms impulse
    Eye position range and resolutionabout ±30° or more; resolution well below 1°Small nystagmus is measurable
    Caloric water temperatures30 °C and 44 °C (air commonly about 24 °C and 50 °C)Equal and opposite thermal stimuli
    Unilateral weakness limitabout 20 to 25 % (laboratory specific)Asymmetric lateral canal function
    Normal lateral canal vHIT gaincommonly about 0.8 to 1.0 or moreLow gain with saccades: canal hypofunction
    Head impulseabout 10 to 20° amplitude, 150 to 250°/s peakHigh-frequency VOR test

    7. Examples of commonly used clinical equipment

    Interacoustics (VisualEyes VNG, EyeSeeCam vHIT, air and water caloric irrigators), Natus (ICS Chartr VNG, ICS Impulse vHIT), Synapsys (VNG and vHIT systems), and other manufacturers of rotary chairs and posturography systems. Examples only; models change, check current manufacturer documentation.

    8. Clinical applications, advantages, limitations and interpretation

    Applications: diagnosing unilateral and bilateral vestibular hypofunction, benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (positional nystagmus patterns), central oculomotor signs, monitoring vestibulotoxic drugs, and planning vestibular rehabilitation. Calorics test the lateral canal at very low frequencies of stimulation and ears separately; vHIT tests all six canals at high frequencies; the two are complementary and can disagree (for example in Ménière's disease). Advantages: objective, quantitative, recorded. Limitations: calorics depend on canal anatomy and irrigation technique and are unpleasant; water irrigation is contraindicated with a perforation (air may be used with care); goggle slippage causes vHIT artefacts; results must be interpreted with history and bedside examination by a qualified clinician.

    SafetyCheck the ear canal and TM with otoscopy before caloric irrigation; check the neck for contraindications before positional tests and head impulses; have a plan for nausea and falls. Caloric irrigator temperature must be verified regularly because a faulty heater can burn the canal.

    Summary comparison

    Demonstration

    Equipment: middle-ear analyser (with wideband option if available), OAE instrument with test cavity, diagnostic audiometer with sound field, a VRA reinforcer if available, an OAE or AABR screener, video otoscope, REM system with test box, and VNG/vHIT goggles if available; volunteers.

    1. Video otoscopy on a volunteer, displayed on the screen; students identify landmarks and confirm that the canal is clear for probe tests.
    2. Tympanometry at 226 Hz, then 1000 Hz on the same ear; compare shapes. Measure ipsilateral and contralateral reflex thresholds at 1 kHz. If available, show wideband absorbance at ambient pressure.
    3. Calibration-cavity check: place the tympanometer probe in 0.5, 2.0 and 5.0 ml cavities and read the volumes; place the OAE probe in its test cavity and confirm that no emission appears.
    4. DPOAE DP-gram on a volunteer; then partly block the probe tip or loosen the seal and show the effect on stimulus levels and noise. Use the u6-6-dpoae demo to predict frequencies and discuss the pass rule.
    5. Screening: run an OAE or AABR screen and show the detector progress, noise indicators and the stop rule.
    6. Sound field: calibrate a loudspeaker at the reference point with a sound level meter; move the meter 15 cm forwards, backwards and sideways and record the level change. Run a short recorded SRT under earphones.
    7. VRA set-up: arrange the room, demonstrate conditioning with a volunteer acting the child's role, and show a control trial.
    8. REM: probe-tube calibration, REUR on a volunteer, then REAR with a hearing aid using ISTS at 65 dB SPL; compute REIG at 2 kHz. In the test box, measure OSPL90 and full-on gain of the same aid.
    9. VNG/vHIT (if available): calibrate the goggles, record saccades and gaze, and deliver a few lateral head impulses; show gain and saccades.

    What students should observe: every probe instrument depends on a good seal and clean tubes; averaging and SNR rules decide what counts as a response; small positioning changes alter sound-field and REM results; instruments cross-check each other.

    Current developments

    • Wideband acoustic immittance moving into routine use, with growing normative data for infants and adults.
    • OAE calibration in forward pressure level and emission-pressure level, reducing standing-wave errors at high frequencies; extended high-frequency DPOAEs for ototoxicity monitoring.
    • Combined screening devices (OAE and AABR in one handheld unit) with chirp stimuli, cloud-based tracking of screening programmes and tablet-based school screening.
    • Integrated fitting and verification in which the fitting software adjusts the aid automatically to measured real-ear targets.
    • Tele-audiology use of video otoscopy and remote-controlled instruments, and research on image-classification algorithms for otoscopic images.
    • Lighter vHIT goggles and new paradigms (for example suppression head impulses) and increasing use of vHIT alongside calorics.

    Practical activity and assessment

    Practical P9 (tympanometer, OAE and ABR demonstration), with elements of P8. Work in groups of three, rotating through stations.

    1. Station 1: video otoscopy and tympanometry on each group member; record ECV, Ytm, TPP and reflex thresholds; save one otoscopic image per ear.
    2. Station 2: DPOAE DP-gram (f2 1 to 8 kHz, 65/55 dB SPL, ratio 1.22) and TEOAE on one ear; tabulate DP level, noise floor and SNR, and apply the example pass rule.
    3. Station 3: sound-field calibration check at the reference point and at four points 15 cm away; run a recorded SRT and compare with PTA3.
    4. Station 4: test-box measurement of one hearing aid (OSPL90, full-on gain, THD) and comparison with the specification sheet; if REM is available, REUR and REAR on one ear.
    5. Deliverable: a station report with measured values, one diagram per station of the signal flow you actually used, and a half-page cross-check interpretation that combines the results for one ear.

    Assessment: accuracy of measurements and calculations, correct use of each instrument, understanding of signal flow, and quality of the cross-check interpretation.
