    6.7

    Electrodes, electrode potential and the engineering of evoked potentials

    Supplementary3 hoursPractical P9

    An evoked potential starts as ionic current in a few thousand synchronised nerve cells and ends as a clean waveform on a screen. In between, the signal crosses brain, cerebrospinal fluid, skull and skin, passes through a chemical junction between skin and metal, and is amplified about a hundred thousand times before it is digitised and averaged. This topic follows that path from the cell membrane to the averaged trace, explains the electrochemistry of electrodes (half-cell potential, offset and drift), sets out the placement procedure step by step, and describes the engineering and recent technology that make sub-microvolt recording possible.

    What it is

    The physical and engineering basis of evoked-potential recording: how neurons generate a measurable field, how that field reaches the scalp, what happens electrically and chemically at the electrode, and how the instrumentation amplifier, filters, analogue-to-digital converter (ADC) and averager turn a microvolt signal buried in noise into a measurable waveform.

    Why it is required

    Most failed or misleading ABR and ASSR recordings are caused by the electrodes or the front end, not by the patient's auditory system. A clinician who understands electrode potential, drift, saturation and the noise budget can recognise the cause of a bad recording within seconds and fix it, instead of collecting thousands of useless sweeps or misreading an artefact as a response.

    How it works

    A sudden stimulus makes many auditory neurons fire at almost the same moment. Their membrane currents add to form a current dipole whose field spreads through the head by volume conduction. Electrodes convert ionic current in the tissue into electronic current in the wire through an electrochemical interface that also creates a steady half-cell voltage. A differential front end rejects what is common to both electrodes, removes the steady offset, amplifies and digitises the difference, and synchronous averaging extracts the time-locked response.

    Key ideaThis topic goes deeper than the earlier notes and deliberately avoids repeating them. The standard montages, impedance targets, thermal noise, impedance imbalance and artefact types are in 6.3. The complete ABR system, stimulus types, filter choices, sampling, artefact rejection and weighted averaging are in 6.2, and the ASSR in 6.4. First principles of differential amplification and averaging are in 4.4. Here the focus is on the physics, the electrochemistry, the step-by-step procedure and the design of the front end.

    1. How an evoked potential is generated in the body

    The resting membrane potential

    Every neuron is enclosed by a membrane about 5 to 10 nm thick, made of lipid that does not conduct ions. Protein channels and pumps set in this membrane control which ions cross it. The sodium-potassium pump uses metabolic energy to move sodium (Na+) out of the cell and potassium (K+) into it, so that the inside of the cell has a high potassium concentration and a low sodium concentration compared with the fluid outside.

    At rest, the membrane is much more permeable to potassium than to other ions, because some potassium channels stay open. Potassium ions therefore tend to diffuse out of the cell, down their concentration gradient. Each ion that leaves carries a positive charge, so the inside becomes negative relative to the outside. This negative inside pulls potassium back in. Very quickly a balance is reached where the electrical pull inward equals the diffusional push outward. The voltage at which this balance holds for one ion species is called the equilibrium potential (or reversal potential) of that ion, and it is given by the Nernst equation.

    E_ion = (R T / z F) × ln( [ion]out / [ion]in ) At 37 °C: R T / F = 26.7 mV E_ion ≈ (61.5 mV / z) × log10( [ion]out / [ion]in ) where E_ion is the equilibrium potential of the inside relative to the outside, R = 8.314 J/(mol·K) is the gas constant, T the absolute temperature in kelvin (310.15 K at 37 °C), z the valence of the ion (+1 for K+ and Na+, −1 for Cl−, +2 for Ca2+), F = 96 485 C/mol is the Faraday constant, and [ion] the concentrations outside and inside the cell.

    Worked example for potassium, using typical mammalian values of 140 mM inside and 5 mM outside at 37 °C:

    • R T / F = 8.314 × 310.15 / 96 485 = 0.02673 V = 26.7 mV.
    • Concentration ratio out/in = 5 / 140 = 0.0357; ln(0.0357) = −3.33.
    • E_K = 26.7 mV × (−3.33) / (+1) = −89 mV.
    • With the base-10 form: 61.5 × log10(0.0357) = 61.5 × (−1.447) = −89 mV, the same answer.

    The same arithmetic with typical values gives about +67 mV for sodium (12 mM inside, 145 mM outside), about −74 mV for chloride (7 mM inside, 110 mM outside, z = −1) and well over +100 mV for calcium, whose free concentration inside the cell is extremely low. Real concentrations vary between cell types; these values are illustrative.

    The actual resting potential of a neuron, typically about −60 to −75 mV, lies between these equilibrium potentials, closest to that of the ion to which the membrane is most permeable. The Goldman equation (Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz voltage equation) expresses this: it is a weighted version of the Nernst equation in which each ion's concentrations are multiplied by its relative permeability. With illustrative relative permeabilities of 1 for K+, 0.04 for Na+ and 0.45 for Cl−, the concentrations above give about −70 mV.

    Vm = (R T / F) × ln( (PK[K]o + PNa[Na]o + PCl[Cl]i) / (PK[K]i + PNa[Na]i + PCl[Cl]o) ) where Vm is the membrane potential, P the relative permeability of each ion, and subscripts o and i denote outside and inside. Chloride appears with inside and outside swapped because its charge is negative.
    Clinical linkThe cochlea is an exception worth remembering. The endolymph in the scala media has an unusually high potassium concentration and a positive endocochlear potential of roughly +80 to +100 mV, maintained by the stria vascularis. Hair cells therefore sit between a positive endolymph and their own negative interior, giving a large driving voltage for transduction current. The cochlear microphonic and summating potential recorded in ECochG (6.5) are produced by this current.

    Try it: Nernst potential calculator

    Choose an ion, set the concentrations inside and outside the cell and the temperature. The bar shows the equilibrium potential on the same scale as a typical neuronal resting potential (dashed line).

    RT/zF
    Ratio out/in
    Equilibrium potential E

    equilibrium potential of the chosen iontypical resting potential (about −70 mV)

    Things to try: with K+ selected, raise the outside concentration from 5 to 10 mM and see E_K rise (become less negative) by about 18 mV. This is why a high blood potassium level makes excitable tissue, including the heart, dangerous. Change the temperature from 37 °C to 20 °C and note the small change: the Nernst potential is proportional to absolute temperature, so a 17 degree change is only about a 5 % change.

    Action potentials and synaptic potentials

    Two kinds of electrical event change the membrane potential, and both contribute to evoked potentials.

    • Action potentials (spikes) are all-or-none events in axons. When the membrane is depolarised past a threshold (typically about 15 to 20 mV above rest), voltage-gated sodium channels open, sodium rushes in and the inside swings positive, reaching roughly +30 to +40 mV. Within about a millisecond the sodium channels inactivate and potassium channels open, and the membrane repolarises. The spike propagates along the axon. Its duration is short, typically under 1 to 2 ms at the recording site.
    • Postsynaptic potentials (PSPs) are graded changes at synapses. When a neurotransmitter binds to receptors on the receiving neuron, ion channels open for a few to tens of milliseconds. An excitatory PSP (EPSP) depolarises the membrane, an inhibitory PSP (IPSP) usually hyperpolarises it. PSPs are smaller than spikes (typically a few millivolts across the membrane) but last much longer.

    The duration matters for the recording. Fast brainstem components such as ABR waves I to V contain energy up to about 1 to 2 kHz and are thought to arise largely from synchronised spikes in auditory nerve fibres and brainstem fibre tracts, together with fast synaptic activity in nuclei such as the cochlear nucleus and superior olive. Slower middle- and long-latency responses (MLR, cortical P1-N1-P2, P300) are dominated by postsynaptic currents in the dendrites of pyramidal cells in the auditory cortex. Postsynaptic potentials last long enough to overlap in time across many cells, which is why cortical responses are larger and slower.

    Why synchronised activity of many neurons is needed

    A single neuron produces a field far too small to detect at the scalp. The scalp potential is the sum of the contributions of many cells. How those contributions add depends on timing:

    • If N similar sources fire at exactly the same moment with the same orientation, their contributions add linearly: the total is N times one source.
    • If they fire at random times, their contributions have random signs at any instant and partly cancel. The total grows only as √N, and it looks like noise rather than a peak.

    For 10 000 fibres, synchronous firing gives 10 000 units of signal and random firing about 100: a factor of 100 in favour of synchrony. This is the physiological reason why the ABR needs a stimulus with an abrupt onset. A click excites a wide region of the basilar membrane within a fraction of a millisecond and makes many auditory nerve fibres fire together. A tone with a slow rise time excites fibres over a longer period, the discharges spread in time, and the summed potential becomes smaller and broader even though the neurons are responding. The same principle explains why the ABR is small or absent in auditory neuropathy spectrum disorder even when outer hair cell function is normal: the problem is often dys-synchrony rather than absence of neural activity.

    Figure 6.7a. From stimulus to scalp potential. Only activity that is synchronised across many neurons produces a field large enough to reach the scalp as a clear peak. Random firing adds up only as the square root of the number of cells and looks like background noise.

    Current dipoles

    When a patch of membrane depolarises, positive ions flow into the cell at that spot. Inside the cell the current spreads along the axon or dendrite and leaves through membrane further away. Outside the cell the circuit closes through the extracellular fluid. Seen from outside, the active spot behaves as a current sink (current disappears into the cell) and the distant membrane as a current source (current reappears). A sink and a source a small distance apart form a current dipole. Its strength is described by the dipole moment, the current multiplied by the separation, in units of ampere-metres (A·m). The dipole moment of an averaged sensory response in cortex is often quoted in the range of a few to some tens of nanoampere-metres (nA·m), although values depend strongly on the response and the method.

    A dipole produces a positive potential on the side of the source and a negative potential on the side of the sink, with a zero line in between. The electrode that faces the positive end records a positive peak, the electrode that faces the negative end records a negative one. This is why the polarity and size of an ABR wave depends on where the two electrodes sit relative to the generator (6.3), and why reversing the electrode leads inverts the waveform.

    Open and closed fields

    Whether many dipoles add or cancel depends on their geometry.

    Open field

    • Neurons are aligned in parallel, with dendrites pointing the same way (for example cortical pyramidal cells, or bundles of parallel axons in a fibre tract).
    • Their dipoles point the same way and add.
    • The field extends far beyond the tissue and can be recorded at a distance.
    • Most scalp-recorded evoked potentials come from open-field arrangements.

    Closed field

    • Neurons are arranged radially or randomly, for example cells with dendrites spreading in all directions in a spherical nucleus.
    • Their dipoles point in many directions and largely cancel.
    • Strong local activity can produce almost no field outside the structure.
    • Such activity is invisible to scalp electrodes, even if it is physiologically important.

    Axons contribute in a particular way. A propagating action potential in a straight axon behaves approximately as a pair of opposed dipoles (a quadrupole) moving along the fibre, whose far field falls off faster than a dipole's. Far-field peaks from axons tend to arise where the propagating volley changes its situation: where the fibre bends, where it passes from one tissue to another of different conductivity, or where it terminates. Several ABR waves are thought to be generated at such points along the auditory pathway, which is one reason the peaks are sharp and have stable latencies.

    Volume conduction through brain, CSF, skull and scalp

    The head is a volume conductor: a three-dimensional body of conducting fluid and tissue. Currents from the dipole spread through the whole volume, and the potential at any point is set by the geometry and by the conductivity of each tissue. Typical conductivity values used in head models differ widely between tissues (approximate figures from the modelling literature):

    TissueApproximate conductivityEffect on the recorded field
    Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF)about 1.5 to 1.8 S/mVery good conductor; shunts current around the brain and spreads the field
    Brain (grey and white matter)about 0.1 to 0.4 S/mModerate conductor; white matter conducts better along fibres than across them
    Skullroughly 0.005 to 0.02 S/m (values vary widely)Poor conductor; attenuates and spatially blurs the field, so scalp maps are smooth
    Scalpabout 0.3 to 0.4 S/mModerate conductor; spreads current sideways under the skin

    Because the skull conducts much less than the tissues on either side, it acts like a resistive layer that smears the potential. A focal source in cortex appears on the scalp as a broad hill of potential several centimetres across. This limits the spatial resolution of scalp recording and is why even high-density caps cannot localise sources as sharply as recordings from inside the head. It also means that small shifts of an electrode (a centimetre or so) rarely change an evoked potential much, as long as the electrode stays on the same side of the zero line of the dipole field.

    For practical frequencies (below a few kilohertz), the tissues behave almost purely resistively and the field is established instantaneously. There is no propagation delay between the source and the scalp; the latency of an ABR wave is set entirely by physiology and by the stimulus path, not by conduction through the head.

    Near-field and far-field recording

    A near-field recording places the electrode close to the generator, where the potential is large and changes rapidly with position. A far-field recording places the electrode far from the generator compared with the size of the source; the potential is small and changes slowly with position. The contrast follows from how a dipole's potential falls with distance.

    V(r, θ) = p cosθ / (4 π σ r²) where V is the potential at distance r from a current dipole of moment p (A·m) in an infinite uniform conductor of conductivity σ (S/m), and θ the angle between the dipole axis and the direction to the electrode. The formula holds when r is much larger than the separation of source and sink.

    The key feature is the 1/r² dependence. Doubling the distance reduces the amplitude to a quarter; ten times the distance reduces it to a hundredth. A monopole (a single isolated source) would fall as 1/r, a quadrupole as 1/r³. Real heads are not uniform, and near the source the dipole approximation breaks down, but the 1/r² rule gives the right order of magnitude.

    Worked comparison: ECochG near-field versus scalp far-field. Take the auditory nerve compound action potential (the ECochG AP, which corresponds to ABR wave I) and consider three electrode positions at rough, illustrative distances from the effective generator:

    • Transtympanic needle on the cochlear promontory: about 5 mm.
    • Ear canal electrode near the tympanic membrane or in the canal: about 15 mm.
    • Mastoid or earlobe scalp electrode: about 35 mm.

    By the 1/r² rule the ratios are (35/5)² = 49 between promontory and mastoid, (35/15)² ≈ 5.4 between ear canal and mastoid, and (15/5)² = 9 between promontory and canal. If wave I at the mastoid is about 0.3 µV, the model predicts about 1.6 µV in the canal and about 15 µV at the promontory. Published values are consistent with this order of magnitude: transtympanic AP amplitudes are commonly several microvolts to a few tens of microvolts, ear-canal (extratympanic) recordings typically a fraction of a microvolt to a few microvolts, and scalp wave I typically a few tenths of a microvolt. The exact figures depend on the patient, the stimulus level and the electrode, and the calculation should be read as an explanation of the trend rather than a prediction.

    Figure 6.7b. Near-field and far-field recording (simplified uniform-conductor model). The same generator seen from electrodes at about 5, 15, 35 and 80 mm gives amplitudes in the ratio of about 49 : 5.4 : 1 : 0.19 relative to the mastoid, because a dipole's potential falls with the square of distance. TT: transtympanic. Distances are illustrative.

    Try it: amplitude against distance from a dipole

    Simplified model: a single current dipole in an infinite uniform conductor (σ = 0.33 S/m), electrode on the dipole axis. Change the dipole moment and the two sites to compare. Both axes are logarithmic.

    Potential at site A
    Potential at site B
    Ratio A / B
    Ratio in dB
    1/r² dipole potentialrecording sites

    The absolute microvolt values in the demo ignore the skull, the finite size of the source and the reference electrode, so they are not predictions for any real response. The ratios between sites are the useful part: 20 × log10(49) = 34 dB separates promontory and mastoid, which is why a transtympanic ECochG needs only a few hundred sweeps whereas a scalp wave I needs thousands.

    Why the ABR is sub-microvolt and cortical potentials are several microvolts

    Several factors combine:

    • Distance. Brainstem generators lie deep in the head, typically 6 to 8 cm or more from the vertex. Auditory cortex lies in the superior temporal plane, a few centimetres from the scalp. With 1/r², halving the distance quadruples the amplitude.
    • Number and alignment of cells. Cortical responses sum postsynaptic currents over large sheets of parallel pyramidal cells (an open field). Brainstem nuclei are smaller and some have partly closed-field geometry.
    • Duration of the underlying events. Postsynaptic currents last tens of milliseconds and overlap in time across cells, so their sum builds up. Brainstem spikes last well under a millisecond, so small differences in timing between fibres reduce the summed peak.
    • Filtering. The ABR is recorded with a high-pass edge of 30 to 100 Hz, which removes slow components; cortical responses are recorded from about 1 Hz upward.

    The result is that ABR waves are typically about 0.1 to 0.7 µV, the MLR about 0.5 to 2 µV and cortical P1-N1-P2 components typically a few microvolts up to about 10 µV in adults. Background EEG and muscle activity are typically several microvolts to tens of microvolts in every case, which is why all of these need averaging, and the ABR needs far more sweeps than a cortical response.

    What synchronises the response: stimulus onset and rise time

    The ABR is an onset response. It is triggered by the rapid change at the start of the stimulus, and the shorter the rise time, the better synchronised the discharge. Some practical consequences:

    • Clicks (typically 100 µs rectangular pulses) give the best synchrony and the largest, sharpest waves but little frequency specificity.
    • Tone bursts need a rise time long enough to keep the spectrum narrow (typically about 2 cycles of the carrier) and short enough to keep synchrony. Low-frequency bursts have long rise times and give broader, later wave V peaks that are harder to identify.
    • Chirps delay the high-frequency part of a broadband stimulus relative to the low-frequency part to compensate for the travelling-wave delay along the basilar membrane, so that more of the cochlea responds at the same moment. Wave V is typically larger than for a click at the same level, particularly at moderate and low levels (6.2).
    • Steady-state responses (ASSR, 6.4) use the same principle repeatedly: each cycle of the amplitude modulation acts as a small onset, and neurons phase-lock to the modulation envelope.
    • The recording system must also be synchronised: the averager must know the exact moment of stimulus onset (the trigger). Timing jitter between trigger and stimulus smears the average in the same way as neural dys-synchrony (section 6).

    2. Electrode potential: electrochemistry made simple

    Inside the body, current is carried by ions (Na+, K+, Cl−) moving through water. In the electrode lead and the amplifier, current is carried by electrons moving through metal. An electrode is a transducer between these two kinds of conduction. The conversion happens through chemical reactions at the surface where metal meets electrolyte, and those reactions create a voltage of their own. Understanding this voltage explains electrode offset, drift, motion artefact and much of electrode noise.

    The metal-electrolyte interface and the half-cell potential

    When a metal is placed in an electrolyte, two opposing processes begin at the surface:

    • Oxidation: metal atoms give up electrons and leave the surface as positive ions (for silver: Ag → Ag+ + e−). The electrons stay in the metal.
    • Reduction: positive ions in the solution take electrons from the metal and deposit as atoms (Ag+ + e− → Ag).

    At first one process dominates, so charge builds up: for example the metal becomes slightly negative (excess electrons) and the solution next to it slightly positive (excess cations). This separated charge creates an electric field that slows the dominant reaction and speeds the opposite one. Within a very short time the two rates become equal and a stable voltage is established between the metal and the bulk of the solution. This voltage is the half-cell potential. It depends on the metal, the ions in the solution, their concentrations and the temperature, in the same way that the Nernst equation describes the membrane: the physics is the same, a balance between chemical tendency and electrical force.

    A single half-cell potential cannot be measured directly. Any measurement needs a second contact with the solution, which is itself another metal-electrolyte interface with its own half-cell potential. What a voltmeter reads is always the difference between two half-cells. This is exactly what happens in an evoked-potential recording: the amplifier measures the difference between two electrodes, each of which contributes its own half-cell potential.

    The standard hydrogen electrode as reference

    To tabulate half-cell potentials, chemists compare each electrode with an agreed reference, the standard hydrogen electrode (SHE): a platinum electrode in an acid solution of unit hydrogen-ion activity, with hydrogen gas bubbling over it at standard pressure. By convention its potential is defined as 0 V at all temperatures. The standard electrode potential of another electrode is its voltage relative to the SHE when all species are at unit activity and 25 °C.

    Electrode reactionApproximate standard potential vs SHERelevance
    AgCl + e− → Ag + Cl− (silver/silver chloride)about +0.22 VThe standard biopotential electrode; its potential depends on chloride concentration, which the gel keeps steady
    Ag+ + e− → Ag (bare silver)about +0.80 VBare silver behaves differently from chlorided silver; an electrode whose chloride coating has worn off loses its advantage
    Cu2+ + 2e− → Cuabout +0.34 VCopper is not used on the skin but appears in connectors; exposed copper in contact with gel produces offsets
    Sn2+ + 2e− → Sn (tin)about −0.14 VTin electrodes are used in some EEG caps
    Zn2+ + 2e− → Znabout −0.76 VIllustrates how different metals can be almost a volt apart
    Au+ + e− → Au (gold)about +1.7 V (tabulated value)Gold is so unreactive that in gel it does not reach this equilibrium; its actual potential is ill-defined and drifts, which is the practical problem with gold

    These are textbook equilibrium values for the metal in a solution of its own ions. A real electrode on the skin sits in a gel containing mostly sodium chloride or potassium chloride, often with other ingredients, at body temperature, so its actual potential differs from the tabulated value. For Ag/AgCl the tabulated value is realistic because the gel supplies the chloride ions that set it. For noble metals such as gold and platinum there is no well-defined reaction in the gel, and the potential is set by traces of impurities, oxygen and surface condition. The important practical fact is that different metals, or the same metal in different surface states, can differ by tens to hundreds of millivolts.

    The electrical double layer

    The separated charge at the interface is organised in a thin structure called the electrical double layer. On the metal side the charge sits at the surface. On the solution side, ions of opposite sign are attracted to the surface and form a layer roughly one ion diameter away, called the Helmholtz layer; beyond it a more diffuse cloud of ions extends a little further into the solution. Two sheets of opposite charge separated by a very small distance are, electrically, a capacitor. Because the separation is only a fraction of a nanometre, the capacitance per unit area is large, typically of the order of 10 to 40 µF per square centimetre for a smooth metal surface. Real electrode surfaces are rough, so the effective area and capacitance are larger.

    Figure 6.7c. The electrode-electrolyte-skin interface. At the silver chloride surface, a double layer of charge (metal side and ionic Helmholtz layer) forms a capacitor with a half-cell voltage across it. The gel carries current as ions. The stratum corneum is the main barrier; sweat ducts provide parallel conducting paths. Below it the living tissue conducts well. Not to scale: the double layer is less than a nanometre thick, the stratum corneum about 10 to 20 µm.

    Current can cross the interface in two ways, and the balance between them defines the electrode type.

    • Capacitive (non-Faradaic) current: charge is added to or removed from the double layer, like charging a capacitor. No chemical reaction occurs. The voltage across the interface changes as charge accumulates.
    • Faradaic current: electrons actually cross the interface through a reversible chemical reaction (oxidation or reduction). The interface behaves like a resistor, called the charge-transfer resistance, in parallel with the double-layer capacitance.

    Polarisable and non-polarisable electrodes

    Perfectly polarisable (ideal limit)

    • No charge crosses the interface; all current is capacitive.
    • Behaves like a capacitor: very high charge-transfer resistance.
    • Voltage across the interface changes when current flows (it becomes polarised).
    • Noble metals such as platinum and gold approach this behaviour.
    • Sensitive to movement, bias current and pressure; larger drift and low-frequency noise.

    Perfectly non-polarisable (ideal limit)

    • Charge crosses freely through a fast reversible reaction.
    • Behaves like a small resistor: low charge-transfer resistance.
    • Voltage stays close to its equilibrium value whatever small current flows.
    • Silver/silver chloride in a chloride gel approaches this behaviour.
    • Stable offset, low drift, less motion artefact, low noise.

    The polarisation voltage that appears when current flows through a polarisable electrode is called overpotential. In evoked-potential recording the currents are tiny (input bias currents of picoamperes to nanoamperes, and impedance-test currents of nanoamperes), but even these can shift the potential of a polarisable electrode slowly, and the charge stored in the double layer is disturbed every time the electrode moves.

    Why Ag/AgCl is preferred

    A silver/silver chloride electrode is silver coated with a thin layer of silver chloride, used with a gel containing chloride ions. Two coupled reactions carry current reversibly:

    Ag ↔ Ag+ + e− (at the metal) Ag+ + Cl− ↔ AgCl (at the coating) overall: Ag + Cl− ↔ AgCl + e− The reaction can run either way with little energy barrier, so electrons can cross the interface in both directions without a build-up of charge.

    Because the reaction is fast and reversible, and because the gel holds the chloride concentration steady, the Ag/AgCl electrode has:

    • a well-defined half-cell potential (about +0.22 V vs SHE under standard conditions), which is nearly the same for two electrodes of the same type and batch, so the difference (offset) between them is small;
    • a low charge-transfer resistance, so little polarisation from bias and test currents;
    • low drift and low intrinsic noise at low frequencies;
    • faster recovery after movement or after a large transient.

    The limitation is that the chloride coating is consumed or damaged with use and cleaning, and silver chloride is sensitive to light and to abrasion. Disposable Ag/AgCl electrodes avoid this by being used once. Sintered Ag/AgCl electrodes (a pressed and heated mixture of silver and silver chloride powder) are more durable because the chloride is present throughout the body of the electrode, not just as a surface layer.

    Electrode offset potential

    The two recording electrodes each have a half-cell potential. If they were perfectly identical, the two potentials would cancel at the differential amplifier. In practice they differ, because of small differences in surface condition, gel composition and concentration, temperature, pressure and the skin under each electrode. The skin itself adds a potential (the skin potential, from ionic gradients across the epidermis and from sweat glands) that also differs between sites. The total difference seen at the amplifier input is the electrode offset potential (also called DC offset or offset voltage).

    • With matched, well-prepared Ag/AgCl electrodes, offsets are typically a few millivolts to a few tens of millivolts.
    • With polarisable metals, worn electrodes, dried gel or poorly prepared skin, offsets of tens to over a hundred millivolts can occur.
    • Industry standards for disposable ECG electrodes, for example, set limits for the DC offset of a pair of electrodes of the order of 100 mV, which gives an idea of the range that front ends must tolerate.

    For comparison, wave V is about 0.5 µV. A 20 mV offset is 40 000 times larger than the signal. The amplifier must pass the microvolt signal while not being overwhelmed by the millivolt offset.

    Figure 6.7d. Extended equivalent circuit for a pair of recording electrodes (building on the single-electrode model of Figure 6.3e in 6.3). Each path from the tissue to the amplifier contains the skin (resistance and capacitance of the stratum corneum, with its own skin potential), the gel resistance, the double layer (charge-transfer resistance in parallel with double-layer capacitance) and the half-cell potential. The differential amplifier sees the evoked potential plus the difference of all the DC sources: V_diff = V_EP + (E1 − E2) + (Esk1 − Esk2).

    Key ideaThe evoked potential is a microvolt difference riding on a millivolt difference. The millivolt part is not a biological signal at all: it is chemistry at the two electrodes. Everything that makes the two electrodes chemically different (different metals, one fresh and one old, one gel dried, one on unprepared skin) makes the offset bigger and less stable.

    Worked example: why an offset can saturate the amplifier

    Suppose the electrode offset is 50 mV and the first amplifier stage is DC-coupled with a gain of 1000. The stage runs from supplies that allow its output to swing about ±5 V.

    • Output due to the offset: 50 mV × 1000 = 50 V.
    • The stage can only reach about 5 V, so its output sits hard against the supply rail: it is saturated.
    • A saturated amplifier no longer responds to small changes at its input. The 0.5 µV ABR would have produced 0.5 mV of output, but it is lost because the output cannot move.
    • The largest DC-coupled gain that avoids saturation is 5 V / 50 mV = 100, and that leaves no room for the signal or for any change in offset. A practical design keeps the first-stage output well inside the range, for example a gain of about 10 (giving 0.5 V from the offset).

    There are two engineering solutions, and both are used:

    1. AC coupling: after a first stage of low gain, a high-pass filter (in its simplest form a series capacitor and a resistor) blocks the steady offset and passes the changing signal. Further gain is applied only after the offset has been removed. This is the classic analogue evoked-potential design (section 6).
    2. DC-coupled, high-resolution front ends: a modest gain (or none) followed by an ADC with enough resolution that the full offset range and the microvolt signal fit into its input range simultaneously. With 24-bit converters this is possible: a range of ±2.5 V divided into 224 steps gives steps of about 0.3 µV at unity gain, and an offset of hundreds of millivolts still leaves most of the range free. The offset is then removed digitally by a high-pass filter in software. Many modern EEG and research amplifiers use this approach (section 7).

    Try the numbers in the front-end calculator in section 6.

    DC drift

    The offset is rarely constant. It changes slowly as the gel soaks into the skin, as the skin warms under the electrode, as sweat accumulates, and as the electrode chemistry settles after application. This slow change is drift. On a DC-coupled display it appears as a wandering baseline; after a high-pass filter, slow drift is mostly removed, but faster changes pass through as low-frequency wobble. Drift is usually largest in the first few minutes after application, which is one reason to apply the electrodes, check impedance and then allow a short settling period (for example while the patient settles) before critical recording. Ag/AgCl electrodes on well-prepared skin drift least.

    Dissimilar metals: never mix electrode types in one montage

    If the active electrode is Ag/AgCl and the reference is gold, the offset is no longer a small difference between two nearly equal potentials. It is the difference between two different chemical systems, potentially tens to hundreds of millivolts, and it is unstable because the gold side is poorly defined. The same problem arises when a new disposable electrode is combined with an old reusable one, or when electrodes from different manufacturers with different gels are mixed. Consequences include:

    • large DC offset, sometimes enough to trigger an offset warning or saturate the input stage;
    • continuous drift and low-frequency noise;
    • greater motion artefact on one channel than the other;
    • a small but steady current flowing between the two electrodes through the amplifier input, which polarises the more polarisable one further.
    Common errorReplacing one detached electrode during a test with whatever is at hand (for example a gold cup where the other electrodes are disposable Ag/AgCl) often produces a sudden baseline shift and persistent drift. Replace it with an electrode of the same type and batch, and if necessary replace all of them.

    Motion artefact as a disturbance of the double layer

    When an electrode moves relative to the skin or gel, the distribution of charge in the double layer is mechanically disturbed. The half-cell potential shifts momentarily and then recovers as the charges redistribute. In addition, pressure on the skin changes the skin potential and the thickness of the gel layer. The result is a transient of up to millivolts that lasts from tens of milliseconds to seconds. This is motion artefact, and it is fundamentally electrochemical, not a pick-up of electrical interference.

    • Polarisable electrodes show larger motion artefacts because their double layer holds more stored charge and recovers slowly.
    • Foam or sponge electrodes with the metal recessed away from the skin (a floating electrode) reduce motion artefact, because the gel column separates the metal from the moving skin surface.
    • Skin abrasion reduces motion artefact because it lowers the skin's own potential and its sensitivity to pressure.
    • Strain relief on the leads (section 5) prevents the lead from pulling on the electrode.

    Electrode noise

    Beyond the thermal noise of the resistance (described in 6.3), electrode interfaces add excess noise, especially at low frequencies. It comes from fluctuations in the electrochemical equilibrium and in the skin. Its spectrum typically rises towards low frequencies, roughly as 1/f. Ag/AgCl electrodes on prepared skin have the lowest excess noise; dry electrodes and polarisable metals have more. For the ABR, whose band starts at 30 to 100 Hz, low-frequency electrode noise is less important than for cortical potentials recorded from about 1 Hz, but it can still add to the residual noise and to drift-related rejections.

    Chloriding silver electrodes

    Reusable silver cup or disc electrodes need a coating of silver chloride to behave as Ag/AgCl. The coating is formed by chloriding, which is done in one of two ways (follow the manufacturer's instructions if they give a method, since some electrodes should not be treated):

    1. Electrolytic chloriding: the clean silver electrode is placed in a chloride solution (for example saline or potassium chloride) and connected to the positive terminal of a low-voltage DC supply through a current-limiting resistor, with another electrode as the negative terminal. A small current is passed for a period of seconds to minutes. Silver is oxidised at the surface and combines with chloride to form an even grey to brown layer.
    2. Chemical chloriding: immersion in a chloride-releasing solution (for example dilute hypochlorite) for a period of minutes, followed by thorough rinsing.

    A well-chlorided surface is a uniform matt grey or brown. A shiny silver patch means bare silver is exposed, and the electrode will behave like a polarisable one at that spot. Newly chlorided electrodes should be rinsed and, ideally, allowed to stabilise in saline for a period before use, so that a matched set has similar potentials.

    Electrode ageing and care

    • The chloride layer wears with cleaning, abrasion and use. Signs of wear are shiny patches, higher offset, more drift and more motion artefact. Worn Ag/AgCl electrodes should be re-chlorided or replaced as a matched set.
    • Gold-plated cups can lose their plating, exposing the base metal (often silver or copper alloys). A cup with worn plating becomes a dissimilar-metal electrode and must be discarded.
    • Lead wires break internally near the connector or the cup, often intermittently. Intermittent breaks cause sudden jumps and spikes that come and go with movement.
    • Paste left in a cup dries and hardens, raising impedance and harbouring microorganisms. Clean cups promptly after use (section 5).
    • Disposable electrodes have an expiry date and should be stored sealed in their pouch. Once the pouch is opened, the gel begins to dry; unused electrodes should be resealed or discarded according to the manufacturer's instructions.
    • Store electrodes away from light and heat. Do not store silver and gold electrodes tangled together in a wet state.

    3. Skin and tissue under the electrode

    The layers that matter

    Between the gel and the conducting tissues lies the skin. Its outer layer, the stratum corneum, consists of flattened dead cells (corneocytes) embedded in lipid, typically about 10 to 20 µm thick on most sites and much thicker on the palms and soles. It contains little water and few free ions, so it conducts poorly. It also behaves as a capacitor: two conducting regions (gel and living epidermis) separated by a thin insulating layer. Below it, the living epidermis and the dermis are well hydrated and conduct like the rest of the body tissue.

    Electrically, then, the skin appears as a resistance in parallel with a capacitance, in series with the electrode interface of Figure 6.7d. On unprepared, dry skin the stratum corneum resistance usually dominates the total impedance at low frequencies, often tens to hundreds of kilohms for a typical small electrode. Preparation reduces it to a few kilohms.

    Sweat glands and the skin potential

    Eccrine sweat glands open onto the skin surface through ducts that cross the stratum corneum. Their density varies with body site, from roughly a hundred to several hundred per square centimetre. When filled with sweat (a salty electrolyte), each duct is a small conducting path through the barrier, in parallel with the stratum corneum. Two consequences follow:

    • Impedance falls when a person sweats, and it changes as sweating changes. A warm, anxious patient may have drifting impedance and a drifting offset.
    • Sweat glands generate their own potentials, and their activity is controlled by the sympathetic nervous system (the basis of the electrodermal or skin-conductance response). Arousal, startle and temperature changes therefore produce slow potential shifts at the electrode that are unrelated to the evoked response.

    The skin potential between the outside and inside of the epidermis is typically some tens of millivolts and varies between sites and with pressure. Mild abrasion reduces it and makes it more stable, which lowers both offset and motion artefact.

    Gels and pastes

    Product typeWhat it isRoleNotes
    Abrasive preparation gel or pasteMildly gritty gel containing fine abrasive particles, often with electrolyteRemoves the outer stratum corneum, oil and dead cellsUsed for preparation only; wiped off before (or partly left under, depending on product) the electrode
    Conductive (electrolyte) gelWater-based gel containing chloride saltsFills the gap between metal and skin; supplies chloride for Ag/AgClRunny gels can spread and bridge between electrodes; they also dry quickly under open cups
    Conductive adhesive pasteThick paste that holds a cup electrode in placeBoth conducts and fixes the electrode on hair-covered sitesStandard with reusable cups; clean off promptly after use
    Solid hydrogelPre-formed conductive gel pad, often adhesive, on a disposable electrodeConsistent, clean contact without liquid gelSlightly higher impedance than wet gel at first; gentle for neonates
    Wet gel (sponge) in disposable electrodesFoam sponge saturated with liquid gelFaster skin wetting, lower initial impedanceCan dry if the pouch is left open

    Higher chloride concentration in a gel generally lowers impedance faster but can irritate the skin during long recordings. For the short durations of clinical ABR, standard commercial gels and pastes are suitable; follow the manufacturer's instructions.

    The science of skin preparation

    Skin preparation has three aims: to remove the high-resistance barrier, to remove oil that stops the gel wetting the skin, and to stabilise the skin potential.

    • Degreasing with an alcohol wipe removes sebum and make-up. The alcohol must evaporate fully before gel is applied; a wet alcohol film dilutes the gel and dries the skin.
    • Mild abrasion removes part of the stratum corneum. Impedance falls steeply with the first few strokes and then more slowly; excessive abrasion adds little further benefit and risks damaging the skin.
    • Gel then hydrates the remaining stratum corneum. Impedance continues to fall for several minutes after application as the electrolyte diffuses into the skin. A borderline reading may therefore improve on its own if rechecked a few minutes later.

    Frequency dependence of skin impedance: the Cole model

    The simple model in 6.3 (a resistance in parallel with an ideal capacitor, plus a series resistance) captures the main behaviour: impedance is high at low frequency and falls towards the series resistance at high frequency. Real tissue does not behave like an ideal capacitor. Its impedance falls more gradually over a wider range of frequencies, because tissue contains many structures with different time constants. The Cole model, widely used in bioimpedance, describes this with one extra parameter:

    Z(f) = R∞ + (R0 − R∞) / (1 + (j 2π f τ)^α) where R0 is the resistance at very low frequency, R∞ at very high frequency, τ a characteristic time constant, and α a number between 0 and 1. With α = 1 the model reduces to the simple resistor-capacitor circuit; smaller α spreads the transition over more frequencies.

    Students do not need to fit Cole models, but the idea explains two practical points. First, an impedance reading is only meaningful together with its test frequency. Second, two electrode sites can have the same impedance at one frequency and different impedances at another, which is why impedance meters use a fixed, standardised test frequency.

    Why impedance is checked at a low frequency

    • At low frequency the capacitive paths through the double layer and the stratum corneum are nearly open, so the reading is dominated by the charge-transfer and skin resistances, which are exactly what preparation changes. A low-frequency test is therefore the most sensitive indicator of poor contact.
    • Imbalance between electrodes matters most at the mains frequency (50 Hz), where common-mode interference is strongest (6.3). A test frequency of the order of 10 to 30 Hz gives a value close to the impedance that matters for mains rejection.
    • An alternating test current is used, not DC, because DC would polarise the electrodes and change the offset. The current is small (typically nanoamperes) for safety and to avoid disturbing the electrode chemistry.
    • A test at high frequency (for example 1 kHz) would read close to the series gel and tissue resistance and would make a badly prepared electrode look acceptable.

    Try it: electrode interface impedance against frequency

    Simple model: gel and tissue series resistance Rs plus charge-transfer resistance Rct in parallel with double-layer capacitance Cdl. Watch how the reading at the impedance-check frequency compares with the impedance in the ABR band. The corner frequency is fc = 1/(2π Rct Cdl).

    |Z| at check frequency
    |Z| at 100 Hz
    |Z| at 1 kHz
    Corner frequency fc
    |Z| of the modelcheck frequencyABR band (100 Hz to 3 kHz)

    Things to try: increase Rct (poorer preparation, or a polarisable metal) and see the low-frequency impedance rise while the 1 kHz value barely changes. Then set the check frequency to 1 kHz: the meter would now report a good contact even for a poor electrode. Increasing Cdl (larger or rougher electrode surface) lowers the corner frequency and pulls the curve down.

    Electrode size and impedance

    Impedance is roughly inversely proportional to the contact area: doubling the area of gel-skin contact roughly halves the resistance of the skin and interface, and doubles the double-layer capacitance. Consequences:

    • Large disposable electrodes (with a gel area of a centimetre or more across) give lower impedance than small cup electrodes on the same skin.
    • Neonatal electrodes are smaller to fit small heads, so their impedance tends to be higher; systems designed for neonatal screening often accept higher impedance values for this reason.
    • Needle and ear-canal electrodes have small or awkward contact areas and typically show higher impedance readings than large surface electrodes, even when working correctly.
    • Gel that spreads beyond the electrode increases the effective area but can bridge to a neighbouring electrode. A gel bridge between active and reference short-circuits the two inputs: the recorded response becomes very small because both electrodes see the same potential.

    4. Electrode types and materials

    Electrode choice depends on the test, the site, the patient and infection-control requirements. The main families are described below and compared in the table.

    • Disposable pre-gelled Ag/AgCl electrodes (snap or tab, foam or cloth backing): a silver/silver chloride sensor on an adhesive backing, with solid hydrogel or wet gel in a sponge. The lead connects by a snap stud or a clip on a tab. They are the most common choice for clinical ABR, ASSR and screening, because they are consistent, hygienic and quick. They need reasonably flat, hair-free skin.
    • Reusable gold-plated cup electrodes: small cups (typically about 6 to 10 mm across) filled with conductive paste and held on by paste or tape. They suit hair-covered sites such as the vertex, can be used many times, and are easy to clean, but are more polarisable than Ag/AgCl.
    • Reusable silver or Ag/AgCl cup and disc electrodes: similar cups in chlorided silver; better low-frequency stability than gold, but the chloride layer must be maintained (section 2).
    • Sintered Ag/AgCl electrodes: made from pressed and heated silver and silver chloride powder, so the chloride runs through the whole electrode. More durable than coated silver and very stable; used in research caps and some clinical EEG electrodes.
    • Tin electrodes: used in some EEG caps. Inexpensive and reasonably stable, but tin should not be mixed with other metals in one recording.
    • Subdermal needle electrodes: fine sterile stainless-steel or platinum-iridium needles inserted under the skin. They are used mainly for intraoperative monitoring, where the patient is anaesthetised, set-up time is short and electrodes must stay in place under drapes for hours. They are invasive, single-use and must be handled as sharps.
    • Ear-canal electrodes (TIPtrode type): a foam insert-earphone tip covered with gold foil, connected to the recording lead. It both delivers the sound and acts as the reference (or inverting) electrode close to the cochlea, which enlarges wave I or the ECochG AP compared with the mastoid.
    • Tympanic membrane (tympanic) electrodes: a soft electrode (for example a wick or a small foam or hydrogel tip on a flexible wire) placed gently in contact with the eardrum. It gives larger ECochG potentials than a canal electrode without piercing the membrane, but needs skill, otoscopic control and patient tolerance.
    • Transtympanic needle electrodes: a fine sterile needle passed through the tympanic membrane to rest on the cochlear promontory, usually by an otologist, often with local anaesthesia. It gives the largest ECochG responses (near-field; section 1) and the best signal-to-noise ratio, but it is invasive.
    • Caps and nets: elastic caps with electrodes at 10-20 or higher-density positions, or geodesic sponge nets. Used for multichannel EEG and cortical evoked potentials, rarely for routine ABR (4.5).
    • Neonatal electrodes: small, often pre-gelled hydrogel electrodes with gentle adhesive, sometimes combined into a single strip or pad containing all three contacts for screening devices.

    Bone conduction and VEMP considerations

    • Bone-conduction ABR: the bone vibrator is usually placed on the mastoid or, in infants, on the temporal bone behind the ear, and its headband can press on or displace a mastoid electrode. The vibrator also produces a large electromagnetic stimulus artefact. Common solutions are to use the earlobe as the reference site, to keep the vibrator and electrode apart, to route leads away from the vibrator, and to use alternating polarity to reduce the artefact. Follow the local protocol.
    • cVEMP: the recorded response is a muscle potential (inhibition of sternocleidomastoid activity), much larger than an ABR (typically tens to a few hundred microvolts), so electrode impedance is less critical. The active electrode sits on the upper or middle third of the muscle and the reference on the upper sternum; placement and muscle tension matter far more than electrode material. EMG level must be monitored and often used to normalise the amplitude (6.5).
    • oVEMP: electrodes are placed below the eyes over the inferior oblique muscle, with the patient looking upward. The skin there is thin and sensitive, so gentle preparation and small electrodes are used.

    Comparison of electrode types

    TypeMaterialHalf-cell stabilityTypical impedanceComfortInfection controlCostTypical use
    Disposable snap or tab (foam, hydrogel)Ag/AgCl sensorGood (non-polarisable)Low after prep (a few kΩ)Good; adhesive can pull on removalSingle useLow per test, recurringClinical ABR, ASSR, screening
    Reusable cupGold-platedPoorer (polarisable, drifts)Low with paste and prepGood; paste in hairClean and disinfect after each patientModerate once, long lifeEEG, hair-covered sites, ABR where local practice uses them
