    Impedance values in the table are qualitative: the actual figure depends on skin preparation, electrode area, gel and the test frequency of the meter. Always follow the manufacturer's instructions for the specific electrode and the local infection-control policy.

    5. Electrode placement procedure in detail (ABR and ASSR)

    This section sets out what needs to be done, in order, for a routine ABR or ASSR recording with disposable or cup surface electrodes. The montage itself (which electrode goes where and why) is covered in 6.3; here the emphasis is on technique. Local protocols and the equipment manual take precedence over this general sequence.

    Step 1: preparation of the room, the equipment and the patient

    1. Check the equipment before the patient arrives: system switched on and warmed up, correct protocol loaded, transducers connected and calibration in date, electrode leads and head box undamaged, a supply of electrodes of one type and batch, prep gel, alcohol wipes, gauze, tape, and a clean pillow or mattress.
    2. Reduce electrical interference: keep mobile phones, chargers and unnecessary mains equipment away from the patient and the head box; place the patient's bed or chair away from wall sockets and extension leads.
    3. Explain the test to the patient (or parent) in plain language: what will be done, that the electrodes are stuck on the skin, that the skin will be rubbed gently, that the test is painless, how long it takes, and that the patient needs to lie still or sleep. Obtain and record consent according to local policy.
    4. Ask about skin allergies (to adhesives or gels), skin conditions, recent surgery, shunts or implants near the electrode sites, and infection-control risks.
    5. Examine the ears (otoscopy) and remove any obstruction that would affect the stimulus. For infants, plan the test around a feed and natural sleep.
    6. Position the patient comfortably, lying down or reclining with the head and neck supported, so that the neck muscles can relax. Muscle tension in the neck and jaw is one of the largest sources of noise in the ABR.

    Step 2: measuring and marking the 10-20 sites

    For routine ABR, the vertex (Cz) or high forehead (Fz), the low forehead (Fpz) and the mastoids are often located by eye and palpation. Measuring properly is still important to learn, for three reasons: repeatable placement between tests, correct placement for multichannel and cortical recordings, and a correct Cz on heads with unusual shapes. The International 10-20 system uses two measurements across the head, each divided into steps of 10 % and 20 %.

    1. Find the landmarks. The nasion is the depression at the bridge of the nose between the eyes. The inion is the bony bump at the back of the skull, in the midline above the neck (the external occipital protuberance). The preauricular points are the small depressions just in front of the tragus of each ear, at the root of the cheekbone (zygomatic arch).
    2. Measure from nasion to inion over the top of the head in the midline with a flexible tape. Mark the midpoint (50 %): this is the anterior-posterior position of Cz.
    3. Measure from one preauricular point to the other over the top of the head, passing through the mark just made. Mark the midpoint (50 %): this is the left-right position of Cz. Cz is where the two midpoints meet. Adjust and re-measure until both are satisfied.
    4. From the nasion-inion line, mark Fpz at 10 % above the nasion and Fz at a further 20 % (30 % from the nasion). Pz and Oz lie at 70 % and 90 % if needed.
    5. From the preauricular line, the temporal points T7 and T8 (older names T3 and T4) lie at 10 % above each preauricular point, and C3 and C4 at 30 % (20 % beyond T7 and T8).
    6. Locate the mastoids (M1 left, M2 right) by palpating the bony prominence behind each ear, below the level of the ear canal. The earlobes (A1, A2) are an alternative reference site. These are not part of the percentage grid; they are identified anatomically.
    7. Mark each site with a skin-safe marker or a wax pencil, or simply keep a fingertip on it while preparing.

    Figure 6.7e. Measuring the 10-20 positions. Left: the nasion-inion arc is divided at 10, 30, 50, 70 and 90 % to give Fpz, Fz, Cz, Pz and Oz. Right: the preauricular arc (measured through the Cz mark) gives T7, C3, Cz, C4 and T8 at the same percentages. Cz lies where the two 50 % points coincide. The mastoids and earlobes are located by palpation, not by measurement.

    Worked example with adult head measurements. Nasion to inion is 35 cm and preauricular to preauricular is 36 cm.

    SiteRuleCalculationDistance
    Fpz10 % of nasion-inion, from nasion0.10 × 353.5 cm above nasion
    Fz30 % from nasion (10 % + 20 %)0.30 × 3510.5 cm above nasion
    Cz (front-back)50 % of nasion-inion0.50 × 3517.5 cm from nasion
    Cz (left-right)50 % of preauricular arc0.50 × 3618.0 cm from either preauricular point
    T7 / T810 % of preauricular arc0.10 × 363.6 cm above each preauricular point
    C3 / C430 % of preauricular arc0.30 × 3610.8 cm above each preauricular point

    For an infant with a nasion-inion distance of 24 cm, Cz is 12 cm from the nasion and Fpz 2.4 cm above it. In young infants the high-forehead position is used as the active site instead of Cz because the anterior fontanelle lies near the vertex (6.3).

    Step 3: skin preparation

    1. Perform hand hygiene and wear gloves according to local policy.
    2. Part the hair at the vertex if a vertex site is used; move hair away from the mastoid.
    3. Clean each site with an alcohol wipe (or soap and water for neonates, following local neonatal policy) and allow it to dry completely.
    4. Apply a small amount of abrasive prep gel with a cotton bud or gauze and rub with a few firm, small circular strokes on adults; use very light strokes on infants and older adults with fragile skin. The skin may become slightly pink; it must not bleed.
    5. Wipe away the excess abrasive and loose skin with clean gauze, unless the product instructions say to leave a thin layer.
    6. Use a separate applicator for each patient and discard it after use.

    Step 4: applying the electrodes

    1. Open the electrode pouch just before use. Check that the gel is moist and within its expiry date.
    2. For disposable electrodes, attach the lead clip or snap to the electrode first if this is easier (it avoids pressing on the electrode after application), then place the electrode on the prepared site and press around the adhesive rim, not on the gel centre.
    3. For cup electrodes, fill the cup with conductive paste so that there are no air bubbles, place it on the prepared spot and press gently so that paste contacts the skin. Cover with a small square of gauze or tape to hold it if needed.
    4. Keep electrodes apart so that gel or paste cannot bridge between them, particularly the ground and the active electrode on the forehead.
    5. Connect each lead to the correct input on the head box: non-inverting (active, +), inverting (reference, −) and ground (common). Colour codes differ between manufacturers; read the head-box labels, do not rely on colour alone.
    6. Use electrodes of the same type and batch for all sites (section 2).

    Step 5: lead routing and strain relief

    • Gather the leads together and route them away from the head as a bundle, twisting the active and reference leads loosely together if practical. Twisting keeps the two leads close so that magnetic and electric fields induce nearly equal voltages in both, which the differential amplifier then rejects.
    • Tape a small loop of each lead to the skin or clothing a few centimetres from the electrode (a strain-relief loop), so that a pull on the lead is taken by the tape, not by the electrode.
    • Keep the leads away from the transducer cables, particularly those of supra-aural earphones and bone vibrators, which radiate stimulus artefact. With insert earphones, the tubing separates the transducer from the ear, which helps.
    • Place the head box close to the patient's head, supported so that it does not hang on the leads. Avoid long, looping leads that form antennas.
    • Keep leads away from mains cables, monitors and power supplies.

    Step 6: impedance check and targets

    1. Run the system's impedance check. Read the impedance of each electrode and the difference between recording electrodes.
    2. Common targets are an impedance below about 5 kΩ for each electrode and an imbalance between the recording electrodes within about 2 kΩ (some protocols prefer below 3 kΩ). Some systems designed for high impedance, and some neonatal electrodes, have different limits; follow the local protocol and the equipment manual.
    3. If a value is high, first wait a minute or two (gel continues to hydrate the skin), then press gently around the electrode rim, and if still high remove it, re-prepare the skin and apply a fresh electrode.
    4. If one electrode is much higher than the others, look for a specific cause: hair under the electrode, a lifted edge, a dried electrode, a broken lead.
    5. If all values are high, check the ground electrode and the head-box connection, because a poor ground affects every reading on many systems.
    6. Record the final impedance values in the test record.

    Step 7: pre-recording checks

    • View the free-running (unaveraged) EEG on the screen before starting. It should look like irregular low-amplitude activity of a few to some tens of microvolts, without regular oscillation or sudden jumps.
    • Look for a regular sinusoid at 50 Hz (20 ms period) or a buzz with harmonics. Its presence means mains interference: recheck impedance balance, the ground, lead routing and nearby equipment before starting (6.3).
    • Look for large slow swings or a flat line against the top or bottom of the display. Both suggest DC offset or drift problems (section 8).
    • Watch the artefact rejection counter or indicator while the patient is still. If most sweeps would be rejected, fix the problem first; do not raise the rejection limit to hide it.
    • Ask the patient to clench the jaw briefly and then relax, to show both patient and tester what muscle artefact looks like and how much quieter the trace is when relaxed.

    Step 8: monitoring during recording

    • Watch the raw EEG, the rejection rate and the residual noise estimate continuously.
    • If rejection rises suddenly, look at the patient first (movement, waking, swallowing, crying) and then at the electrodes (lifted, pulled lead).
    • If a baseline shift or saturation appears after movement, pause, let the amplifier recover, and check the electrode that moved.
    • Recheck impedance between conditions if the recording is long or the patient has moved, especially in infants.
    • Note events (movement, arousal, electrode reapplied) in the record so that they can be related to the waveforms later.

    Step 9: after the test

    1. Remove the electrodes gently, peeling the adhesive back along the skin rather than pulling upward. Use water or an adhesive remover on fragile skin, especially in neonates.
    2. Wipe away gel or paste from the skin and hair. Check the skin for redness or abrasion and record any reaction.
    3. Discard disposable electrodes, applicators and gauze as clinical waste according to local policy. Needles go into a sharps container.
    4. Clean reusable cup electrodes immediately: remove paste with warm water and a soft brush, then disinfect according to the manufacturer's instructions and local policy, rinse and dry. Do not soak connectors or use abrasive cleaning on plated or chlorided surfaces.
    5. Wipe the head box, leads, pillow cover and any surfaces in contact with the patient with a compatible disinfectant.
    6. Inspect leads for damage and set aside any electrode with worn plating or shiny silver patches.

    Step 10: documentation

    Record at least: date and tester; patient identifiers; consent; patient state (awake, asleep, sedated); electrode type and batch if relevant; montage (sites for active, reference and ground, and channel assignments); impedance values; stimulus, filter and averaging settings; any problems and corrective actions; and any skin reactions. Good documentation lets the next tester reproduce the recording and lets a reviewer judge whether a poor waveform reflects the patient or the recording conditions.

    Checklist (printable)

    BeforeDuringAfter
    Equipment on, protocol loaded, calibration in dateWatch raw EEG, rejection rate and residual noiseRemove electrodes gently (peel along skin)
    Electrodes of one type and batch, within expiryPatient relaxed or asleep, head and neck supportedClean skin; check for redness or abrasion
    Consent obtained; allergies and skin checked; otoscopy donePause and check electrodes if rejection risesDispose of single-use items; sharps in sharps bin
    Sites measured or located and markedLet amplifier recover after movement before continuingClean and disinfect reusable electrodes and leads
    Skin cleaned, abraded gently, wipedRecheck impedance between conditions if neededInspect leads and electrodes for wear
    Electrodes applied, no gel bridges, leads to correct inputsReplicate key waveformsRecord montage, impedances, settings and events
    Leads bundled, twisted, strain-relieved, away from transducersNote events (movement, arousal, reapplication)Report any adverse skin reaction
    Impedance below about 5 kΩ each, balanced within about 2 kΩ (local protocol)Do not raise rejection limits to hide a problemRestock electrodes and prep materials
    Free-running EEG clean; no 50 Hz, no saturationKeep phones and mains equipment awaySave and back up the data

    6. The engineering chain that turns microvolts into a waveform

    The recording chain has to do five things at once: accept a signal of a fraction of a microvolt from a source with kilohms of impedance, ignore a common-mode voltage that may be tens of millivolts or more, tolerate a DC offset of tens of millivolts, keep the patient electrically safe, and deliver samples that are precisely timed relative to the stimulus. Figure 6.7g shows a typical arrangement. The overall architecture of an ABR system is described in 6.2; here each stage of the recording path is examined as an engineer would design it.

    Figure 6.7g. The recording chain from scalp to averaged waveform (classic AC-coupled arrangement). In DC-coupled designs the high-pass and most of the gain are replaced by a high-resolution ADC and digital processing. The stimulus trigger defines time zero for each sweep, so the averager can align the sweeps.

    The instrumentation amplifier

    An operational amplifier (op-amp) is an integrated circuit with two inputs (+ and −) and one output, whose open-loop gain is enormous (typically 105 to 106). It is almost always used with negative feedback: resistors feed part of the output back to the − input, and the circuit then settles where the two inputs are at nearly the same voltage. The resistor values, not the op-amp, set the gain. Almost no current flows into the op-amp inputs.

    The instrumentation amplifier (INA) is a differential amplifier built from three op-amps (or an integrated equivalent) and designed for exactly the biopotential problem: very high input impedance on both inputs, accurately set differential gain, and high common-mode rejection.

    Figure 6.7f. The three-op-amp instrumentation amplifier. Stage 1 (A1, A2 with the two resistors R and the single gain resistor Rg) gives differential gain 1 + 2R/Rg while passing common-mode voltage at unity gain. Stage 2 (A3 with matched R3 and R4) takes the difference and removes the common-mode component.

    How it works, stage by stage:

    1. The electrode signals go directly into the + inputs of A1 and A2. Since op-amp inputs draw almost no current, the input impedance is very high (typically 109 ohms or more in parallel with a few picofarads).
    2. Feedback forces the voltage at each − input to follow its + input. So the top of Rg sits at V+ and the bottom at V−, and a current (V+ − V−) / Rg flows through it. The same current flows through both resistors R. The voltage between the two stage-1 outputs is therefore (V+ − V−)(R + Rg + R) / Rg, which is the differential input multiplied by 1 + 2R/Rg.
    3. If both inputs rise together (common mode), there is no voltage across Rg, no current flows, and both outputs simply follow the input with unity gain. Stage 1 therefore amplifies the difference by 1 + 2R/Rg but the common-mode voltage only by 1, improving the ratio between them before stage 2.
    4. Stage 2 is a difference amplifier. With matched resistor ratios, it outputs (R4/R3) times the difference of its two inputs and cancels the common-mode voltage that came through stage 1.
    G = (1 + 2R / Rg) × (R4 / R3) where R is each of the two stage-1 feedback resistors, Rg the single gain-setting resistor, and R4/R3 the stage-2 gain (often 1).

    Worked example: R = 25 kΩ and R4/R3 = 1.

    • Rg = 5.56 kΩ: G = 1 + 50 / 5.56 = 1 + 9.0 = 10.0.
    • Rg = 1 kΩ: G = 1 + 50 / 1 = 51.
    • Rg = 500 Ω: G = 1 + 50 / 0.5 = 101.

    Changing a single resistor sets the gain, which is why integrated INAs expose the Rg pins. In biopotential front ends the INA gain is kept modest (about 5 to 50) because the electrode offset passes through the same gain; the stage must not saturate (section 2).

    Why gain in stage 1 helps rejection: the CMRR of stage 2 is limited by resistor mismatch. For a unity-gain difference stage built with resistors matched to a fraction ε, the CMRR is roughly 1 / (2ε) in the worst case, about 54 dB for 0.1 % resistors (the exact figure depends on how the mismatches combine). Because stage 1 has already multiplied the difference signal by G1 while leaving the common mode unchanged, the overall CMRR is improved by roughly the factor G1: with G1 = 10, about 20 dB more. Integrated INAs with laser-trimmed resistors typically reach CMRR of about 90 to 120 dB at low frequency, depending on gain.

    Input impedance

    The electrode impedance and the amplifier input impedance form a potential divider. For the signal itself the loss is small: with a 5 kΩ electrode and a 10 MΩ input, the signal is reduced by 5 / 10 005 = 0.05 %. The real reason for very high input impedance is the conversion of common-mode voltage into a differential error through impedance imbalance, worked in 6.3. Two refinements matter for design:

    • At 50 Hz the capacitance of the input and the cable dominates: 100 pF has an impedance of about 32 MΩ at 50 Hz. Keeping leads short, using an amplifier at or near the head, and driving the cable shield with the common-mode voltage (a driven guard or active shield) all raise the effective input impedance.
    • Dry and high-impedance electrodes (hundreds of kilohms to megohms) need input impedances of the order of gigohms and above, which in practice means an amplifier at the electrode (section 7).

    Input bias current and why it matters with electrode impedance

    Real op-amp inputs draw a small DC current, the input bias current, and also produce a small random current noise. Both flow through the electrode impedance and turn into voltages at the input.

    • DC error: a bias current of 1 nA through 5 kΩ gives 1 × 10−9 × 5000 = 5 µV of DC. If the two electrodes differ by 20 kΩ, the difference is 20 µV. This is small compared with the electrode offset but it changes whenever the impedance changes, adding to drift.
    • Polarisation: a steady current through a polarisable electrode slowly shifts its half-cell potential. With Ag/AgCl the effect is negligible; with gold or steel it adds drift.
    • Current noise: an input current noise of 1 pA/√Hz flowing through 10 kΩ produces 10 nV/√Hz, comparable with the thermal noise of 10 kΩ itself (about 13 nV/√Hz at body temperature). Through 1 MΩ (a dry electrode) it would produce 1 µV/√Hz, which is far too much.

    This is why biopotential amplifiers use input stages with very low bias and current noise (JFET or CMOS inputs, with bias currents typically in the picoampere range), especially where electrode impedance may be high. Bipolar-input amplifiers have lower voltage noise but higher current noise; they suit low-impedance sources. The bias current also needs a DC return path to the amplifier common, which is one of the jobs of the ground electrode. If the ground electrode comes off, the inputs float, the bias currents charge the input capacitances, and the amplifier drifts into saturation within seconds.

    CMRR and effective CMRR

    The amplifier's own CMRR (specified at a stated frequency, often 50 or 60 Hz) is only one part of the system's rejection. As shown in 6.3, the effective CMRR at the electrodes combines the amplifier CMRR with the rejection limited by electrode impedance imbalance, and the worse of the two dominates. Two further points:

    • CMRR falls with frequency because internal capacitances are never perfectly matched. A specification of 110 dB at 50 Hz may be 80 dB or less at a few kilohertz. Mains harmonics and high-frequency interference (for example from switching power supplies) are therefore rejected less well than the 50 Hz fundamental.
    • Unequal lead lengths or cable capacitances add a frequency-dependent imbalance equivalent to unequal electrode impedances. Leads of equal length routed together help.

    Driven right leg and active ground

    The principle of the driven-right-leg circuit is described in 6.3. In engineering terms it is a feedback loop: the common-mode voltage is sensed (usually as the average of the two INA stage-1 outputs, or of all inputs in a multichannel system), inverted with a high gain, and fed back to the body through the ground electrode. If the loop gain is A, the common-mode voltage on the body is reduced by a factor of about 1 + A, for example by about 40 dB for a loop gain of 100 at 50 Hz. A series resistor (typically hundreds of kilohms or more) limits the current that can flow into the patient if the amplifier output saturates or fails. Loop gain must be limited at higher frequencies to keep the loop stable. Some multichannel systems split the functions between two electrodes, one for sensing the common mode and one for driving it.

    Isolation

    The patient is connected by low-impedance electrodes, so the equipment must prevent any hazardous current reaching them, even if a mains fault occurs in the computer or the power supply. The front end is therefore electrically isolated: it runs from an isolated power supply (a small transformer or DC-DC converter with reinforced insulation) and sends its data across an isolation barrier (optical, magnetic or capacitive coupling). The medical safety standard IEC 60601-1 classifies applied parts and limits the leakage current that may flow through the patient; for example, limits in normal condition are of the order of 100 µA for body-floating (type BF) parts and 10 µA for cardiac-floating (type CF) parts. Evoked-potential applied parts are usually at least type BF. Consult the standard and the device documentation for the exact requirements.

    Isolation has a side effect on interference. The isolated front end is coupled to earth through the small capacitance of the isolation barrier (a few to some tens of picofarads). The smaller this capacitance, the less mains current flows through the patient and the lower the common-mode voltage. Battery-powered and wireless amplifiers, which have no connection to mains at all, benefit from this (section 7).

    Filter stages

    • High-pass for offset (analogue): in AC-coupled designs, a first-order high-pass filter after the INA removes the DC offset. Its cut-off frequency is f = 1 / (2πRC); for example, R = 1 MΩ and C = 1.6 µF give about 0.1 Hz, with time constant τ = RC = 1.6 s. A low cut-off preserves slow waveforms but means slow recovery: after a sudden 10 mV offset step, the filtered output decays as e−t/τ, and it takes about ln(10 mV / 1 µV) = 9.2 time constants (about 15 s here) to fall below 1 µV. This is the reason for slow recovery after an electrode is knocked (section 8). Some amplifiers provide a fast-recovery or reset function that briefly shortens the time constant.
    • Anti-alias low-pass (analogue): before the ADC, a low-pass filter removes energy above half the sampling rate, which would otherwise fold back into the recording band as aliases (1.8). Sigma-delta converters oversample heavily and include a sharp digital decimation filter, so the analogue anti-alias filter can be simple.
    • Digital band-pass: after digitisation, the clinically chosen band (for ABR typically 30 or 100 Hz to 1500 or 3000 Hz) is applied in software, where it can be changed without affecting the hardware and applied with zero phase shift offline. The effects of these choices on the waveform are described in 6.2.

    The ADC: bits, input range and LSB size

    The analogue-to-digital converter maps a voltage range onto 2n codes, where n is the number of bits. The smallest step it can represent (the least significant bit, LSB), referred back to the electrodes, is:

    LSB (referred to input) = full-scale span / (2^n × G) input range = ± (half span) / G quantisation noise (RMS) = LSB / √12 where the full-scale span is the total ADC input range in volts (for a ±2.5 V converter, 5 V), n the number of bits and G the total analogue gain before the ADC.

    Worked examples:

    • 24-bit ADC, ±2.5 V, gain 1: LSB = 5 / 16 777 216 = 2.98 × 10−7 V, about 0.3 µV. Quantisation noise is 0.3 / 3.46 ≈ 0.086 µV RMS per sample. The input range is ±2.5 V, so a 50 mV offset uses only 2 % of it. This is the DC-coupled design: the ADC resolves the signal and tolerates the offset at the same time.
    • 16-bit ADC, ±2.5 V, gain 5000: LSB at the ADC = 5 / 65 536 = 76.3 µV; referred to the input, 76.3 µV / 5000 = 0.015 µV. The input range is only ±2.5 V / 5000 = ±0.5 mV. Any offset larger than 0.5 mV saturates the converter, so this design is only possible with AC coupling before the main gain.
    • The same 16-bit ADC with gain 1 would have an LSB of 76 µV at the input: useless for evoked potentials. Resolution and gain trade against each other.

    The nominal bit count overstates real performance. Converter noise and reference noise mean that the effective number of bits (ENOB) of a 24-bit converter is lower, typically several bits lower depending on the sampling rate. What matters for the recording is the total input-referred noise of amplifier plus converter over the recording bandwidth, which should be well below the background EEG.

    Sampling rate choice

    The Nyquist criterion requires a sampling rate above twice the highest frequency present after anti-alias filtering. For an ABR low-pass edge of 3 kHz, the theoretical minimum is 6 kHz, but three practical factors push the rate higher: the anti-alias filter needs room to roll off, peak latency must be resolved to a few tens of microseconds, and waveform display should look smooth. Typical ABR systems sample at roughly 10 to 50 kHz. At 20 kHz the sample interval is 50 µs (0.05 ms); interpolation between samples can locate a peak more finely. Cortical potentials, with little energy above 30 to 100 Hz, are commonly sampled at a few hundred hertz to about 1 kHz.

    Trigger synchronisation and latency accuracy

    Averaging only works if every sweep starts at the same time relative to the stimulus. The stimulus generator and the ADC should run from the same clock, with the trigger defined in samples rather than by software timing. Two kinds of timing error matter:

    • Fixed delay: for example the acoustic travel time through insert-earphone tubing, typically about 0.8 to 1 ms depending on tube length. It shifts every latency equally and is corrected by the software or by the normative data, but it must be known and consistent.
    • Jitter: random variation in the trigger-to-stimulus time from sweep to sweep. Jitter smears the averaged response, acting as a low-pass filter. For Gaussian jitter with standard deviation σ, a component at frequency f is multiplied by exp(−2π²f²σ²).

    Worked example: at f = 1 kHz, σ = 0.1 ms gives exp(−2 × 9.87 × 106 × 10−8) = exp(−0.197) = 0.82, an 18 % loss of the high-frequency content of waves I to V and blunted peaks. With σ = 20 µs the factor is 0.992, a negligible loss. Jitter must therefore be kept well below 0.1 ms, which is why ABR systems use dedicated hardware for stimulus and acquisition timing rather than general-purpose computer sound cards and operating-system timers. The same arithmetic explains why neural dys-synchrony reduces the ABR (section 1).

    Averaging and artefact rejection

    The averager sums sweeps aligned on the trigger and divides by N. With stationary noise, uncorrelated between sweeps, the residual noise falls as 1 / √N (4.4). Weighted averaging, Fsp and residual-noise estimation, and the setting of artefact-rejection limits are covered in 6.2. Two engineering details belong here:

    • Sweeps in which any amplifier stage or the ADC was saturated must be rejected even if they appear within the voltage limit after filtering, because a clipped sweep contains no valid information. Good systems flag clipping separately.
    • The rejection test should be applied to the filtered signal referred to the input, in microvolts, so that the limit has the same meaning at every gain setting.

    Noise budget

    A noise budget lists every independent noise source, referred to the input over the recording bandwidth, and combines them as the square root of the sum of squares (because independent random noises add in power). The example below uses a 3 kHz bandwidth, electrodes totalling 10 kΩ (5 kΩ each), an amplifier input noise density of 8 nV/√Hz, a 24-bit ±2.5 V converter at unity gain, and 5 µV RMS of background EEG and muscle activity in a relaxed patient. Values are illustrative.

    SourceCalculationRMS per sweepComment
    Electrode thermal noise (10 kΩ total)√(4 × 1.38e-23 × 310 × 10 000 × 3000)0.72 µVJohnson noise; falls with lower impedance (6.3)
    Amplifier voltage noise8 nV/√Hz × √30000.44 µVFrom the data sheet of the input stage
    Quantisation (24-bit, ±2.5 V, G = 1)0.298 µV / √120.086 µVNegligible; ENOB makes it somewhat larger in practice
    Background EEG and EMG (relaxed)assumed5 µVDominant; depends on patient state
    Total per sweep√(0.72² + 0.44² + 0.086² + 5²)5.07 µVInstrument adds only about 1.4 %
    Residual after N = 2000 sweeps5.07 / √20000.11 µVAbout one fifth of a 0.5 µV wave V

    The budget shows why clinical effort goes into relaxing the patient: the instrument's own noise is a small fraction of the total when electrodes are good. It also shows when the instrument does matter. With electrodes of 100 kΩ (dry or unprepared), thermal noise alone rises to about 2.3 µV, and excess electrode noise and imbalance-related mains error add more; in a sleeping infant with low EEG, the instrument contribution becomes a much larger share.

    Try it: front-end design calculator

    Set the electrode offset, the analogue gain before the ADC, the ADC range and bits, the bandwidth, the amplifier noise and the electrode impedance. The calculator checks for saturation and builds an input-referred noise budget. DC coupling is assumed (the offset reaches the ADC).

    Offset at ADC input
    Headroom
    Input range (referred to input)
    LSB referred to input
    Quantisation noise
    Electrode thermal noise
    Instrument total

    instrument noise sourcesquantisationbackground EEG (5 µV, reference)

    Things to try: start at gain 1 with 24 bits (the DC-coupled design); the offset fits easily and quantisation noise is well below thermal noise. Raise the gain to 1000 and watch the 50 mV offset saturate the converter. Then choose 16 bits: at gain 1 the LSB is far too coarse, and at a gain high enough for good resolution the offset saturates. This is the dilemma that AC coupling solved for older designs and that 24-bit converters solve today.

    7. Technological updates in electrodes and front ends

    Electrode and amplifier technology has changed considerably in the last two decades, driven mainly by research EEG, brain-computer interfaces and consumer wearables. Some of these developments are now in clinical evoked-potential systems; many are still research tools. The descriptions below stay general; check current manufacturer documentation and the peer-reviewed literature before relying on any specific claim.

    Active electrodes

    An active electrode contains a small amplifier, usually a unity-gain buffer, mounted directly on or in the electrode. The buffer has a very high input impedance and a very low output impedance. The high-impedance node (electrode to amplifier input) is only millimetres long, so it picks up very little interference, and the low-impedance output drives the cable, whose movement and capacitance then matter far less. Active electrodes make it practical to record with higher electrode impedance and with lighter skin preparation, and they are standard in many multichannel research EEG systems. In clinical ABR, at least one manufacturer describes a preamplifier located at the electrode site and promotes it for recording in awake, active children; claims of this kind should be checked against independent studies.

    Dry and semi-dry electrodes

    • Dry electrodes contact the skin without gel, using metal or conductive-polymer pins or combs that pass through the hair, flat conductive pads, or capacitive (insulated) electrodes that couple through a thin dielectric. Their impedance is typically much higher and less stable than that of wet Ag/AgCl, often hundreds of kilohms to megohms, and they are more sensitive to movement. They therefore require active electrodes with very high input impedance.
    • Semi-dry electrodes hold a small reservoir of electrolyte that is released slowly onto the skin, aiming for wet-electrode performance without the preparation and clean-up of gel.
    • Dry and semi-dry electrodes suit quick set-up, repeated or long-term recording, and home use. For sub-microvolt clinical ABR, where every microvolt of noise matters, wet Ag/AgCl on prepared skin remains the reference standard.

    DC-coupled 24-bit sigma-delta amplifiers

    Sigma-delta (ΣΔ) converters sample at a very high rate with coarse resolution and then filter and decimate digitally to produce high-resolution samples at a lower rate. Their high resolution (nominally 24 bits) allows the DC-coupled design of section 2: modest gain, the whole electrode offset within range, and removal of the offset in software. Integrated analogue front-end chips for biopotentials now combine several channels of low-noise programmable-gain amplifier and 24-bit sigma-delta ADC with bias drive (a driven-right-leg function) and lead-off detection on one chip. The Texas Instruments ADS1299 is a widely used example in research and open-hardware EEG; as an illustration of the arithmetic, a ±4.5 V reference range divided by a programmable gain of 24 gives an input range of ±187.5 mV and an LSB of about 22 nV. DC coupling also allows the offset of each electrode to be displayed continuously, which is a useful contact-quality indicator.

    Wireless and wearable amplifiers

    Battery-powered amplifiers that send data by radio have no galvanic connection to mains and a very small capacitance to earth, which reduces common-mode interference and improves safety. They also allow recording outside shielded rooms and during movement. The engineering challenge for evoked potentials is timing: radio links introduce variable delays, so the stimulus trigger must be captured on the amplifier's own clock (for example through a trigger input or a synchronised timestamp), not inferred from when data arrive at the computer. Otherwise the trigger jitter of section 6 would smear the average.

    High-density caps

    Caps and nets with 64 to 256 electrodes are used in research on cortical auditory evoked potentials, mismatch negativity and speech processing. The large number of channels allows spatial filtering and source estimation, which can separate overlapping generators and improve signal-to-noise ratio. Set-up time, cost and the need for more careful impedance management limit their routine clinical use (4.5).

    Ear-EEG and EEG-steered hearing aids

    Ear-EEG records EEG from electrodes placed in the ear canal (on a custom or generic earpiece) or around the outer ear (flexible printed arrays behind the ear). It was proposed as a discreet, user-centred way of monitoring the brain over long periods. Research groups have reported recording auditory steady-state and other evoked responses with ear-EEG, with smaller amplitudes than scalp recordings because the electrodes are close together and the effective montage is short. For audiology the interest is twofold:

    • Objective measures from the hearing device itself: a hearing aid or implant earpiece with electrodes might one day record responses for fitting or monitoring without a separate clinic set-up.
    • Auditory attention decoding: research has shown that the talker a listener is attending to in a multi-talker scene can be decoded from EEG, because cortical activity tracks the envelope of the attended speech more strongly. This has led to research on EEG-steered or cognitively controlled hearing aids that would enhance the talker the user attends to. Decoding currently needs tens of seconds of data for good accuracy with standard methods, works less reliably with few in-ear electrodes, and remains a research topic, not a clinical feature.

    Printed, textile, soft and graphene electrodes

    Research directions include electrodes screen-printed or inkjet-printed on flexible films (allowing low-cost, disposable arrays shaped to the ear or face), conductive textiles woven into headbands, soft skin-like electrodes that stretch with the skin and so reduce motion artefact, and ultra-thin electrodes made of materials such as graphene, applied to the skin like a temporary tattoo. These aim at comfort, long-term wear and conformal contact. Their stability, reproducibility and performance for sub-microvolt signals are still being evaluated.

    Automated impedance and contact monitoring

    Instead of a single check before recording, some systems monitor contact quality continuously: by injecting a small test current at a frequency outside the recording band and measuring the resulting voltage, by tracking the DC offset of each electrode in DC-coupled systems, or by lead-off detection circuits that signal when an electrode loses contact. The tester is prompted when an electrode degrades during a test, which matters for long recordings and for infants who move.

    Noise-reduction and detection methods

    • Weighted averaging, in which quieter sweeps or blocks receive more weight. Bayesian weighted averaging was described for the ABR by Elberling and Wahlgreen; Kalman-filter-based weighting is used in at least one commercial system. The principle is covered in 6.2.
    • Statistical response detection (for example Fsp, and frequency-domain tests for the ASSR in 6.4), which allows stopping when a response is confirmed or when residual noise is low enough, instead of after a fixed number of sweeps.
    • Multichannel methods (spatial filtering, combining channels with partly independent noise) in research systems.

    In-ear and single-channel screening systems

    Automated ABR screening devices typically use a single recording channel, disposable electrodes (sometimes integrated into one pre-gelled strip), ear couplers or insert probes, and an automated detection algorithm that gives a pass or refer result. Combined devices offering OAE and automated ABR are common in newborn programmes. Their electrode requirements are deliberately forgiving (higher permitted impedance, simplified placement) because the tests are run by screening staff in busy wards. Understanding the electrode physics in this topic helps explain their typical failure modes: dried electrodes, poor placement on vernix-covered skin and movement.

    8. Troubleshooting: electrode potential, offset and drift

    The table focuses on problems caused by electrode potential, offset and drift. Mains interference, muscle artefact and impedance imbalance are covered in 6.3.

    SymptomLikely causeWhat to do
    Baseline wandering slowly up and down on the raw EEGDrift of electrode offset: electrodes recently applied, sweating, skin warming, polarisable electrodes, gel dryingAllow a few minutes for settling after application; cool the room or uncover the patient if sweating; use Ag/AgCl electrodes of one batch; replace dried electrodes; confirm the high-pass filter is set as per protocol
    Trace flat against the top or bottom of the display after an electrode was touched or the patient movedAmplifier or ADC saturated by a sudden offset step (double layer disturbed)Pause averaging; wait for recovery or use the system's reset or fast-recovery function; secure the electrode and add strain relief; recheck impedance
    "Large DC offset" or "electrode offset out of range" messageMixed electrode types or metals, one electrode dried or detached, worn chloride or plating, gel bridge to another electrodeCheck that all electrodes are the same type and batch; replace the suspect electrode (or all); clean skin and reapply; discard electrodes with worn surfaces
    One channel much noisier than the othersPoor contact or high impedance on the electrode unique to that channel (usually its reference), broken lead, polarisable electrode on that siteCompare impedances; swap the lead to another input to see whether the problem follows the lead or the electrode; replace the electrode or lead
    Slow recovery after each artefact, many sweeps rejected for seconds afterwardsVery low high-pass corner in the analogue stage (long time constant) combined with large offset steps; polarisable electrodes that recover slowlyReduce movement; improve electrode stability; use the protocol's recommended hardware high-pass setting; use Ag/AgCl; consider fast-recovery options
    All channels drift and saturate within secondsGround (common) electrode off, so the inputs float and bias currents charge themCheck the ground electrode and its lead; reapply
    Sudden steps or spikes that come and go with movement of the leadIntermittent break in a lead wire or connectorFlex each lead gently while watching the raw EEG; replace the faulty lead
    Offset and drift appear after replacing a single electrode during the testNew electrode of different type, metal or age from the othersReplace with an identical type from the same batch; allow settling
    Very small response in all conditions despite good impedanceGel or paste bridge between active and reference, shorting them togetherInspect for spread gel; clean and dry the skin between the electrodes; reapply
    RememberLook at the raw, unaveraged signal first. Offset and drift problems are obvious on the raw trace (slow swings, flat lines at the rail, steps) long before they are visible in an average, and no amount of averaging rescues sweeps collected during saturation.

    9. Demonstration

    Equipment: an evoked-potential or EEG system (ideally one that displays raw EEG and, if available, electrode offset), disposable Ag/AgCl electrodes from one batch, one or two gold cup electrodes with paste, prep materials, a flexible tape measure and skin-safe marker, a beaker of physiological saline, a high-input-impedance digital multimeter, and a computer with this page open for the interactive demos.

    1. Half-cell potentials in a beaker (no patient involved). Place two disposable Ag/AgCl electrodes (gel side down) or two chlorided silver electrodes in saline and measure the DC voltage between them with the multimeter: typically a few millivolts or less. Replace one with a gold cup and measure again: the voltage is typically much larger and keeps changing. Students observe that the offset is a property of the electrodes, not of the body. Never connect a multimeter to a person.
    2. Use the Nernst calculator (section 1) to show how ion concentration differences create voltages, and relate this to the electrode half-cell as the same balance of chemical and electrical forces.
    3. Use the dipole demo to compare promontory, ear canal and mastoid distances, and relate the ratios to the number of sweeps needed for ECochG and for scalp ABR.
    4. Measure a volunteer's head and mark Cz and Fpz with the 10-20 method, reading out the percentages and distances.
    5. Prepare and apply a standard montage with matched Ag/AgCl electrodes. Record impedance before preparation, after alcohol, after abrasion, and again 3 to 5 minutes later. Students observe the fall with preparation and the further fall with time.
    6. Show the free-running EEG. Tap the reference electrode gently: observe the transient (motion artefact) and the recovery time. If the system displays offsets, read them.
    7. Replace the reference with a gold cup (with consent and fresh preparation) and repeat. Observe any larger offset, more drift and slower recovery; then restore matched electrodes.
    8. Pull gently on an unsecured lead to show saturation and recovery, then add a strain-relief loop and repeat.
    9. Use the front-end calculator (section 6) to show why a 50 mV offset saturates a DC-coupled stage with a gain of 1000, and why a 24-bit converter at low gain does not.
    10. Use the interface impedance demo (section 3) to show why a meter testing at 1 kHz would hide poor preparation.

    10. Practical activity and assessment

    1. Electrode offset bench test: in pairs, measure the DC voltage between pairs of electrodes in saline (Ag/AgCl with Ag/AgCl; Ag/AgCl with gold; new with old if available) at 0, 1, 5 and 10 minutes. Tabulate and plot the results.
    2. Head measurement: measure nasion-inion and preauricular distances on a partner, calculate the positions of Fpz, Fz, Cz, C3 and C4 in centimetres, mark them, and photograph the marks (with consent).
    3. Impedance and preparation: apply a standard ABR montage and record the impedance of each electrode before preparation, after degreasing, after abrasion and after 5 minutes. Calculate the imbalance between recording electrodes at each stage.
    4. Raw signal observation: record 30 s of raw EEG at rest, during a gentle electrode tap and after a lead pull; note saturation and recovery times.
    5. Noise budget: using the system's residual noise estimate and the measured impedances, estimate the per-sweep noise and compare it with the thermal-noise and amplifier-noise contributions calculated from the equations in section 6.
    6. Front-end design exercise: for an electrode offset of up to 100 mV, choose an ADC resolution and gain (DC-coupled) that give an LSB below 0.5 µV referred to input without saturation, and justify the choice with calculations.

    Deliverable: a report of about three pages containing the offset table and plot, the head-measurement calculations, the impedance table, annotated screenshots of raw EEG events, the noise budget and the front-end design calculation, with a short discussion linking each observation to electrode potential, polarisation, volume conduction or amplifier design.

    Assessment: correct calculations (Nernst, 10-20 positions, LSB, noise), correct identification of offset, drift, motion and saturation events, sound explanation of the electrochemistry, adherence to the placement and infection-control procedure, and quality of documentation.
